Rows of daffodils were standing with their necks bent, like praying monks, in Boltzmann’s yard, and Viktor remembered when Gina Ball had planted them, about a year before she got sick. She’d be pleased to know that they were still blooming even after she was gone, but Viktor found the idea incredibly sad, perhaps because he didn't feel that he had yet planted much in his life.

When Viktor rang the bell it was the Adolescent Boltzmann who came to the door. He was delighted to see Viktor, and they went straight downstairs and snapped open two cans of Dr. Pepper.

The picture, padlock, and mirror were still there, and as soon as he saw them Viktor wanted to ask his dark-haired neighbor about one or more of those things, but he didn’t dare. Instead, after a few shots, he commented that there were a lot of lessons in pool that applied to life.

“Such as,” the Brilliant Boltzmann asked, not to challenge Viktor, but because he was the sort of fellow – a rigorous, scientific type –  who liked to back assertions with facts.

“Well,” Viktor said, “one thing would be that with each shot you need to focus on a specific outcome. I mean, rather than just thinking, ‘after I make the seven, I’ll send the cue ball off one rail down to this end of the table, it's better to pick an exact spot where you want whitey to end up, not that I'm good enough to put it on a dime, or even a silver dollar, but it increases your focus to think in precise terms. And I think that’s true in life as well, that you want to be as precise as possible about your goals.”

Boltzmann nodded his approval, and Viktor was as pleased as if the man had been his father.

Having uttered this wisdom, Viktor now found himself focusing more on precise locations, which helped his game, and he pulled ahead. But then he also found himself wondering whether he applied that rule in his own life as well, and that reminded him of something that had been bothering him on and off for the past few days – something he didn't really want to address, but felt that he had to, and now, when he was shooting so well, might be a good time.

Viktor waited until he’d made his 100th ball, and Boltzmann had applauded, politely but not wholeheartedly, then he made a face and said that he had a small confession to make.

Bang!  Boltzmann’s eyes popped like he’d just heard a gunshot, and widened even more when Viktor said that the thing he wanted to say was that he had not been entirely truthful the last time they played pool.

“In what way?” The Wary Boltzmann asked, not breathing.

“Well, when we talked last time I think I said that I work on special projects at National Financial. But that's not really true. I mean, I did some of that in the early days, because I have a quirky ability to see patterns in numbers, but that stuff’s all been turned over to the supercomputers now.”

Boltzmann looked less concerned, but still wary. As if another shoe might drop.

Viktor continued. "The point is, I think I just told you that to make myself sound more important, or at least interesting. In fact, I'm just a boring actuary who has six months to go before fully vesting his retirement plan, and that's my real story.”

“That is it?” Boltzmann asked.

“Yeah, that's it.”

Now the Wary Boltzmann’s dissolved, right before Viktor’s eyes. But it wasn't the Adolescent Boltzmann who reappeared, or the Brilliant Boltzmann, it was a Fourth Boltzmann who stepped out of the wings, a personality that Viktor would later nickname the Bad Actor Boltzmann. This personality was flat – kind of like Data, in Star Trek – and instead of speaking for himself, it seemed to speak lines that it had memorized, but without enough feeling.

“It is just the same in my company,” the Bad Actor Boltzmann said, without any facial expression.

“What do you mean?” Viktor asked.

“We have also turned much of the analysis over to the mainframes.”

“What kind of analysis?”

“Actuarial analysis.”

Time stopped, and Viktor felt a cold knife pass through his heart.

“What do you mean?” he asked. “Where do you work?”

“I work at The General,” Boltzmann said, but his dark eyes seemed dull as he said the words, as if his battery had died.

Viktor had no right to be surprised, since The General was the company that Boltzmann’s parking sticker was for. Still, he was astounded.

“What do you do there?” Viktor asked, trying to sound calm.

“I am a software engineer.”

“Then how do you know about the Lorentz Transformations?”

There was a hesitation, as if the Bad Actor Boltzmann had to search its database for an answer to this more complicated question. Finally he replied. “Like you, I took many physics courses as an undergraduate.”

“So,” Viktor stammered, “it would appear that we have more in common than I thought.”

“Yes,” Boltzmann said, looking at the mirror out of the corner of his eye, “it would appear that we do.”

“I guess I shouldn’t be surprised,” Viktor said, trying to lighten the moon, “since ten out of nine people in this area work in the insurance industry.”

“You mean nine out of ten, do you not?”

“Right,” Viktor said, “I meant nine out of ten.”

“Oh yes,” Bad Actor Boltzmann said, in a monotone voice, “I remember now that you made that joke one time before.”

26)                Boltzmann’s Story Reminded Viktor of His Father

In his office the next morning, Viktor did not work on 3C. Instead he researched databases in and around Albuquerque, New Mexico. This was because, right after telling Viktor that he worked at The General, the Bad Actor Boltzmann had picked up the picture on the side table and explained in uncharacteristic detail how he’d previously worked at a field office in Albuquerque, but had been transferred to The General’s headquarters in Hartford, where he’d purchased the Ball’s three bedroom house so that there would be enough room for he, his wife, Nina, and their three children – Andy, Bill, and Casey. But now Nina was saying that she wasn't sure that she wanted to move after all.

That story made sense on the face of it. The problem was that Viktor had not been able to find anyone named Boltzmann in the Albuquerque phone book, nor in the real estate records of the area, although the family in the picture had been standing in front of a stucco house that Boltzmann said they owned. Although this was in 1991, before the Internet destroyed everyone’s privacy and made them incredibly lonely at the same time, Viktor had access to a good many databases, but he had not been able to find a Norbert H. Boltzmann – or even a Norbert Boltzmann – in any of them.

At first, Viktor had felt frustrated. But soon he was smiling a crafty smile, and each time he came up empty, his smile only widened, because it was sure beginning to sound like Norbert H. Boltzmann was not his neighbor’s real name. Which meant that it was possible that he didn't really work for The General, either. Which meant that he might really be an Alien, a Mad Scientist, a Terrorist, in some sort of Witness Protection Program, or something even more wonderful.

#          #          #

Viktor faithfully began his real work at nine, but on and off during the day he chased down other Boltzmann angles as they occurred to him  When he checked the real estate records for Avalon, he found that the house Boltzmann was now living in had been purchased, for cash, by the Robert A. Smith family Trust. When he cross-referenced the Robert A. Smith Trust with Albuquerque, he found a house that the trust owned out there.

While he was having lunch he remembered the invoice Boltzmann had received from the pet store in Albuquerque, and with his quirky memory Viktor could actually see the return address, and it was the same as the house owned by the Smith Trust. On his way back to the office he called the pet store from a phone booth, said he was Norbert H. Boltzmann, and would like to update his address. Bing! – they had a Norbert H. Boltzmann in their files. He had bought cat food, the lady thought. But then – Boing! – their file was empty.

It was beginning to sound like Boltzmann really had lived in Albuquerque. But The General didn't have any data processing operations out there – just a sales office, and Boltzmann surely wasn't a salesman.

Viktor asked himself what else was out there and – Bang! – the idea that popped into his head was Sandia Labs. When he looked up Sandia he found that their mission was to “maintain the reliability and surety of nuclear weapon systems, conduct research and development in [arms control](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arms_control "Arms control") and [nonproliferation](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonproliferation "Nonproliferation") technologies, and investigate methods for the disposal of our nation’s nuclear weapons program's [hazardous waste](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazardous_waste "Hazardous waste").”

Viktor’s eyes popped and he exclaimed, “Wow!”  Based on what he knew so far, he thought it was entirely possible that Norbert H. Boltzmann was a high-level scientist doing secret work for the government. But then why had he moved to Hartford?  Not to do research or anything of that ilk. If he had moved to East Hartford, where Pratt & Whitney was located, that might be a possibility. But not to Avalon, and to a job at The General. But maybe that was not really where Boltzmann went every morning. Maybe he went to a secret research facility somewhere in the area, maybe even buried deep in Avon Mountain. Viktor liked that idea. Yes, he liked that idea very much.

#          #          #

As he proceeded through his day, Viktor couldn’t help but think about his father, with whom Boltzmann’s story seemed to resonate.

After his father ran off with the waitress from Goffstown, he continued to send money to Viktor’s mother, even though there had been no official divorce, much less a decree of alimony. Following his ostensible death, Viktor’s father had willed the house in New Hampshire, everything in it, and all his money, which wasn't much, to his mother. Viktor, their only child, was given a heavily-mortgaged cabin and all its contents in Oregon.

Viktor flew out to Oregon, where his father’s lawyer – a chain smoking, bubble-nosed, old Army buddy – met him. As they drove to his father’s cabin, the lawyer told him that his father had died in a car accident and the body, burned beyond decency, had been cremated immediately, in accordance with his father’s wishes. However, partly because of how the Army buddy said this – a bit like the Bad Actor Boltzmann – and because of how mysterious his father had always been, and also because no one wants to believe that their father is dead, Viktor had never quite accepted the story.

He’d examined the house for clues that something shadowy had happened. He found absolutely nothing. To him that seemed to confirm that something shadowy had indeed happened.

The theory that Viktor had since assembled was that his father really had been involved in some hush-hush activities. Although he had ostensibly worked as an electronics salesman, rep’ing a number of different companies, Viktor guessed that his goal had been to entrap foreign agents into attempting to purchase sensitive or even secret technology, such as a line of new DRAM chips. Then, when the political situation in Czechoslovakia had heated up, he’d been called back into active duty, because he was Czech-born and spoke the language.

Had he been killed over there?  It was possible. It was also possible that he’d been captured and imprisoned. And it was possible that he was still over there leading a covert life as a Czech chip salesman.

Although Viktor had absolutely no proof, Boltzmann’s story felt similar to the story he’d invented for his father.

27)                Viktor’s Father Stops By

Viktor went for a Power Walk when he got home from work that day, took a shower, and when he came out he smelled cigar smoke.

He knew immediately who it was, so he dressed quickly and hurried down to his office and there was Viktor Sr., seated in Viktor Jr.’s Mission oak rocking chair. Viktor Sr. had a lit Cuban cigar in his left hand, a glass of scotch in his right, and a spy-thriller open in his lap. He was dressed in the olive green turtleneck with elbow pads and a shooting pad on the right shoulder that he had often worn. His hair was slicked back, as always, and looked darker than Viktor remembered it, but not as if he’d dyed it, though Viktor had found such dye in the medicine chest of the master bedroom in New Hampshire, after his father had left. His father’s mustache also seemed to have lost its touches of gray.

Before Viktor could say a word, his father looked up, smiled indulgently, swirled the ice cubes in his drink, took a sip, made a face, sniffed, and then said, “Have I mentioned that I prefer Black Label?”

“I thought you were dead,” Viktor explained.

“Have a seat,” his father said, brusquely, motioning Viktor toward his own desk chair.

Viktor sat and swiveled to face his father, who took a puff from his cigar, smiled out of the corner of his mouth, and then said, “You're right, I am dead. Officially, that is.”

“Were you in Czechoslovakia?” Viktor asked.

“Do you mean, was I ‘monitoring’ the Velvet Revolution?  No, of course not,” his father replied, with a grin.

“That’s what I hoped,” Viktor said. “I thought the whole thing about you being disfigured in a car accident sounded a bit suspicious. Especially given your background.”

“You mean that I sold computer chips?” his father chuckled.

“No. I mean that you had done some work for, you know, a certain three-letter agency right after the War, and it did sometimes sound like you were still doing the odd assignment.”

His father looked pleased. “Oh, those stories,” he said. Then he puffed his cigar, exhaled, and watched the smoke swirl, but didn't reply.

“Does mom know you're alive?” Viktor asked.

His father snorted. “If she did, she wouldn’t be able to keep the life insurance money, now would she.”

Viktor nodded, thinking that that piece fit the puzzle as well, especially since his father had always been anti-insurance. Viktor had heard him say, innumerable times, that people should be responsible for their own lives and not try and pass it all off to the insurance companies, and he was proud that he had never carried any insurance. So then where had this life insurance policy with the triple ADB (accidental death benefit) rider come from?  Uncle Sam seemed like the only answer.

His father cleared his throat. “Speaking of insurance, how’s that racket going?”

“Not that well,” Viktor confessed. “I mean, I’ve done OK, but I haven't really felt, you know, fulfilled. But the good news is that in a few months I can retire on an 80% pension and then start a new career.”

“Such as?”

“I’ve been thinking I might go back to school for Astrophysics.”

His father snorted again, then coughed.

“Engineering, Viktor. That's what I told you, years ago, and I was right, wasn't I.”

Before Viktor could frame a reply his father glanced at his many-buttoned chronometer, quaffed the rest of his drink, squashed his cigar into the ice cubes, and muttered, “But enough chitchat. I'm only here because I want to make sure that you remember the code.”

“You mean Levi – “ Viktor started to say.

“Don’t say it,” his father said, waving his hand in front of him as if to erase the two syllables (out of five) that Viktor had uttered.

“I wasn't going to say the whole thing.”

“Good. But you do remember it?”

Viktor nodded.

“The correct middle initial, too?”

Viktor nodded again, and then opened his lips to ask his father if he knew anything about him being recruited – because of the calls that Provencher had received, and the car that had followed him, and the clicks on the phone line – but before he could get the words out his father stood, all six foot seven of him, and glared down at Viktor.

“I knew you would remember. You always had a good memory, son – could have been a code-cracker if you wanted to – but I needed to be sure. Never take anything for granted. Always check, double-check, and then check again. Measure twice …

“And cut once,” Viktor said.

“Good boy. Always remember that.”

“I'm glad you came,” Viktor said.

“Even though this meeting never happened.”

“Even though it never happened.”

As they headed for the door Viktor noticed that a dark car was now waiting outside, its engine running. For a second he thought it was Norbert H. Boltzmann, because it looked like his car, but it was different in some small way.

“Will I see you again?” Viktor asked, as his father pulled on a trench coat.

“I tell you what,” his father said. “You dump that tub of lard, Marianne, or whatever the f–– her name is, marry a younger woman, give me a son, or two, and I’ll be at the damned christening. With bells on.”

And with that he was gone, passing silently through the closed door, then invisible behind the tinted windows of the dark sedan as it melted into the shadows of night.

Still standing in the doorway Viktor kicked himself for not having thought to mention the Giants big win in the Super Bowl, because his father had always been a big Giants fan. It wouldn’t have made up for the time when he missed the deer, but it wouldn’t have hurt any.

28)                Marlena Meets Boltzmann

The desk in Viktor’s home office was covered with neat stacks of paper and labeled envelopes. It was two weeks before taxes were due, and our hero-to-be was almost done with his. All that remained was to decide how he wanted to treat one particular item.

Viktor’s feelings about taxes were more complicated than the forms. On the one hand, he thought that people in this country were taxed too much because the government (a) tried to run a lot of programs that would have been better left to the free market, and (b) did so very inefficiently. Also (c) this country was way too intrusive in international matters that were not our responsibility.

On the other hand, Viktor felt the laws were what they were, and it was his responsibility as a law-abiding citizen to follow them to the letter, though that didn't prevent him from cracking jokes about the subject. He often muttered that the IRS was a four letter word. If that got a laugh he would say, “You know, I still have the first dollar I ever earned. The blinking IRS has all the rest.”  Or, “I just wish that I could claim the government as a dependent.”

While many people labor over their taxes, Viktor had two attributes that made them a snap: he was very organized, and he was extremely good with numbers. But neither of those talents were helping him with “Schedule A, line 14: Gifts to Charity, Contributions by cash or check.”

His dilemma was that Marlena gave a fortune to charities, but she was not one hundredth as careful about receipts as Viktor was. There were checks and credit card records for the big expenses and donations, but she handed out gobs of cash – anyone who came to the door left with a twenty or two. Also, she drove thousands of miles to service her charitable organizations, and used up reams of paper and innumerable ink cartridges, and generated some fierce long distance bills, since this was back in the days before long distance was “free” if you paid a lot more for it. She also donated all sorts of items, for which she never got any receipts.

Viktor’s problem was that he couldn’t document it all. One didn't have to have a receipt for an item under $25, but if he put down everything he thought she gave the number would be so large that it might trigger an audit, especially if there was any chance that he was being considered for a CIA assignment. On the other hand, he didn't want to pay more than he really owed.

In addition to not knowing what to do, Viktor was mad at Marlena. He was mad at her for giving away so much to other people. He was mad at her for not keeping better records. He was mad at her for getting fat. And he was mad at her for a number of other things for which there is no line on the Form 1040.

#          #          #

Suddenly, as if she’d read his mind, Viktor heard Marlena coming down the hall.  Actually, he felt her approach more than he heard her. The joists of their shoddily-constructed B-Plan home were barely strong enough to support her planetary weight, so the floor of his office seemed to literally tip in that direction.

She popped her head around the corner. “Are you too busy to talk?”

Viktor said no, he was just doing taxes.

She plopped herself into his rocker. It was strong enough to hold her, but if she got an inch or two wider she might get wedged in there.

Breathing heavily, she smiled a smile that didn't look quite right, then said, “I thought you’d like to know that I met him.”

Marlena did that all the time – left out the antecedent. She assumed everyone would know what she was talking about.

Viktor figured she meant Boltzmann, but he didn't say that because didn't want her to think that their new neighbor was at the top of his mind.

“You met who?

“Our new neighbor. Your pool pal. Dr. Boltzmann.”

She made a face, as if she was thinking about something very complicated, and then said, “You said he’s an astrophysicist?”

“Yes,” Viktor nodded.

Now she got a weird smile on her face and said that it was such a coincidence that Viktor had wanted to be an astrophysicist, and then one moved in next door. “What are the odds of that?” she asked, meaning that she thought something was rotten in Denmark.

Viktor put on an equally weird smile and said, “Give me a second and I’ll calculate them for you,” and pretended to reach for the mechanical pencil in his pocket.

“Anyway,” she said, “I met him.”

That hit Viktor, but he pretended it didn't. “Oh, where was that?” he asked, without looking up from his papers.

“At the hardware store.”

“What on earth were you doing at the hardware store?”

“Committee work. I needed some duck tape (sic), and he was at the checkout right in front of me.”

“I didn't even know you would recognize him.”

“I’ve seen him getting in or out of his car a couple times. And now that I’ve seen him up close, I think I know what you mean.”

“What I mean by what?

“When you say that he’s weird.”

“I never said he was ‘weird.’”

“Well, ‘odd,’ or ‘different,’ or whatever it was you said.”

“I don’t think I said anything like that. I might have said that he seemed brilliant, or like an absent-minded scientist. And he is a PhD.”

“Well, that all fits. He had trouble using the credit card reader. The clerk had to show him how to swipe it. He said they were all different, which is true, but they’re not that different. And there was something else about him, too.

“And what was that?” Viktor asked, with cached trepidation.

Now Marlena stared off into the air, as if a portrait of Boltzmann was hanging on the wall. She started to speak, decided not to, then shrugged and went ahead. “I had the feeling that he was someone who was very involved with his work, and that he paid a heavy price for it. With his family, I mean. But, of course, that could just be because you me that he worked so much and he and his wife were breaking up.”

Viktor didn't remember saying that.

“At the same time,” she continued, and this sounded like her real message, “it didn’t feel like it bothered him. Getting divorced, I mean. It felt like marriage hadn't really suited him.”

“You mean because he works so much and is geeky?”

“In other ways, as well. He reminded me in some ways of Tim Jenkins at the hospital. The phlebotomist.”

“I can’t say I remember him.”

“You met him once, at the Christmas party.”

Viktor remembered him now, and got the idea. “Was he the guy who was a little, you know,” Viktor said, waving his hand limply in the air.

“Viktor!” Marlena cried, even though she was the one who’d brought it up.

“Sorry,” he said, and then asked her if Tim Jenkins had three kids.

“No, of course not, why do you ask?”

“Well,” Viktor said with a smile, our new neighbor does. He has three kids and a wife in Albuquerque and they will be moving here once the school year is over.” 

That shut her up, though Viktor himself doubted that either of them would be meeting Norbert H. Boltzmann’s family in a couple months, or ever, though not for the reason Marlena had suggested.

29)      LDF’s, IBNR’s and GRE’s

The Ed Committee meetings were televised, so on a Wednesday night in early April Viktor was able to tune into Channel One, the local cable station (which was free), at 7:00 and confirm that Marlena was indeed there, occupying a good portion of the first row, with her eyes looking down on the socks she was knitting things for children in a country that hadn't existed when Viktor was a boy, and a skeptical ear cocked toward the proceedings.

Leaving the TV on, Viktor went out to the living room and peered down at Norbert H. Boltzmann’s house. Five minutes ago the blind had been down, but now it was up!  Viktor wanted to grab his cue and run right over, but forced himself to wait 15 minutes to not seem too eager. To occupy that time he put his dishes away, wiped the counters clean, and neatened up various shelves. HehEFinally, he grabbed his cue stick and a six pack of Dr. Pepper, and marched down Lancelot Lane, keeping his pace reined in.

Viktor had two things on his mind as he covered the thirty or forty yards to Boltzmann’s. One was that he was going to try to subtly work a couple insurance terms into the conversation – to see if Boltzmann, who claimed to work at The General, understood the lingo. Or not. Also, he was going to keep an eye out for clues as to whether his new friend might be gay, as Marlena had implied. Viktor wasn't quite sure what to look for in that regard, though, because he knew of several examples of co-workers at NFG who did not seem gay in the slightest, but were reported to be so, even though they had wives and children. On the other hand, there was at least one fellow who seemed totally gay, but wasn't in the slightest. What’s more, gayness did not appear to be a simple yes or no question like when Viktor was growing up – today there were as many types of gayness as there used to be flavors of Howard Johnson ice cream, and several of them were vanilla.

#          #          #

It was the Adolescent Boltzmann – eager to see Viktor, but reluctant to make direct eye contact – who opened the door. He was wearing the black suit pants and white, short-sleeve shirt that all the Boltzmann’s wore, somewhat crumpled, as if he’d worn the same outfit all day at work, and the previous couple days as well. The whiteness of the shirt had the effect of making Boltzmann’s complexion feel less pale, but in the fluorescent light of the basement it took on a greenish cast, and his many zits and pimples – which seemed more pronounced when he was the Adolescent Boltzmann – looked unhealthier.

After they’d been playing for about ten minutes Viktor said, “Here’s something that you, as a fellow insurance guy, might find amusing. “When I first started at NFG, fresh out of college, I found what I thought was a neat parallel between quantum physics and my actuarial work.”

“How so?” Boltzmann asked, not catching the irony in Viktor’s tone.

“Well, you know,” Viktor said, smiling now, “how physicists can tell you how many electrons will be deflected in a certain direction by a magnetic field, but they can’t predict which ones?”

Boltzmann nodded.

“Well, actuaries such as myself can tell you how many insured will die of a given cause in a given year, but we can’t tell you which ones. If I could crack the code on that baby (chuckle) I’d stand a good shot of getting a company parking space.”

Boltzmann seemed confused for a moment, but then he either got the joke, or pretended that he did, and let out a thin laugh.

“Anyway, instead of applying the Lorentz Transformations to the motion of electrons, I found myself calculating LDF’s,” Viktor continued, in as natural a tone as possible, even though he’d just set a little trap.

Boltzmann nodded as if he understood, but Viktor didn't think he really had, so he pushed the trap an inch closer. “I assume your company also uses Loss Development Factors to calculate your Incurred Losses Not Reported.”

“Yes, we use both LDF’s and ILNR’s,” Boltzmann replied.

“Right, everyone does,” Viktor said. But to himself he muttered, “Wrong!”  The correct answer would have been that he used IBNR’s (Incurred But Not Reported). Although it was just a one letter difference, Boltzmann was a precise fellow, and Viktor’s guess was that he had NFI (no flipping idea) what he was talking about, and therefore did not really work in the insurance business.

Perhaps sensing Viktor’s skepticism, Boltzmann said, “Of course we have our own protocols, and acronyms, but given that our companies are competitors, I probably should not talk about them.”

“Good point,” Viktor smiled. “And I’ll do the same. Because if I tell you too much ... I’ll have to kill you.”

Whereupon Boltzmann’s magnified eyes grew huge for a second.

#          #          #

The two men shot pool for a while without speaking, and then Boltzmann asked Viktor if he had given any further thought to the idea of going back to Physics.

“Yes,” Viktor said, “I have, but the problem is ... well, the problem is the same as it was twenty years ago.”

“Your wife is pregnant again?”

Viktor shook his head, took a deep breath, looked at the floor, looked up and said, “You know, if the truth be told, I'm not sure that that was the real reason why I didn't pursue Physics.”

“Your wife-to-be was not pregnant?”

“No, she was pregnant. But, well, if I'm being really honest, I think that in some ways her situation was just a convenient excuse for me to not go to Cal Tech. Otherwise I would have suggested that we move out to California together.”

Boltzmann, who wasn't following him, asked, “Are you saying that you did not want to go to graduate school?”

“I did, but I didn't. The thing is, I’d been a math superstar in a small high school in a small city in a small state. But it was different when I got to college. By my junior year I could see that I’d be able to get by, but I’d never be able to perform at the level of some my classmates. I mean, I could do big long division problems in my head, but there were three or four fellows – and one gal – who could do the square roots of the same numbers without moving their lips. And they’d skipped more than just third grade.

The other thing that bothered me was that they were a pretty cold bunch. I don’t think any of them ever laughed at one of my jokes, or if they did, it was for the wrong reason. So I could see that if I pursued a career in astrophysics, I was destined to be a hack, instead of a superstar, and wouldn’t have much fun, either. By contrast, I really was kind of a star in my early days at NFG. So I kept telling myself that I’d go to grad school, but I never did, and it wasn't just because of Marlena. The real reason I didn't become a World-Famous astronomer is that I wasn't up to it, and I'm probably not up to it now, either.”

“If you thought you were, you would have applied by now,” Boltzmann said. “Or at least taken the GRE’s.”

“You're right,” Viktor confessed. “I would have.”

Viktor had looked down as he said these words, almost in shame, and now, when he looked up, he half-expected to see Boltzmann regarding him with disapproval. Instead he was surprised to see a look of great empathy. It was as if something similar had happened to Boltzmann himself, and he understood, at a deep level, what Viktor was talking about. It was such a vulnerable and human look that Viktor felt bad about trying to trap his friend with tricky questions about LDF’s and IBNR’s and wondering if he was secretly gay.

30)                McNutty Suggests That Viktor Get His Act Together

Viktor was sitting in the cafeteria, prodding his non free-range tortellini, when he spotted McNutty rolling toward him at great speed, with a huge grin on his face.

Viktor didn't think the first draft RIF list was out yet, but maybe it was, and McNutty was coming to tell him that he wasn't on it, and Viktor wasn't on it, either. It was either that or, from the way he looked, McNutty had won a medium-size lottery payout.

It wasn't any those things. It was something Viktor never in nineteen million years would have predicted. It was that the Company had announced a talent contest.

McNutty told him that there was a poster in the hall. The contest was called “Get Your Act Together,” and the idea was that an employee, or employees, could do any kind of creative performance they wanted, for up to five minutes, during an upcoming casual Friday afternoon event with food and (non-alcoholic) refreshments. The winner(s) would receive an extra week of vacation. And he thought Viktor should do a stand-up routine!

Viktor reminded McNutty of HR’s Rule Number One, that if something was funny you were not allowed to say it because you would be offending someone.

“Ha!” McNutty exclaimed. “You should open with that.”

“Or maybe the Galileo joke?” Viktor said, in a challenging tone.

McNutty said no, he thought that would be going too far, especially in the current environment.

“That’s exactly the problem,” Viktor nodded. “If you go too far, it's too far. But if you don’t go far enough, it isn't funny.”

“Oh, you’ll find a way,” McNutty exclaimed, then glanced at his watch with some anxiety, saw that it was after one o’clock, and hurried back to his room-without-a-view.

As Viktor walked back to his own office, he took a peek at one of the posters, out by the elevators, and saw that the event would take place in six weeks, on June 1st, which stuck in Viktor’s mind because it was his mother’s birthday.

“My guess,” Viktor muttered to himself, is that the RIF will be announced one week before the event – they’re always on a Friday. Then the fun event will be part of the strategy to rebuild morale among the survivors.”

On the way back to his office Viktor passed by an office where a Level III was vociferously protesting that a fellow Level III had a Level IV chair and that was “not right, not fair, and not legal,” even if the woman did have a bad back.

“There’s certainly plenty of material here,” he thought to himself.

When he checked his calendar, Viktor saw that the company’s mid-year meeting was scheduled for Monday May 20th, so all the pieces fit. At the company meeting they’d talk about the need for efficiency. That Friday, the 24th, they’d hand out the pink slips. Then, one week later they’d roll the music and sing, “On with the show!”

Viktor pictured all that with a cynical smile. Then, the smile began to take on a sly energy of its own as he considered the possibility that maybe he should do a stand-up routine. And maybe he didn't have to be all that careful. By June 1st he’d be just three months short of his 20th anniversary at NFG. Even if his routine stepped on a couple toes, there was no way they’d fire him that close to his full vesting. So he’d be fairly bulletproof. And, who knows, maybe he’d be good at it.

Viktor opened a Word document and began, “Ladies and Gentlemen.”  Then he closed his eyes, and pictured the scene.

#          #          #

Viktor was sitting in of a single row of chairs that was facing about five hundred chairs pointing the other way – at him. To Viktor’s left there was a sort of stage – just a bunch of risers with maroon skirting. A four-person group had just finished dancing and lip-syncing to “On With the Show.”  The audience continued to applaud even after they’d left the stage. Then Frank Drysdale, the company’s SVP of Marketing, dressed in a tuxedo, stepped to the podium and read the introduction that Viktor himself had written.

“Our next act is a one-person show. Although he has not appeared on the David Letterman Late Night show this month … an unnamed source tells us that he is the ... third funniest man on the 11th floor. Please join me in welcoming Viktor Hal.”

Because Drysdale failed to punch the phrase ‘this month,” no one laughed at that, but the “third funniest man” line got a few chuckles, and there was polite applause as Viktor stepped to the microphone,

“Ladies, gentlemen and, uh ... managers,” he began.

A couple people chuckled.

“You know,” Viktor said, shading his eyes and squinting, “these lights are so bright, that none of us up here can see you. So, I guess that if you were ever tempted to make a certain gesture in my direction, or Frank’s … this would be a good time.”

He got a couple chuckles on that, too.

“I admit,” Viktor continued, in a brighter tone, “that one good reason I am likely to win the prize is that, unlike the other acts that have so far ... defaced this stage, there is only one of me. Thus, only one week of vacation to give away!  I think that aspect will be especially popular with our CFO. You know, our ... Cheap Financial Officer.” 

When he added that Ernie Chirichetti was so cheap he would only go to an eleven step program a few people coughed, because Chirichetti was in fact a bit of a boozer. Otherwise, the room was so quiet that you could hear all the digital watches not ticking.

“I hear that Ernie has been inspired by Japanese gardening techniques. Instead of Western bonuses, we will now get bonsai bonuses.

“I figured that joke would be OK with HR. “But not the one about bonsai ... penises.”

Chairs began scraping on the linoleum.

“When I asked HR about guidelines for my routine they basically told me to not offend anyone in the room. But how about the two hundred and nineteen people who got RIFed last week?’ I asked. ‘They’re not in the room, so can I offend them?  Or have you offended them enough already?’”

Viktor noticed some movement off to his right.

“HR also cautioned me to not use the ‘f’ word,” Viktor continued. “So I'm going to substitute the word ‘lovely’ wherever I would have used the ‘f’ word if I was working at a more ... progressive company. As in the sentence, ‘I'm starting to like our ‘lovely’ managers better and better, though it might just be the ... “lovely’ Stockholm Syndrome.’”

Viktor’s eyes had adjusted to the light, and now he could see the audience. People were exchanging looks, and a few were whispering to each other.

Viktor had been taught in public speaking class that if you were struggling you should look for a friendly face in the audience and speak to them. So he searched for McNutty. But, of course, McNutty had been RIFed.

“Some other words I'm not allowed to use are ‘alcoholic’ ...‘perverted ... and ‘retarded.’  I’ll substitute “lovely” for them as well. In that spirit, do you know how you can distinguish between a Level III and a Level IV?”

Viktor gave that one an extended pause, then said, “Hardly any of the IV’s are middle-aged white guys. But many of them are lovely, lovely and/or ... lovely.”

That did it. Drysdale appeared, took Viktor by the arm and quickly ushered him off the stage while the audience, eager to endear themselves to the Biggies began booing, and a couple people threw pastries on the stage. Only one person had a lovely grin on his face, and that was Viktor.

31)                Boltzmann’s Virginity

While most of the men in Avalon Acres were watching the Red Sox play their season opener in Toronto, Viktor and his new neighbor were shooting pool. During the first five minutes of their match, Boltzmann made a point of weaving in a number of arcane insurance terms. Like BACM (Best Available Control Measures), MPL (Maximum Possible Loss) and PLE (Primary Loss Expectancy). If he was trying to convince Viktor that he really did speak the lingo, it had the opposite effect.

The two men had fallen into a pattern of playing to 100 balls, and then sitting and talking for a bit – Viktor on the couch, and Boltzmann in a lounger that he kept in the upright position.

Viktor opened this evening’s conversation by apologizing for having sounded negative about his career the previous time. “I think I'm just having a minor Midlife Crisis,” he said. “And I guess that's normal at my age. The problem in my case is that none of the usual solutions appeal to me.”

Like anyone from another planet, Boltzmann looked confused. “What are the ‘usual solutions?’” he asked.

“Oh, you know. A guy will buy a red sports car. He’ll get hair implants, or a rug. Or he’ll carry on with a woman half his age.”

“Are the red sports cars more expensive?” Boltzmann asked.

Viktor laughed. “What I meant was that I'm just not the sports car type, and I especially wouldn’t buy red, because it's too flashy. And it would bother me to do something so predictable.”

“Do you view yourself as unpredictable?” Boltzmann asked.

“I do,” Viktor said. “Not that I'm some brave, rebel type. But I do see myself as different in my own little ways.”

He thought Boltzmann would ask him what those ways were, but instead he moved down to number two on Viktor’s list. “I have heard you refer several times to your hair loss,” he said.

“I’m more thinning than losing, but implants!  I'm not that vain.”

“And what is a ‘rug.’”

“‘A rug’ is a term for a toupee.”

This time it was Boltzmann who laughed an awkward laugh, showing his teeth. But then he leaned forward and asked, “And what about carrying on with a woman half your age?”

Now Viktor hesitated, long enough that even Boltzmann – who was not great when it came to social cues – seemed to notice it.

“Well, I get tempted, like any other fellow. But I'm probably too loyal to actually do anything about it.”

Boltzmann nodded as if to say that he not only accepted that as true, but agreed with it.

“And what about you?” Viktor asked, half-thinking of Marlena’s theory.

“Oh no,” Boltzmann cried, with embarrassment. I am a virgin.”

“A virgin?  But you have three kids!”

Now Boltzmann looked flustered, and it was a full fifteen seconds before he explained that he did not mean that he was an absolute virgin, but that his wife, Nina was the first woman he had had intercourse with.

“But have you ever had thoughts?” Viktor asked.

Boltzmann nodded and said that yes, of course he had, like any normal man. But it didn't feel like a normal response.

“You know,” Viktor now said, partly to get Boltzmann off the hook, “I have tried a few things to break out of the box.”

“Yes?” Boltzmann asked, and he looked genuinely interested in this notion of breaking out of the box.

If Boltzmann had been a different sort of fellow, Viktor might have now used the lines he’d used a few times at work – about how he’d begun arriving for meetings only two or three minutes early, even though this still put him six or seven minutes ahead of the next earliest arrival. Or how, in his new, rebellious mode, he sometimes left the toilet seat up at the office after using it, rather than putting it down, as Marlena as always telling him to, even though no women used the men’s bathroom. But those quips would have gone way over (or under) Boltzmann’s head, so Viktor went straight for a real one and told him about going skydiving with Peterson.

“Unlike in war movies,” Viktor said, “you don’t jump out of the side yelling ‘Geronimo.’  Instead, you put out one foot out onto a sort step, grab the strut, and pull yourself outside the plane, and stand there squinting into the wind until the jump master taps you on the thigh. Then you let go, arch your back as the wind takes you, and when the chute opens, it's incredible. You're looking down, between your feet, and the ground is about 2,500 away. You're floating. And it's very, very quiet up there. And …”

“And?” Boltzmann asked.

“Well,” Viktor said, “there was a wonderful moment, especially the first time when, after the chute opened and I looked up to make sure it was a good chute, and then down, looking down at that 2,500 feet of air, I felt for a second like I had been reborn – like I had stepped out into a nothingness from which I might not awake, or else I would be reborn. I felt like I’d put my life on the line. Not that the odds of dying are very big, but there is a risk chance, and I took it, and when I came through it alive, that was what it felt like, like being born. I felt free and clear and open and it was great. I felt like I really existed.”

Boltzmann regarded him with awe.

“We did another jump the weekend after that, and it was good, and I wanted to go six times so I could get off the static line, but when we went the third time a guy broke his leg, and Marlena started to make some noise about how it was a long drive and she didn't think I would die but I could break a leg, and she didn't want to be wheeling me all around. So I kind of let it go.”

“But that feeling?” Boltzmann asked.

“It was wonderful. It was the opposite of what I’ve been feeling lately, which is that I'm neither dead nor alive, that I'm just …”

“Propagating in isolation?”

“What?”

Now Boltzmann explained how, in quantum physics a photon can be deflected to the left, or the right, as Viktor had mentioned earlier, and both possibilities were operable until you “took a measurement.”  Then the sine wave of possibilities collapsed into one outcome or the other.

Viktor nodded and said that yes, he did remember that from his studies, while thinking that it sounded a lot like his famous koruna coin toss decision making process.

With that, Boltzmann opened his mouth and looked like he was about to say something else. But before he could form the words, the Frightened and Wary Boltzmann tapped him on the shoulder, so he cut himself off. Not only that, but he quickly wrapped up the evening, saying that he had an early meeting tomorrow, even though when Viktor looked out his window the next morning Boltzmann left at exactly the usual time.

32)                The Cincinnati Boondoggle

On his drive into work that morning, Viktor heard that the Red Sox had won their opener, 6-2 and made a mental note that if anyone mentioned the game he would comment that Roger Clemens (who may not have even been using steroids at this point in his career) had had a strong outing.

When he arrived at his office, the first thing he did was to fish out the two small cardboard boxes in which he stored the business cards he’d received from people over the years. Because his conversation with Boltzmann the previous evening had reminded him of someone.

Rather than organizing the cards by company name, or person name, as most (OCD) actuaries would have done, Viktor had just slipped them into the box as he acquired them, so the cards were in more-or less-chronological order, like geological strata. He’d met the person he was looking for in October, so her card should be very close to the front and – Bingo! – there she was.

His hand shaking, Viktor slid the card out. Paula J. Bancroft was a Senior Risk Analyst at a Specialty Lines insurance company in Minneapolis, but Viktor had met her in Cincinnati, at a 3 day-2 night marketing boondoggle. Before he went, Viktor had told people that he was pretty sure it was a boondoggle – if, indeed, as he quipped, the very idea of a boondoggle in Cincinnati was not an oxymoron – because, in addition to having all expenses paid, each participant was to receive a personalized attaché case from a prestigious leather goods company, although Viktor was willing to bet twenty dollars that the attaché case he was to receive would not be made of real leather.

#          #          #

If Viktor had any doubts about the conference being a boondoggle, the shrimp at the opening reception put them to rest. “They looked like they’d been raised in the outlet pipe of a nuclear power plant,” he later told his co-workers. Viktor skipped the shrimp, because his mother had warned him about the dangers of eating shellfish, but he did not skip the open bar.

He was about halfway through his first martini when he found himself increasingly attracted to PAULA (first names were in ALL CAPS on the name tags). Though short, she stood out because (a) there were only about twenty women at the conference, and (b) she was particularly animated. Motion attracts the eye, and Paula was in constant motion – smiling, laughing, gesturing, pushing a wisp of hair out of her eyes, poking a fellow actuary in the arm. But it wasn't flirtatious – Paula came across more like a pal. Viktor guessed – correctly – that she was from the Midwest, and had older brothers.

Viktor thought she was good looking, in a wholesome way, which to his mind was the best way to be good-looking. She had straight, dark hair in a Dorothy Hammil bowl, a slightly pudgy, field hockey figure, and wore no make-up that Viktor could detect from fifty feet away.

What Viktor liked, especially, about Paula was that she radiated a very positive energy – akin to what he thought he had felt from Marlena in the early days. Whatever people said to her, she seemed to agree with it, or at least sympathize, which caused them to say more. Viktor had the feeling that he could say more to this woman in five minutes than he’d said to Marlena in the previous five years, which wasn't saying much.

Although Viktor prided himself on having been 100% faithful to Marlena for almost twenty years, at the very same time he wanted to meet this woman, and he might have even had the nerve to go stand next to her, but she was surrounded by people the whole evening, like the bright center of a whirling galaxy. So he had a second martini, instead, then threw caution to the wind and had a few shrimp.

He woke with what he called “big head, little feet.”  Downstairs, the ballroom had been sliced in half by an air wall. Breakfast was being served in the left half. Viktor loaded a plate at the buffet, and then looked around, hoping to find Paula at a table with an empty seat, but she was nowhere to be found.

At 8:30 everyone filtered into the meeting half of the ballroom carrying cups of coffee. There was a stage with a wide projection screen and two podiums. The seating was “classroom style,” meaning that each row of chairs had a table in front of it, so people could take notes. In front of each seat was a packet of promotional information, a pencil, a pad with the hotel’s name on it, and a package of mints. Viktor took a seat three rows from the back and flipped through the packet of information.

The company sponsoring the boondoggle had developed a ... so-called “revolutionary new risk-assessment methodology” for introducing new insurance products in niche markets. Their premise was that the people you were selling a policy to had the clearest view of the risk, so you could use them to set the price of the product. So, instead of looking at the experience in the field, you would offer the new product to qualified candidates in test markets. You’d begin by offering it at a high premium, and then work your way down the ladder, kind of the way you adjust a carburetor by starting with a rich fuel mixture and then leaning it out until the car begins to stall, only in reverse.

It felt like it respected the free market, and Viktor was very much a free market guy. But he didn't like it that all the companies in the case studies were anonymous. He didn't like it that every single product that had been launched with the new system had (allegedly) been a big moneymaker. And he didn't like it that it came with a “money back guarantee.”

Just as the lights were going down for the opening video presentation, Paula Bancroft slid into the seat on Viktor’s left. He turned away, closed his eyes for a second, and when he looked back, she was still there.

When the room lights came back up, Paula introduced herself, and Viktor did the same.

“NFG’s a big company isn't it?” she said, looking at his name badge.

Viktor nodded.

“How many people work there?” she asked.

Viktor screwed up his face as if doing a complicated calculation, then replied, “Oh, about half.”

That line almost always got a chuckle. It got a chuckle from Paula Bancroft, as well. But it also got something else. Her eyes tightened in on Viktor’s, with concern, like she’d spotted something behind his laughter, something kind of the opposite, and wanted to reach her hand out, pat his forehead and say, “Oh, poor boy. Why do you tell all these silly jokes?  Where does it hurt?”  And Viktor wanted, desperately, to tell her where it hurt, even though he had no idea where that was.

33)                Viktor Follows Boltzmann to Work

Despite Boltzmann’s parking sticker, and the myriad insurance terms he’d tossed out at their last meeting, Viktor still didn't believe that he worked in the insurance business. He simply did not seem like the type.

“Never guess,” his former mentor had often advised him. “Find out.”  So that was exactly what Viktor had decided to do. His plan was to follow Boltzmann and see where he went – to The General, or someplace else. And so, on a Wednesday morning in the middle of April, Viktor exited Avalon Acres about forty-five minutes earlier than usual, and immediately pulled into the parking lot of a convenience store on Route 44 where things cost even more than they did at Gil’s. He bought a Courant, took it out to his car, and flipped through the pages, while keeping an eye on the stone gates of Avalon Acres.

Skimming a story about how Bush and Gorbachev were supposedly negotiating a treaty to scale down their stockpiles of long-range nuclear weapons, Viktor had two thoughts. One was that if the Russian economy was weak, and the Commies couldn’t afford the arms race, we shouldn’t slacken the pace.

Viktor’s other thought was that Norbert H. Boltzmann’s work might well be defense-related. Star Wars, for example. It was hard to see how that line of work would have brought his new neighbor to a bedroom community like Avalon. But if he should cross the river this morning, over to East Hartford, for example, where Pratt & Whitney was located – now that would be, as Viktor liked to say, a horse of a different feather.

#          #          #

Boltzmann typically left his house at 6:15 and – sure enough – as Viktor’s dashboard LCD silently blinked 6:17, the nose of Boltzmann’s sedan poked out from between the stone pillars. Viktor felt a Niagara of excitement. He was tailing someone!  Like a spy!

Boltzmann turned right and headed east on 44, toward Hartford. Viktor hunched down, let one car pass between them, and then slid in behind it, feeling calmer now that he was in motion.

They both slipped through the first traffic light together. Then they were both stopped by the second one. As they approached the third light – at Bishop’s Corner – Boltzmann moved to the left and signaled a turn. Now, since the car between them was continuing straight on 44, Viktor would have had to pull up directly behind Boltzmann to take a left with him. Instead, he pulled a move that his old man would have appreciated. He continued straight ahead behind the other car, then turned left into the plaza, and gunned it through the parking lot, which wasn't too difficult since none of the stores were open at this hour. And it timed out perfectly!  By the time Viktor arrived at the exit onto North Main Street, Boltzmann had gotten his left turn arrow and started up the road. Viktor let a car pass between them, as before, and then pulled in behind.

“Pretty slick,” Viktor smiled, but his happiness lasted only an instant, because Boltzmann was now headed not for East Hartford and Pratt & Whitney, or even Hartford, but for the suburban location of The General.

The scenery was more rural, and more colorful. There were daffodils and tulips in many yards, and forsythia was popping all over the place, along with crab apples and other fruit trees.

Viktor let himself fall back out of sight for a couple turns, half hoping he’d lose Boltzmann when the fellow turned off on some secret side road. But he had no such luck, and soon they were approaching the campus – as it was called – of The General, which had built a sprawling facility on several hundred acres where they grew premiums on what had once been corn fields.

Viktor’s spirits leaped in joy when Boltzmann drove past the main gate, but crashed all the harder, a couple seconds later, when his neighbor’s brake lights lit up, and he turned in at the next entrance.

Viktor pulled off to the side of the road, whipped out his birding binoculars, and observed as Boltzmann stopped at a guard station, flashed an ID, and was waved in. He continued watching as Boltzmann parked his car and entered the building.

#          #          #

After Boltzmann had disappeared from sight, Viktor slumped in his seat, feeling deeply discouraged. There didn't seem to be any way around the evidence – his new neighbor really did work at The General.

It took Viktor fifteen minutes to put himself back together, and the way he did it was by reminding himself that this confirmation of Viktor’s Number Three Theory – that Boltzmann worked in insurance – really only eliminated one of his other four theories, that being the idea that Boltzmann was employed by the government to do secret military research. All his other theories were still viable.

Viktor’s Number Five Theory – that Boltzmann was an alien – was still possible. In fact, what a great job it would be for an alien to work in systems at an insurance company, where he, she or it would have access to vast databases about earthlings.

His Number Four Theory – a.k.a. Chuck’s Theory, that Boltzmann was doing something shady in his garage, possibly involving automatic weapons – also remained in play. Plenty of terrorists had day jobs. For example, [Patrick Sherrill](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Sherrill "Patrick Sherrill"), who had shot and killed 14 postal employees just a few years earlier had been, by day, a postman.

Viktor’s Number Two Theory  – that Norbert H. Boltzmann was in a Witness Protection Program – still held water as well. In fact, what better way to become anonymous than to work in an insurance company that employed tens of thousands of more-or-less identical drones.

Although Viktor’s Number One Theory – that Boltzmann was a scientist – no longer looked tenable, it remained possible that he was engaged in something scientific in his personal life. Maybe building something like a perpetual motion machine. Or a (gasp) matter transmitter!

As soon as Viktor uttered the words “matter transmitter,” the image that leaped to mind was of the scene in the 1958 version of movie “The Fly,” where André, the scientist played not by Vincent Price, as many people think, but by some other actor, has built what he calls a disintegrator-integrator – two glass-enclosed boxes that look like mini phone booths, separated by about twenty feet of open space. It was designed to disintegrate an object in Box A, and then reintegrate it in Box B. After testing the equipment with a China saucer that said  “Made in Japan,” André gathers up the family cat and says, “You won’t feel a thing, Dandelo. And tomorrow you will be the most famous cat in all the world.”

André places the cat in Box A, and pats him reassuringly. Then he goes and sets what looks like a darkroom timer on a 15 second countdown, and puts on protective goggles. The room lights dim, and we hear an accelerating whine as an array of blue and green neon tubes flash, along with a checkerboard of lights. Box A begins to glow. When the timer hits zero there’s a crash and a bright flash of light and the cat disappears from the box.

Now André looks expectantly at Box B. But then his brows furrow. He rushes over, and the cat isn't there.

“Dandelo?” he cries. But the only reply is an eerie meow as the kitty’s atoms zip out into the ether. That was by far Viktor’s favorite part of the movie, never dreaming, of course, that a mackerel tabby named Dandelo would soon sit purringly in his own lap.

34)                Step One: Preheat Oven to 450 Degrees

Because he’d gotten up early to follow Boltzmann, Viktor arrived at work about a half hour ahead of schedule that morning. On the one hand, this gave him more time to work on 3C, and hopefully make the breakthrough that would lead to his promotion. On the other hand, it was now only a couple weeks until his 41st birthday, so he was basically out of time. He didn't really feel like working on 3C, anyway, because he was still feeling bummed out about Boltzmann working at The General. So he decided to take a look at his “Get Your Act Together” stand-up draft instead.

He read what he’d written, and concluded that it would be suicide. But then – with the image of Dandelo’s atoms whizzing out into space lingering in his mind – Viktor had what felt like a brilliant idea!  Instead of zinging management, maybe he could zing cats. The idea had a special appeal because, if there was a skeleton in Viktor’s closet, it was the skeleton of a cat.

I never met a cat I liked,” Viktor often said, and it was 101% true. It wasn't just that he didn't care for the “beasts.”  There was a visceral anger that rose up in him. One evening, when he and Marlena were having dinner with some “cat lovers,” as he called them, sounding like his father, one of the beasts tried to jump up into his lap, and he flung it halfway across the room.

“Viktor’s terribly allergic to cats,” Marlena explained quickly.

“Right,” nodded Viktor, rubbing his eyes and sniffling.

And that was far from the first – or last – time that the issue had come up.

Early in their relationship, Marlena had asked Viktor what he thought the root of his animosity was, and Viktor replied that it dated back to his infancy, when a pack of wild cats had dragged his little brother off into the woods and slowly eaten him alive.

“But you don’t have a little brother,” Marlena protested.

“Not any more, I don’t,” Viktor replied with a grin.

Hidden away in the study of his house, Viktor had a small library of books along the lines of “101 Uses for a Dead Cat,” “How to Kill Your Girlfriend’s Cat,” and “Kitty Kare,” a tongue-in-cheek manual for taking care of one’s cat in which the first chapter, titled “How to Keep Kitty Warm and Cozy,” read:  “Step One: Preheat Oven to 499 degrees,” a line which never ceased to delight him.

While Viktor didn't make cat jokes, per se, he did make a lot of cat references, none of which were flattering. Like when he would say that so-and-so was “about as loyal as a cat,” meaning not at all. Or observe that a certain fragrance that Marlena was trying on would be “perfect for camouflaging a kitty litter box.”

To not sound like a total loony, Viktor had amassed a goodly pile of information to justify his feelings. For example, that cats kill an estimated 100 million birds per year across our fair land. That, in what Viktor called Entitled States of America, people spent about six million dollars a day on cat food, versus about two million per day on baby food. Or that there are about three hundred diseases that are transmitted by cats, including several that can be fatal to infant children.

So now, thinking that perhaps he could use the little monsters as scapecats, as it were, Viktor closed his eyes, and edited his routine.

#          #          #

After stepping to the microphone he paused for a long moment, to draw the audience in. Then he took a deep breath, and began in a very serious tone.

“Ladies and gentlemen. After writing a first draft of my material, I had a chat with HR, because I wanted to remain within the guidelines of propriety. What they basically told me was that I should not make jokes about any people or classes of people because any comments that were funny would be discriminatory, which is forbidden. So I went through my first draft and snipped out all the offensive parts, and all I was left with was what I just said – “Ladies and gentlemen.’”

To Viktor’s delight, that got a flutter out of the audience.

“I was working on my material at home, because it would have been against company policy to work on it at the office, even for a company show. I brewed a cup of coffee to stimulate my creativity, and as I was pouring it I observed the behavior of my wife’s cat, Fluffer-Nutter.”

Another ripple from the audience.

“Fluffer-Nutter was just finishing his business in the litter box, spraying some bits of who-knows-what here and there, including one piece of something dark and sticky that made it all the way up onto the kitchen counter. Fluffer-Nutter then jumped up on said counter to have a snack, stepping on our salmon with the very same paws that had just dug through the kitty litter.”

At this point the audience seemed to divide, like the Red Sea, into those who liked cats, which seemed to be mostly the women, and those who liked cats less, or not at all, which was mostly the men.

“That was when I had the thought that, instead of making jokes about people, perhaps I should make jokes about cats. To be sure I was on safe ground, I got a copy of HR’s new three hundred and twenty-seven page Diversity Guidelines and, sure enough, cats are not mentioned once. Perhaps because none of our insured are cats.”

That elicited a guffaw from one gentleman in the back of the room.

“Cats do play an important role in business, though. In medicine, for example. About 25,000 cats are used yearly in the United States for vivisection, which is experiments conducted on live animals. That excludes governmental research, for which the numbers are not public, so the actual number of cats contributing to the advancement of medical science is probably much higher. And that's just the United States.

“By the way, if you should need a cat for research, just contact what is called a Class B Dealer. They obtain their specimens from shelters and the like. See me after the show if you need the name of a Class B Dealer in your hamlet.

“But now, since I only have five minutes, I’d better hurry up and get on with the actual jokes.”

And then he’d tell the same basic jokes, except that the victims would be cats. For example, how do you tell the difference between the Level III cats who are promotable and the ones who are not?  None of the promotable ones are middle-aged white cats.

And there were some new jokes he could mix in as well. Such as, who was the most powerful cat in China? Chairman Miaow. Or, in which month do cats kill the fewest birds?  Probably February, because it's the shortest month.

Out of the corner of his eye Viktor caught someone in the corridor casting a curious glance at him, and he realized that he’d been laughing out loud. He expanded his chuckle to a smile, and waved innocently, as if he’d just been looking at a Dilbert cartoon.

35)                [T-O-R-T-U-R-E](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torture "Torture")

The Dow topped 3,000 for the first time on April 17th but Viktor’s portfolio had not risen nearly as much. As if that wasn't bad enough, when he got home from work that evening his lawn was suddenly dotted with bright yellow dandelions.

Viktor got his digger tool out of the garage and began to yank them from the soil, which he found satisfying. As he did he noticed that almost no one else in Avalon Acres had dandelions. That was because they all used lawn services that doused their lawn with chemicals, or did the same themselves.

As he worked he also noticed two men raking Norbert H. Boltzmann’s yard. Whereas most of the grounds keepers he saw in this neighborhood were young, and often didn't look like they had been born in this country, these two men were clean cut, professional looking fellows – a lot like the movers, Viktor thought. They had a clean white truck, too.

#          #          #

Viktor and his new neighbor didn't play pool for a week because Marlena was on the Day Shift and home in the evening, but Viktor thought about his new neighbor plenty during that time. Especially during his PowerWalks. Now that the days were longer, and the evenings warmer – or at least some of them – he was in the woods a lot, where the yellow wood violets were out, along with the true violets, and Virginia bluebell. (smell of cedar chips)

As Viktor walked he sometimes talked to himself, saying things like, “If Boltzmann goes to The General every morning, I guess that means that he isn't doing anything weird and mysterious out there. So if he’s doing something weird and mysterious, he must be doing it at home. Most likely in the garage.

“Those were good questions Chuck had raised about why Boltzmann doesn’t keep his car in the garage, or put his trash out there, and those questions remain unanswered. The padlock he put on the door leading only adds to mystery.

“So, then, how do I find out what’s going on out there?

“Well, the windows are blacked out, so there’s nothing to see. I could sneak up to the side of the house one night when he’s off on one of his errands and look at the electrical meter. But if he’s not home, he wouldn’t be doing any high-voltage experiments, now would he.

“I could rent a Geiger counter and stroll past the garage.

“Or I could just come right out and ask him, but Boltzmann would be unlikely to tell me what he’s doing, unless it’s something really boring, like building an extensive model railroad set, and that’s hardly what I'm hoping to hear.”

#          #          #

Finally Marlena shifted back to nights, and the shade on the side of Boltzmann’s house went up that very evening. That suggested to Viktor that his new neighbor somehow knew that Marlena wasn't home, even though she would have left the house long before Boltzmann returned home from The General. It also suggested that Boltzmann was as eager as Viktor was to resume their get-togethers, and that thought pleased him. Unless, of course, Marlena was right and their dark-haired new neighbor wanted to lure him out to an S&M dungeon in the garage.

The moment that Viktor’s foot touched the floor of Boltzmann’s rec’ room he noticed an envelope on the side table – the same table that had the framed picture of Boltzmann and his presumptive family. It felt like the envelope had been placed there on purpose, and it wasn't ten minutes before Boltzmann brought it up.

“By the way,” Boltzmann said, in his Bad Actor Voice, I just got a letter from Nina with a nice picture that you might like to see.”

Viktor said he would love to see the picture.

Boltzmann opened the envelope and pulled out a color photo of a young boy in a powder blue Little League uniform with “Rangers” written across the chest.

Viktor admired the picture, then asked Boltzmann which of his three sons it was.

When Boltzmann replied that it was Nicky, who was nine, an alarm went off in Viktor’s head. The mnemonic device he’d used to remember the names of Boltzmann’s three children was that they were A, B, and C – Andy, Bill, and Casey. Boltzmann’s wife was an “N,” like him – Nina – but there was no “N” among the boys.

Viktor did his best to not show any reaction.

Boltzmann didn't seem to have noticed that he’d made a mistake. And now he went on to say that, sadly, Nina was now thinking that she definitely would not move to Hartford, that they were going to separate.

Viktor thought it was odd that Boltzmann was so matter-of-fact about the separation, and also wondered if Boltzmann had made the “Nicky” mistake on purpose, as a way of signaling to him that something was wrong, a bit like the story his father had told him about the captured pilot who had been forced to confess during a TV show aired by the North Vietnamese, but had used his eyes to blink the Morse code letters for the word "[T-O-R-T-U-R-E](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torture "Torture")".

#          #          #

After their game, the two men sat for a while. This was when Viktor noticed, for the time, that Boltzmann seemed to keep the basement unusually warm. Previously, he’d thought it was just the heat from the oil burner. Now he saw that his neighbor had a couple space heaters going. Also, Boltzmann was wearing a sweater.

Things heated up even more, so to speak, when they talked politics. Boltzmann was pleased when Viktor said that he was a Republican, but less pleased when Viktor added that he was actually more of a Libertarian. Viktor said he believed in “free markets” and “free people,” and thought government should be as small possible and stick to its proper role.

“And what, then, is the proper role of government?” Boltzmann asked.

Viktor said that Defense was the biggie, because it was something that we couldn’t handle individually, or even as states. He didn't think we should be meddling in other countries’ affairs, but we needed a strong defense, because we had an enemy with hundreds of nuclear missiles aimed at us.

Boltzmann nodded vigorously.

“At the same time,” Viktor continued, knowing that he was now risking the approval he’d just obtained “I don’t take things as far as my father.”

Sure enough, Boltzmann’s face clouded over.

Viktor explained that his father had been vehemently anti-communist. He’d blamed pretty much everything on the “Commies,” and had stockpiled guns and ammo at their house in New Hampshire against the day when the “Reds” invaded. “He was about a half mile over the edge,” Viktor said.

“Perhaps that was because he had seen the other side?” Boltzmann suggested.

When Viktor asked him what he meant by that, he said that Viktor’s father had seen all that when he was a CIC Agent.

Viktor’s brain kicked into warp speed. He didn't think he had ever mentioned that to Boltzmann. And he couldn’t think of anyone else he might have mentioned it to who might have mentioned it to Boltzmann, especially given that Boltzmann didn't talk to anyone.

“Well,” Viktor said, “he might have and he might not have. He was kind of mysterious about that subject.”

“As all spooks are,” Boltzmann grinned.

And, for the second time that evening, Viktor thought that Boltzmann was trying to suggest something that he wasn't allowed to say directly.

36)                Viktor’s 41st Birthday Non Party

Viktor’s birthday (April 29th) fell on a Monday that year, and as he drove to work he imagined that when he arrived at his office there would be an envelope taped to his door asking him to see HR at 9:00, and when – worried to death – he went over there, they all laughed and said they just wanted to tell him that he’d been promoted to Level IV at the eleventh hour. But that couldn’t be true, because HR never laughed.

Driving home that evening he recalled the previous year’s fiasco, when what Marlena had advertised as a quiet dinner with the Danahers exploded into a surprise party with forty people and joke presents that included hair loss tonic, wires for jump-starting your sex life, a box of adult diapers, a large-type crossword puzzle book, and a bag of marbles to replace the ones Viktor had lost.

Marlena had sworn that nothing like that would happen this year, and it didn't. In fact Marlena was at the hospital when Viktor got home from work this year. They’d celebrated his birthday the previous night, but the cake was just a cupcake with a single candle, and there was only one present – a cardigan.

Marlena asked him if this was better than last year – if she had honored his request and given him the kind of birthday celebration he wanted.  Viktor said yes, she had, but he didn't add a joke, the way he would have if it had really been just the kind of birthday celebration he really wanted – for example, he didn't say that the only thing that would have made it better would have been if she’d gotten him another box of those big boy diapers, because he had used up the ones from last year. When Marlena accepted his comment, and then carried his dessert plate out to the kitchen for her to clean up, Viktor felt sad and alone. He told himself that it was because he had turned 41 without making Level IV, or accomplishing any other noble goals, and that was all true, but in this moment he was disappointed that Marlena hadn't made more of a to-do about his birthday.

#          #          #

Since Marlena was at work on his actual birthday, Viktor had hoped that he could shoot pool with Boltzmann, but his neighbor’s window shade was down. Viktor went into his study and retrieved one of his cat books from their hiding place in the bottom of a filing cabinet.  Thinking that maybe he could borrow a line or two for his comedy routine, he flipped through “Kitty Kare.”  One of his favorite chapters was titled, “How to Teach Kitty to Read.”

Step 1:  Show kitty the letter “A” and ask kitty to say “A.”

Step 2:  If kitty says “A,” say “good kitty” and advance to letter “B.”

Step 3:  If kitty does not say “A,” or says “a,” whack kitty with commercially available rolled up newspaper or flexible stick and repeat Step 1. Continue repeating Step 1 until kitty says “A,“ then proceed to Step 2, and so on, advancing through the alphabet until kitty can read.

That cheered him up a squinch.

Next, he pulled out a videocassette of one of his favorite movies, about a commando raid in WWII, back when Marlena’s father was daydreaming about Marlene Dietrich.

Because Viktor had recorded this movie off-the-air, it had commercials for knives so sharp they could slice through tin cans, and glue so strong that construction workers could dangle from their helmets. During one of the commercials he took the cardigan out of the box and confirmed that it was, as he’d known from the first moment he saw it, too blue. Not as pale a blue as Boltzmann’s ostensible son’s Little League uniform, but not far from it. Marlena had said three times that she’d gotten it because it brought out the blue in his eyes, but Viktor knew that the real reason was that it had been on sale was that it was too blue.

The other reason she might have bought it was that she had been wearing brighter colors herself. A lot of women, when they get heavier, wore all black, but Marlena went for brighter colors, especially purple. To look more arty, or upbeat, Viktor thought.

The movie started up again. Allied commandos had detonated charges in a German dam, almost killing themselves in the process. But it was a sturdy, German dam, so nothing happened. The faces of other commandos who were watching from a hillside fell in disappointment. Then, a tiny crack appeared. Viktor pressed the pause button and froze the scene on a close-up of the dam with the tiny cracks. He held it there for the longest time, the image twitching like a racehorse in the gate.

Finally, he let the video go. Now the cracks widened, jets of spray shot out, and eventually, the whole dam gave way, sending zillions of tons of water rushing downstream to wipe out a bridge, which was the real objective of the commandos, who were now, ironically, trapped on the wrong side of the river.

Viktor smiled.

#          #          #

After finishing that movie, Viktor started another one – one of the “Star Wars” trilogy – and allowed himself another glass of wine, because it was his birthday, and Marlena wouldn’t be home until 11:45.

At about 10:30 – just as Yoda was telling Luke, “Do, or do not. There is no try” – the phone rang with a wrong number, and suddenly Viktor – by now two sheets to the wind – had the idea of calling Paula Bancroft. When she picked up he would just sing, as he had in Cincinnati, “Hey, hey, Paula.”  Not that he was the only person who’d ever used that line with her, but she’d made him feel like he was.

Of course Viktor immediately thought of three reasons why he shouldn’t call her. Number one, it was too late, even if it was only 9:30 in Minneapolis. Number two, Paula was married, and if her husband answered the phone it would be very awkward. Viktor would also be incriminating himself, since the long-distance call would show up on their phone bill. And, number three, the last time he’d checked, Viktor himself was also married.
