The phone had also played a role in Cincinnati. On the last night of the boondoggle there had been a dine-around, where different groups of attendees went to different restaurants. He and Paula had gone to the same Italian restaurant, had sat together on the bus back to the hotel, and had then gone up to their rooms in the same elevator. The rest of the group had all gone to the bar, so it was just the two of them in the elevator.

Paula’s room was on 9. Viktor’s was on 11. When the elevator stopped at her floor, he put his hand between the doors to keep them from shutting. She smiled and said goodnight. Then, as she headed for her room, Viktor thought that if she turned and looked back it would mean something. And, sure enough, Paula turned and looked back at Viktor, right as the elevator doors slid shut.

When Viktor got to his hotel room he had a shot of Scotch from the minibar, and stared at the phone so hard the handle melted. After much debate, and a second shot of Scotch, he called her room at close to midnight. He was afraid she’d sound annoyed, or worse. But she didn't.

37)                Till Death Do Us Part

At about 11:00 all hell broke loose outside – lightning, thundering rain, and a wind that shook the walls of Viktor and Marlena’s shoddily-constructed home. He went to the living room and watched a torrential downpour pelt the street, slanting past his lamppost like a meteor shower.

Marlena would be driving home soon, and Viktor knew she hated driving in the rain – especially on an undivided road like Route 44, and particularly at night. He wasn't going to wait up for her, though, because he never did when she was on the Night Shift, and especially not when he’d wound up drinking a whole bottle of wine, even if it was his birthday.

He was about to go to bed, but he was still looking out the window when the phone rang.

Was it the same wrong number again?

Could it be Paula?

Still staring out the picture window, rivers of rain running across it diagonally, Viktor picked up the extension in the living room. Right then a crack of lightning split the sky.

#          #          #

The officer sounded like he was about nineteen.

“To whom am I speaking?” he asked.

Viktor said it was him, Viktor Hal.

Still all-business, the officer asked him if he was the husband of Mrs. Marlene Hal.

Viktor said he was, without bothering to correct her name.

There was a longish pause in which the young officer might have covered the mouthpiece and asked an older office a question. Then he asked him if he could please come down to the police station in Avon.

Viktor asked him what had happened, and the officer said that he was not allowed to give out any information over the phone, but would Mr. Hal please come to the station as soon as possible.

“To the station, or to the hospital?” Viktor asked.

“To the station,” the boy said, his voice cracking a little.

“I’ll be there in twenty minutes,” Viktor said.

As Viktor put down the phone he knew with absolute certainty that Marlena was dead, and he felt a huge emotion rise up in him, thought he wasn't sure what to call it.

There was shock. Of course.

There was also sadness, because this was tragic. He could picture the scene – Marlena peering through the rain streaked windshield, the blurry headlights, her car buffeted by the wind, and by her fears, because she was a fearful driver. She strained her eyes at the wet, blinking white line. Then, somehow, she was on the wrong side of it, and when she looked up, it was there – right in front of her – the doom she had seen coming for years. And just like that she was gone. A good person. A person who had her faults, but someone who had tried, despite her fears, to do good things.

It was terribly sad. But Viktor also felt something else. He was ashamed to admit it, but he felt relief. There was no way he could leave such a good person to whom he had promised to be faithful forever without breaking his “till death do us part” pledge. But if fate should take her … he would be released. And she would be released, too. She had not been any happier than him. There might even be a part of her that welcomed death, as an end to her suffering.

There was another flash of lighting, and a peal of thunder that made the picture window shake, then a pair of headlights came down Excalibur.

“Please,” Viktor thought, “don’t that car turn this way. Don’t let it be her.”

And it wasn't.

#          #          #

Viktor knew Marlena would make it home, and she did, though it was well after midnight when he heard her heavy footfall in the hall, and then the mattress tilted with her enormity.

“It must have been a tough drive tonight,” he said.

“God, yes,” she groaned.

“I was up,” he said. “I was worried about you.”

To which she might have replied that that was sweet. But what she actually said was, “Did you have a lot to drink tonight, Viktor?  I saw an empty wine bottle in the kitchen.”

“I had a couple glasses with dinner. Since it was my birthday. And then, like I said, I was worried about you.”

He was worried that she might comment that that wasn't a reason to drink so much, that she had noticed him drinking more lately, and he should be careful because it could sneak up on you, but she didn't say any of that. All she did was say “Well, good night,” and then rolled over, away from him.

38)                Bobby Fischer Calls

Like many of the people in Avalon Acres, Viktor gave the lawn its first real haircut in early May. Unlike most of them, however, he did it himself, rather than hiring a service. And unlike any of them, he used an old-fashioned reel mower. Because he was kind of a traditionalist. Because he thought the exercise was good for him. And because he didn't have an enormous front lawn, and the grass barely grew in the back, what with the shade from his neighbors trees on one side, and the woods on the other.

Viktor liked being out and doing yard work. And he liked his walks at this time of year. In the woods he spotted wild geranium and Canada anemone. And as he walked through the neighborhood he spotted azalea, bleeding hearts, and many flowering plants for which he didn't know the names. He also found a broken robin’s egg, which felt precious.

#          #          #

It was right about this time that Norbert Boltzmann began to confide in Viktor. In reconstructing the timeline, years later, Viktor was knew that was when it was because George (HW) Bush had just been released from the hospital following his atrial fibrillation incident.

Quayle jokes were flying around like French fries in a fraternity food fight, and when Viktor saw Norbert that evening he couldn't help but tell him a couple that he’d heard on the radio driving home from work.

“You know how I told you about my dad being a Commie hater?” Viktor said.

Out of the corner of his eye Viktor could see Boltzmann nod.

Viktor made the shot, and as the cue ball rolled back down the table he continued. “Well, if my dad is still alive, I'm sure he was happy to see that our president is OK.”

Viktor took a moment to go to the other side of the table for his next shot, and also to insert a pause, and then said, grinning, “I heard that while Bush was having his heart examined, Dan Quayle was ordered to stand at the ready in the waiting room.”

Viktor paused again, as if he was telling the joke to a normal human being, and then delivered the punch line. “That was in case they needed a heart transplant donor,” he said, laughing out loud.

Probably one reason why Boltzmann didn't laugh was that Viktor was winning the game, and Norbert didn't like to lose. Another reason was that he seldom laughed at Viktor’s jokes. But Viktor – full of beans tonight – continued anyway.

“I also understand,” Viktor said, “that this incident has gotten people thinking about the possibility that Dan Quayle might become President. In fact I hear that the Secret Service now has special orders. If President Bush should be shot, they are to immediately shoot Quayle as well.”

Not only did Boltzmann not laugh or even smile, but he frowned.

“I give up,” Viktor said. “I can't make you laugh.”

“I understand that those comments are humorous,” Boltzmann said, “but there are serious matters going on in the world, Viktor, as we have discussed, and I am not sure that they should be the subject of this kind of humor.”

“I think it's because they’re so serious that I have to laugh at them,” Viktor said.

Boltzmann did not appear satisfied by that answer, and was just opening his mouth to reply when the phone rang. That surprised Viktor, because his new neighbor had made a point of saying that he didn’t have a telephone.

Boltzmann noticed the surprise on Viktor face and looked flustered. He quickly explained that it was the fax machine.

Sure enough, Viktor now heard the distinctive fax tones, and then saw a piece of paper curling out of a machine near Boltzmann’s desk.

Boltzmann shifted his weight from foot to foot like a child who needs to go to the bathroom. He clearly wanted to attend to the fax, but he didn't want to do it while Viktor was there.

Viktor told him to go ahead take care of business, that the game could wait a minute, whereupon Boltzmann hurried over. Viktor pretended to look uninterested, but watched carefully as Boltzmann studied the fax, looked up at the ceiling, as if something was written there, then scribbled a reply onto the fax, slid it into the machine, and sent it back to whoever had sent it.

When Boltzmann returned to the pool table he had a conflicted look on his face – like he wanted to tell Viktor something, but he wasn't sure if he could, or should. Viktor, despite the failure of his earlier humor, tried to defuse the tension with a joke.

“From Werner Heisenberg, was it?”

“You are close,” Boltzmann replied, dead serious.

When Viktor looked confused, Boltzmann hesitated for a long moment, then sighed the sigh of a man about to jump off a high diving board, and told him that the fax had been from Bobby Fischer.

“The Bobby Fischer?”

Boltzmann nodded and said yes, that he and Fischer – he didn't call him Bobby – played chess by fax.

“Wow!  Do you ever win?” Viktor asked.

“I have only won once, but we tie about one in twelve games,” Boltzmann said, then added that Fischer had about twenty games going on at any moment and kept them all in his head whereas he – Boltzmann – could only keep three or four in his head at once.

Viktor said that he thought one tie in twelve was pretty darn good against Bobby Fischer.

Boltzmann’s smile widened and his lips parted, as if to say something else, but then closed. He looked down and to his left, as if consulting notes, then looked back up at Viktor and asked him, with some drama, if he could keep a secret.

Viktor replied that he was one of the best secret keepers on the planet. He was going to add that there were secrets people had told him years ago that he hadn't even told himself yet, but decided to skip the levity

Boltzmann still looked torn. But then, after adding that it was just a small secret, and not a dangerous one, he told Viktor that Bobby Fischer was gearing up for a rematch with Boris Spassky. He said they were hoping to schedule it close to the 20th anniversary of their original match, and were considering having it in Eastern Europe.

When Viktor said “Wow!” Boltzmann looked pleased and proud, as if he had hoisted himself higher in Viktor’s estimation.

Boltzmann reminded Viktor of his pledge of secrecy, and then they got back to their pool match. As they played, Viktor wondered if it could possibly be true. On the one hand, he could imagine that Boltzmann was a very good chess player, and if he had somehow bumped into Fischer, well, it could have happened. And it was almost too incredible a thing for Boltzmann to have made it up. So it almost had to be true.

And indeed true it was. A year and a half later Fischer and Spassky would have a rematch. Not only that, but the first game would take place on the very same day – September 2, 1992 – when Viktor would supposedly learn the truth about what had happened to his father.

39)                A Trip to Cleveland, New Hamster

Each year, Viktor and Marlena would spend Mothers’ Days with one of their mothers, and then spend Christmas Day with the other one. This year it was Agatha’s turn for Mother’s Day. It fell on the day after Marlena’s birthday – May 11th – so they decided to kind of join the two events. They’d drive up to New Hampshire on Saturday, have a modest party for Marlena that night, take Agatha out for a Mothers’ Day lunch on Sunday, and then drive back to Avalon that evening.

Viktor dreaded long drives with Marlena because she inevitably used them to up “issues.”  Indeed, right after they swung up onto the Mass Pike – as if entering another state had opened a door – Marlena asked Viktor basically the same question she’d asked him a couple months recently. Whether anything was wrong. Because she thought he seemed “off.”

Viktor replied that if he seemed “off” it was probably the RIF, not that he thought he was personally at risk. There was also his upcoming 20 year anniversary, and the freedom that would come with being fully vested. Not that he had to leave NFG right away, or anything like that, but the opportunity would be there, if he wanted to do something else. Not that he had (sigh) the slightest idea what that something else would be. And, oh yes, maybe part of it was also the “Midlife thing,” you know, being in his forties.

“Maybe I should just give in and buy a red sports car,” he joked.

When Marlena did not utter a chuckle or sigh of amusement he shrugged and said, “I can read your thoughts and you're right. Blue would be more my style.”

Marlena still didn't laugh. Instead, she suggested, a few miles later, that perhaps this transition would be a good time for him to see someone. And it was clear to Viktor that she didn't mean that he should see another woman. She meant a therapist.

Marlena had suggested therapy to Viktor about as many times as she had asked him to please remember to put down the toilet seat. Because she thought he was a “touch” depressed. Viktor found that idea ridiculous, and wanted to tell her that she was the one who had become negative, going on and on about how teachers and politicians were cynical, and the moral fabric of America was coming apart at the seams, so much so that in addition to her Medic Alert bracelet that said “Diabetic,” she should add one that said “Depressed.” 

Instead, Viktor was tempted to tell the old “How many therapists does it take to change a light bulb?” joke (only one psychologist, but it takes two hundred sessions.)  Either that or he might say, with some edge, “Isn't there a pill I can take instead,” because Marlena had several times suggested that Viktor might benefit from either a mild antidepressant or an anti-anxiety drug. Instead, since it was now her birthday, he said, “You might be right. This might be a good time to take a peek inside the old psyche. I’ll give that some thought.”  But he said it as sincerely as he’d said that maybe he would buy a pair of Earth Shoes.

#          #          #

Of the many places Viktor had lived as a boy (Texas, Kansas, California), Cleveland, New Hampshire was the only place he’d ever thought of as home. That was partly because he’d loved the woods and the fields, and because the stars felt close enough to touch. It was also because, after shooting off to a new place almost every year since he was born, his family had finally stuck here, like an arrow, in 1957. And his mother still lived in the eleven room Victorian in which, as Viktor often put it, he had “almost grown up.”

Ever since Viktor Sr. had run off with the waitress from Goffstown, people had been telling Agatha she should downsize, but she continued to insist – even after her husband had been declared legally dead – that Vic, as she called him, would eventually come back. As he had come home from Korea. And from other “assignments and assignations,” as she put it, loving to insert an interesting word here and there.

There were always at least three things wrong with Agatha – one serious, one exaggerated, and one that was just imagined. She embraced these ailments like misbehaving children, and the moment one ailment was resolved (e.g. the mole that was not malignant) a new one (e.g., a tingling in the fingers of her left hand) would raise its hand, so to speak. So what they mostly talked about during Marlena’s birthday celebration was Agatha’s blood pressure, the bunions that prevented her from getting the exercise her doctor recommended, and the voices she’d begun hearing.

Still tired from her Graveyard Shift and the long drive, Marlena went up to bed at about 10:00. Viktor sat with his mother for half an hour sipping a Scotch, while she had her fiftieth coffee of the day (although not a teetotaler, she had never drunk much alcohol). He wanted to ask her if she had any special reason for thinking that Viktor Sr. might still be alive, but he knew she would just say what she’d said the last time he saw her – that she felt it in her bones. He was also tempted to ask her if his father had indeed been a spook, but she would have just said that if she had been sworn to secrecy, she would take those secrets with her to her grave. Instead, her helped her with the crossword she was working, suggesting a solution for a five letter word for “a dreaded Sun Sign” which Agatha pretended to not know, even though was a good chance she was growing some in her lungs.

#          #          #

The next day, when Agatha commented, during lunch at a Chinese restaurant, that Mother’s Day must be tough for Marlena, Viktor just looked down at his plate. Agatha had not said it with any sense of commiseration. Rather, her intent had been to communicate that, very directly and unequivocally, that she, as Viktor Sr.’s wife, supported his view that Viktor should have accepted the divorce Marlena had offered and found someone who would give him an heir. If so, he would not have run off with the waitress, though he might have still had a secret assignation, from time to time.

Marlena replied that she regretted not having had children of her own, but she had hundreds, at the hospital. She said it in a sweet and loving voice, but Viktor felt that he detected something else behind her sweetness.

#          #          #

On the drive home Viktor suggested that maybe they should move up to Cleveland, once he had his twenty years. There was plenty of room in the house, and his mother could use some help. Marlena could easily find work in the hospitals in Manchester, and he could get his CPA and start a one man accounting practice. He could also have a vegetable garden, he said, which would help to ground him in a way that he missed, living in the suburbs.

Viktor was not only (a) saying things that Marlena herself had said several times before, he was also (b) kind of answering the question that she’d asked him on the drive to New Hampshire, about what was wrong. But Marlena shook her head in a very slow and definite manner, to convey utter intractability, and said no, she could not possibly live with a woman who smoked in the house, and she was quite certain that Agatha would not quit on her behalf.

“No,” Viktor sighed, “I don’t think she would.”  And not on his behalf. Or even her own.

40)                “Now There’s an Idea for You!”

When Viktor saw Boltzmann a couple days later he told him how, on the Sunday morning when he was in New Hampshire, he’d gotten up earlier than Marlena and Agatha and gone for a walk outside, and how the lilac bushes alongside the house were in bloom, not mentioning that the smell had reminded him of a certain young lady he’d had a crush on when he was a boy.

Viktor had headed up through the apple orchard into the woods of his boyhood, loving the smell of the apples, which had not changed since he was a boy, and went straight to a south-facing hillside where he’d always found bloodroot and hepatica at this time of year, and to his joy, they were still there. From there he hiked up to the clearing in the woods where he’d met the aliens, thirty years earlier, and saw that the teensy white pines were now fifty feet high, such that it would have been impossible for a ship to hover there now.

As he circled back to his old house he’d noted, with pleasure, that you could still make out a rectangular patch in the field out back – like an old scar –where he’d had his boyhood vegetable garden, and he’d had the thought that maybe he should start a vegetable garden right here in Avalon.

“I realized,” Viktor said, “that I really miss the connection to the land that I had up there, and a garden might help to ground me, no pun intended.”

“That sounds like an excellent idea,” Boltzmann said.

“The time is right, too,” Viktor added. “The last frost day around here is right around Memorial Day, and it would take me a week or so to get the ground ready, so now is the time, but …”

Boltzmann cocked his head. “But what?”

Viktor made a face. “Well, I actually had this idea once before, or maybe twice, but the problem is that our backyard is very shady, so I’d be very limited in what I could grow. It gets OK light in the morning, when the sun is over your house, but it goes shady right after noon because of the trees that my neighbor on the other side planted to protect his … so-called privacy. Even the grass on that side of the our backyard has trouble growing. It looks kind of like my hair.”

Boltzmann looked like he was formulating an idea.

“I thought about cutting his trees down,” Viktor said, with a knavish smile, “you know, one weekend when he was getting sloshed on his boat – that's what he and his wife do every weekend starting right about now, hang out on their boat and get sloshed – but that would be pretty extreme, especially now that those trees are forty or fifty feet tall. And I like trees.”

“What about your front yard?” Boltzmann asked.

Viktor shook his head. “No, there are no trees out there.”

“I meant, what about planting your garden in the front yard?”

“You mean where the lawn is?”

“Yes,” he said, with an adolescent gleam in his eye.

Viktor regarded his new neighbor with incredulity, like how could even someone as socially maladroit as Boltzmann make such a silly suggestion. But then Viktor’s blue eyes twinkled, and he exclaimed, with a grin, “Now there’s an idea for you!”

#          #          #

While they continued the game, Viktor mulled the possibility that Boltzmann had mentioned, but concluded that putting a garden in the front lawn was undoubtedly forbidden in the Avalon Acres Homeowner’s Association’s bylaws, as were many other things – like painting your house with non-Colonial colors, using fluorescent lighting in outside fixtures, the display of political or commercial advertising with the exception of approved real estate signs, placing any statuary on the lawn, and the erection of any but split-rail fences. But Viktor didn't want to have to tell his neighbor that a garden in the front lawn was probably prohibited. Because he didn't know for sure. And he didn't want to sound like a wimp.

But then he thought that there was something else – something non-wimpy – that he could tell him. So after they sat down for their post-game conversation, before Boltzmann could press his garden suggestion, Viktor exclaimed, “Oh, there’s something else I’ve been meaning to tell you about.”

Boltzmann regarded him warily.

“I have a hunch you’ll like this, but I have to swear you to secrecy.”

It was as if an air raid siren went off with the word “secrecy.”  Boltzmann shot bolt upright and extended his hand in the “stop” gesture of a traffic cop.

“No,” he said, shaking his head very sharply.

“No, what?” Viktor asked.

“No, I do not want to hear your secret. Not now. Not here.”  And as he said the word “here” Viktor felt that Boltzmann’s eyes rotated inside their sockets, so that while he continued to look straight at Viktor it was as if he was also looking through the side of his head at the mirror at the end of the pool table.

Viktor laughed. “I didn't really mean it was secret,” he said. “Actually, it's more like funny. It's something that I'm thinking of doing at work that could maybe get me fired, but probably wouldn’t because I'm so close to my retirement age already.

Now Boltzmann put down his hand, and Viktor told him about the “Get Your Act Together” contest, and how he had had the idea of lampooning management, but now he thought he would lampoon cats.

“I’d rather harpoon them,” he joked, “but I would settle for lampooning them.”

At the mention of cats Boltzmann went very quiet, and studied Viktor’s face carefully. Finally he asked him if he did not like cats, and when Viktor said that he couldn’t stand the little monsters Boltzmann asked him if he had ever acted on his feelings.

“If I see one in the road I will sometimes steer right at it, until their eyes bug and they leap out of the way.”

“But have you ever ... killed one.”

“I guess not,” Viktor said, feeling like a wimp again.

#          #          #

When Viktor got home it wasn't the image of a garden that he pictured. Or the image of a cat in the middle of the road. It was the image of Boltzmann looking suddenly, and with enormous fear, at the mirror at the end of the pool table.

He went to Marlena office, where she had a number of medical books, and looked up “paranoid schizophrenia,”  where he read:  “One may feel that he or she has been singled out for harm, for example that the government is monitoring every move you make. One may also have delusions of grandeur – the belief that he or she can fly, is famous, or has a relationship with a famous person.”

“Like Bobby Fischer?” Viktor wondered.

But then he thought, no, he was just having these thoughts because Boltzmann was pushing him to do something. And he was afraid to do anything. But if Boltzmann was crazy, then who was he to tell Viktor to do anything.

On the other hand, Viktor did want to do something. Whatever that might be.

41)                Shake It Up

After the annual employee meeting Viktor took the elevator up to a men’s room on another floor, because the ones outside the auditorium were mobbed with men who’d had to hold it too long. He went into a stall, locked the door behind him, and then held the t-shirt he’d just received out in front of him. As it unfurled, he laughed hard enough to shake, but with more irony than when he’d received the nineteen previous t-shirts.

Viktor liked to tell his co-workers that every year’s company theme could be summed up in the same four letter word.

If a timid fellow raised an eyebrow at mention of a “four letter word,” Viktor would run his hand in the air like he was erasing a blackboard and say, no, no, he didn’t mean that kind of four letter word.

At this his victim would usually cock their head, wondering what word that was.

The word was “more.”  The core message of every annual employee meeting, Viktor would explain, was that the company wanted more: more sales, more productivity, more market share, more policy holders. And, above all else, more profit. In some years the way to achieve “more” was through vigorous growth in the amount of business (“Pump it Up”). In other years the method was to work faster (“In High Gear”). In some year the key was to be more progressive (“Lead the Way”). And in many years the idea was to behave like a steroid-enhanced athlete (“Go for the Gold,” “Out to Win,” “Refuse to Lose”). But the desired result was always the same: more.

Before revealing this year’s theme, Lloyd Withington III, NFG’s president had spoken about how success in the 90’s depended on doing things in new ways. In this last decade before the next millennium, he said, pointing boldly toward the future, companies needed to question every assumption and strike out boldly in new directions. One line that Viktor had actually liked was a quote from some management guru who’d written that “Sacred cows make the best burgers.”

As a Level III, Viktor was privy to at least the shallower levels of the company’s deeper thinking, so as he heard Withington speak about “challenging the limits” and “building on success,” he knew that what he really meant was, “We’ve already automated the hell out of our clerical staff. Now, let’s use business process software to optimize our workflow so we don’t need as many overpaid middle managers – especially the middle-aged white guys.”

Of course NFG’s esteemed president would never come right out and say that. And the t-shirt didn’t say it either. What the t-shirt said, in tipsy, colorful letters was, SHAKE IT UP!

Viktor actually liked this theme, and not just because of how cheerfully it disguised the fact that layoffs would soon be announced. He liked it because it also expressed, clearly, what he needed to do with his own life. It was time for him to question every assumption, to strike out boldly in new directions, and eat more red meat.

And so, Viktor did something he had not done in nineteen years. He took off his white undershirt and replaced it with NFG’s FY 91-92 theme shirt. When he came out of the stall and looked at himself in the mirror he noticed that the bright colors of his theme shirt shone dimly through his white shirt. People who didn’t know Viktor might think he was a kiss-ass. People who did know him would think just the opposite – that he was mocking the theme. But that was OK. And only Viktor himself would know that, wearing this t-shirt, he felt a bit like Superman. Or at least Jimmy Olsen.

#          #          #

When Viktor got home from work he went into the master bedroom to change into shorts for his Power Walk. Marlena was in there getting dressed to go to an envelope-stuffing party for her Ed Committee candidate. When she saw him wearing the t-shirt she did a double-take, then asked him if SHAKE IT UP was this year’s company theme. When he said that it was, she expressed surprise that he was wearing the shirt, because he’d always mocked the company’s themes. So he told her the truth, explaining that he was wearing it not for the company, but because he thought it was time for him, too, to shake things up.

Marlena cocked her head and squinted at him from the corner of her eye, trying to judge whether this was some kind of joke.

“Speaking of which, do you maybe have time to “Shake it Up” before you leave for your meeting?” he asked, making big eyes at her as she stood there in her bra and plus-plus underwear.

She did another double-take, then laughed and shook her head, as if to say, “What a crazy nut you are,” and finished getting dressed.

#          #          #

Now that it was warmer, Viktor did his Power Walks in the woods. He was tickled to spot some celandine poppies and Canadian anemone, and lingered by the wetland behind Boltzmann’s house to listen to a pair of red wing blackbirds who seemed to be talking to each other – the conk-la-ree of a male that he could see, up on a high branch, and the short chit-chit-chit’s of a female hidden in the bushes.

Marlena had left for work by the time Viktor got home, and he was pleased to have the house to himself. After he showered he went straight into his study, got out the bylaws of the Homeowners’ Association, and read it while he prepared and then ate his dinner. It was a long, complicated document and it listed all sorts of things that were prohibited, but it didn't look like a front yard garden was one of them. There was even language about preserving the heritage of the land, which was zoned R-A, meaning Residential-Agricultural.

“Yes!” Viktor exclaimed. He could do a garden. And it would be legal.

Viktor opened the front door, stood on the porch, and surveyed his front yard in the silver light of the moon, which was off to the west. Just beyond it, Jupiter, Mars and Venus were all in a line. It felt magical that the planets were arranged like that, and Viktor wished it could be a sign, though he didn't see how it could be, without at the same time being a message for the other 5.4 billion people on the planet. One of those people – he suddenly realized – was Paula Bancroft, and before he could remind himself of the three reasons why it would be a bad idea to call her, he had dialed her number.

He worried that her husband would pick up, but he didn't. As luck would have it, he was off at his Monday night poker game. She said the kids were in bed, too. So Viktor’s timing was “perfect.”

He asked Paula if the sun had set out there and she said it had. Then he asked her if she could see the moon. When she said she couldn’t because it was cloudy in Minneapolis he said that was too bad, and described the incredible line-up of planets.

“It’ll still be there tomorrow night if the sky clears up,” he said, and she said she hoped so because she would like to see it.

They talked for 45 minutes about nothing much. And not once did she ask him to explain why he had called.

42)                McNutty Gets RIFed

When you know something is going to happen, and then it does, it sometimes feels more shocking than if you hadn't known it was going to happen. That's how it was when McNutty got his pink slip.

It was a Friday, of course. The way they did it was to leave a message on your office phone telling you that you had an appointment to speak to someone in HR at such and such a time. They tried to schedule the “executions” in the morning, so that people would have the afternoon to pack their things, and skedaddle, but so many people were released in this RIF that McNutty’s appointment wasn't till 1:00 in the afternoon.

He stopped by Viktor’s office at about 9:45. He knew what HR wanted to talk to him about, he said, with tears in his eyes, and he wanted to say goodbye now, because he didn't think he’d be able to handle it after his appointment.

“I'm very sorry,” Viktor said.

“Thank you,” McNutty said, “but what I could really use right now is a joke.”

“OK,” Viktor said, “One afternoon, Brad Barlow, the esteemed General Manager of SRD, and Caroline O’Donnell who, as we all know, is Senior VP of HR for the whole megillah, decide to jump off the tippity top of our building at exactly the same moment. Don’t ask me why they want to go together, because I'm not allowed to say.”

McNutty began laughing immediately. Or crying. It was hard to tell which. After a few moments of that he wiped his eyes and asked, with a smile, “Hey, if I jumped off the top of our building without a parachute how long would it take me to hit the ground?”

“Well,” Viktor replied, “the formula is that “s,” the distance, being 527 feet, equals one half of “a,” the acceleration, or gravitational constant, so 16, times “t,” the time, squared. So …you would be airborne for the square root of 527 divided by 16. And the square root of 32.9375 is (pause to search brain) 5.739 seconds. But that ignores the friction of the air, so let’s call it about 6 seconds. But please tell me you're not really going to jump.”

“I could never climb all the way to the tippity top, and if you're not going to jump from the top, what’s the point. Also, I might land on one of our insured’s.”

Viktor laughed, and McNutty looked pleased that he’d made a joke, albeit inadvertently.

“I'm really sorry,” Viktor said. That was the advice Marlena would have given him – to say “Oh My God How Awful!” – but he didn't say it for that reason. He said it because he truly meant it.

“Thank you,” McNutty said, accepting it with the sincerity with which it had been offered. And a few minutes later he was gone.

#          #          #

Although only two people on the 11th floor had become redundant, Viktor felt like he saw them passing his window all morning long, each victim, carrying a small cardboard box, and accompanied by a Rent-a-Cop.

This being a Friday, Viktor went to Carl’s for (fish and chips) lunch. There was a mirror on the side wall, the purpose of which was undoubtedly to make the tiny deli appear twice as large as it wasn't. But it reminded Viktor of the mirror at Norbert H. Boltzmann’s rec room, and how his neighbor had suggested that there were people behind that mirror, watching.

Suddenly, Viktor combined that thought with another time when he’d sat here, and a theory leaped into his mind with such sharpness that he thought it must be true. That theory was that the same government that was watching Norbert was watching him, and the inquiries that HR had received about Viktor were not about a secret mission to rescue his father, but had to do somehow with Norbert H. Boltzmann.

“So maybe Boltzmann isn't a paranoid schizophrenic,” Viktor murmured to himself. Then he cracked a smile and added, “Maybe I’m the paranoid schizophrenic.”

#          #          #

Driving home from work that afternoon, Viktor rolled down his windows for the first time this year and took in the air. It smelled of dirt, and Viktor thought he really would like to plant a vegetable garden in the front lawn, but he also thought he should talk to Marlena about it first. In the meantime, though, he had a smaller but related idea. He would stop in at the Garden Supply Center on Route 44 and buy a small rhododendron bush to replace the one that had died in the winter – one that would fit in the trunk or back seat. He and Marlena had already talked about that, and it would be a nice project for the weekend.

As Viktor approached the Garden Center, though, everything changed, because parked right in front of the store was a bright red Roto-Tiller with a blaze orange sign that said “Rent Me.”

“I think it was a bunch of things that pushed me over the edge,” he would later tell Dr. French. “First, I really was itching to have a garden. Also, I was mad about McNutty being let go, even if I didn't disagree with the decision. And finally, I hate to admit this, but I think the bright Red Roto-Tiller reminded me of the sports car I wasn't going to buy.”

#          #          #

When Viktor got home he looked out at his front lawn and thought that Saturday morning would be the very worst time to do what he was about to do because all the neighbors would be home from work. Therefore, he thought, with a wicked grin, it would be the very best time to do it.

He didn't say a word about it to Marlena at dinner. The Roto-Tiller would be delivered at 8:30 in the morning, but she would have left for her 7AM shift long before that. And by the time she got home, at 4:15 or so, it would be a done deal. “And it's easier to ask for forgiveness than permission,” he thought.

When Marlena noticed an “odd” look on his face, he told her that McNutty had been let go.

Marlena’s eyes – which looked tiny because her face was so vast – tunneled in anger. She shook her head and went into a diatribe about how the social contract between management and labor had been broken, and how that boded ill for society. They had a union at the hospital, but fewer and fewer people had union protection these days. Meanwhile the big companies were “raking” in huge profits.

Viktor was tempted to suggest that maybe they should use leaf blowers instead of rakes, but he resisted the temptation.

#          #          #

That night he had a dream. He’d decided to turn the front lawn over in the dark, because if you expose the seeds of dormant weeds to sunlight it wakes them up and you get more weeds. He didn't want to wake Marlena, or alert the neighbors, so he was doing the job by hand – outlining tile-sized squares of grass with his old Boy Scout knife, and then peeling them back.

The peeling image must have reminded him of scalping, because when he pulled back the next square it revealed the bony material of a skull, gleaming faintly in the moonlight. He brushed away the soil and saw a face, with two eye sockets. They were dark, but alive with squirming worms. Then the worms glistened into eyes, and not just any eyes – they were Norbert H. Boltzmann’s dark eyes, and they were looking at him.

Viktor sat up in bed with his mouth gaped open. He was afraid Marlena would ask him what he’d been dreaming about, but either she hadn't noticed or – a trick she might have learned from him – she was just pretending she hadn't.

43)                The Roto-Tiller Wasn't as Loud as Viktor Had Hoped

At 5:00 on Saturday morning, Viktor was awakened by Whitney Houston singing “I’ll be Your Baby Tonight.”  As Marlena hit the OFF button, Viktor got up to pee, but then popped back under the covers, because he went crazy and let himself sleep till 6:00 on the weekend. By then Marlena would be out in the kitchen, having oatmeal and fruit. She’d pop her head into the bathroom to say good-bye, while he showered and shaved, but would be gone before he got out to the kitchen.

It went just like that this morning except that after she said good-bye and closed the bathroom door she opened it again and asked him if he was OK.

“As far as I know,” he replied, pretending to examine his torso for bullet wounds or missing limbs. “Why?”

“You were tossing and turning all night,” she said.

“RIF dreams,” he offered, and she seemed willing to accept that.

Viktor remained in the bathroom until he heard the garage door motor up and then thud back down. Then he came out into the house that was (wonderfully) all his own.

At 8:30 a kid earing a Phish t-shirt pulled into Viktor’s driveway with the red sports car and six bags of peat moss. The truck’s radio blared “U Can’t Touch This” as the kid rolled the Roto-Tiller down a ramp and lumped the bags of peat moss onto the edge of the driveway.

The kid ogled Viktor’s SHAKE IT UP t-shirt, perhaps thinking that it was from some 60’s band. He lingered, maybe hoping for a tip, asked Viktor if he wanted him to lump everything around to the back, but when Viktor said no thanks, this would do just fine, the kid whined the truck back in reverse, and sped off.

Viktor sensed that a number of people – including Chuck, Flat Top Nelson – were now watching him from behind curtains, and were thinking, “Finally, that irresponsible man is going to do something about that awful lawn of his.”  But they were in for a surprise.

Viktor used four of the eight peat moss bags to mark a rectangle about 25 feet wide and 45 feet long in the center of the lawn – the area that was pretty much always in sun.  He eyeballed it from the sidewalk, shifted the bags a tad to the west, then eyeballed it from the driveway and shifted the rectangle a few feet toward the street. Once he got the area centered, like a picture in a mat of green, he pulled the starter on the Roto-Tiller. The machine wasn't as loud as he’d hoped, but he loved the way the energy vibrated up through the handles when he chunked it in gear and began cutting into the grass.

Viktor’s front yard was mostly fill, which had been used to lift these homes a few inches above the wetland, so the machine cut through the soil easily, and Viktor was disappointed by how quickly the work went. He had the rectangle turned up in less than an hour, so he took another run through in the opposite direction.

Viktor was in the midst of that second pass when Richard Troy’s wife, Helen, (her real name) came by on her Power Walk. She gave the earthy rectangle a quizzical look, cast an eye at our hero-to-be’s dirty SHAKE IT UP t-shirt, and then asked him if he was replacing just the lawn in just the middle.

When Viktor said no, he was putting in a garden, she pinched her face and asked if it was going to be a Japanese garden. When he said no, it was going to be an American vegetable garden, she pretended to look confused and asked him what kind of vegetable garden. To which he replied, “The kind of vegetable garden with vegetables in it. You know, corn and tomatoes and things of that ilk, the kind of food that people used to grow back when real people lived here.”  And maybe he said it with a bit too much sarcasm because she turned and headed back to her own house walking even faster than when she’d arrived.

This was back before everyone had cell phones, so it was a full ten minutes before Richard Troy himself drove up in his BMW. Richard was a lawyer was, as someone had said, “as crooked as a barrel of fish hooks.”  He asked, in a deep voice, if Viktor believed if it was permitted under the Association Bylaws to put a vegetable garden in your front lawn. Viktor said that as far as he could tell it was not prohibited. Troy retorted that “not prohibited” was not the same as “allowed,” and asked if Viktor had consulted the Zoning Bylaw. Viktor said that he had, and this was a Residential-Agricultural zone, so he thought he was well within his rights. He could even keep some small animals if he liked, though pigs were specifically excluded.

“And do you think your neighbors will be pleased by this?” Troy asked in a sharp tone.

Viktor smiled, and then just said, “Frankly Charlotte …” even though he knew that the man’s name was Richard.

#          #          #

By the time Marlena got home the Roto-Tiller was gone. Viktor had worked in the peat moss, leveled the soil, marked the rows, and put up a trellis for the snow peas. His plan was to buy the seeds and seedlings on Sunday, and begin planting. He was still a few days short of the traditional frost-free date, but the traditional frost-free date pre-dated global warming.

Someone had phoned Marlena at work because, instead of putting her car in the garage, she left it in the driveway and marched over to him, her face twisted in anger.

“What’s all this?” she asked.

“A garden,” Viktor replied with a too-wide smile. “A vegetable garden. Remember I tried some tomatoes out back, but they didn't get enough light,  Out here will be great. We’ll have fresh vegetables all summer.”

She didn’t look at the work he had done. Instead, she looked at him. Then she rotated her head and surveyed the neighborhood, where people were undoubtedly watching from many windows. And then she spun and rolled into the house, but Viktor had a hunch that his answer had not totally satisfied here.

When they talked about it at dinner Viktor made a number of good points: that this whole area had once been a farm; that he would use organic growing methods; and that they had previously agreed that he was in charge of all “Lawn and Garden” matters.

Marlena said that she was very concerned about what the neighbors thought. The thing she didn’t say – although this would come out later, in their counseling sessions – was that this was the moment when she decided that Viktor was definitely having some kind of Midlife Crisis, but it was just as well that she didn’t, because Viktor had his answered prepared.

“Well then,” he would say, with a defiant look, “are you suggesting that, instead of the garden, you would rather that I do what some of the other men in the neighborhood have done when they had Midlife Crises, and have an affair with a blonde half my age plus seven?”

44)                Put this note in your pocket

When they shot pool a few days later, Boltzmann mentioned the front-lawn garden in a tone that indicated that he was pleased to see that Viktor had taken some action, and Viktor would have been pleased that his new neighbor was pleased except that the project was already on hold. As expected, Richard Troy had protested to the Association, and Viktor had received an official letter telling him to cease and desist activities until the matter had been resolved, or risk a fine of $200 per week.

“And $200 a week is a ton of tomatoes,” Viktor sighed.

When Boltzmann looked at him disapprovingly, Viktor added that he’d read the section of the Bylaws that Troy had referred to and, unfortunately, he thought the man might be on solid ground, so to speak. There was language about “maintaining the propriety and aesthetic character of the neighborhood,” so-called, that looked like it might apply, and the fact that 107 out of the 108 members of the Association would side with Troy would work against him.

“In my heart I knew that they wouldn’t like it,” Viktor said, “and I guess that was part of the reason the idea appealed to me so much. It wasn't just the idea of fresh tomatoes. If the truth be told, I'm not even that wild about tomatoes unless you put a ton of salt on them, and I'm supposed to be on a low-salt diet.”

Boltzmann then asked him about his Get Your Act Together routine.

Viktor shook his head and said he had decided to not do that, either.

“It was the same deal,” Viktor said. I think I just wanted to do it to offend people. And cracking jokes is probably not the answer to my, my … he didn't want to say “Midlife Crisis” but couldn’t think of another word.

“Angst,” Boltzmann suggested.

“Yes,” Viktor nodded, “that's a great word for it!”  He tried to nod and smile his way to a better mood, but his head drooped back down and the smile wilted.

“Also,” he said, “I think the problem is that, just as I saw that Richard Troy had a point, I can also see the company’s point of view. We’re in the middle of a ‘grand readjustment’ in which American companies need to become competitive with overseas companies that have much lower labor costs. We really do need to increase productivity and ‘right-size’ our labor. Otherwise not just McNutty, but everyone will be out of a job.”

Boltzmann looked even more displeased. At this point in time, Viktor did not yet realize that Boltzmann’s felt as trapped as he did. He just took the look to mean that Boltzmann thought he was a wimp. He had not bought a red sports car, had not gotten hair implants, or had an affair with a younger woman. He had not decided to go back and get his PhD in Astrophysics. He was not standing up to the Association. He was not lampooning management. In fact, there wasn't much that he was doing.

But then he remembered that yes, there was something else.

“There is one other thing,” Viktor said, in a small but hopeful voice.

Boltzmann looked intrigued, so Viktor continued.

“At the same time that I see the company’s position, I do think the company goes too far sometimes. Not just in their treatment of people like McNutty, but in their treatment of society as a whole.

Now Viktor described several oil spills, drug scandals, and financial shenanigans that had been in the news in the last few years, each of which indicated to him that if there was a buck to be made by bending or breaking a rule, your modern corporation would not hesitate to break it. “And that,” Viktor said, building up some speed, “is something that I might like to do something about. And it isn't just a comedy routine I'm thinking of.”

Boltzmann definitely looked intrigued now, if not by what Viktor had said, then by the force with which he’d said it – a force that had surprised Viktor himself.

“This is something I would very much like to hear about,” Boltzmann said.

“It's what I called ‘Plan Z,’ but you would have to promise to not tell anyone about it. Not a living soul. Because it really could be dangerous, and this time I mean it.”

At the word “dangerous” the Wary Boltzmann’s eyes exploded and he looked quickly at the mirror. When he looked back at Viktor he shook his head slowly from side to side as if to say, “Stop, no, do not say this thing in front of the mirror.”

Viktor had at least two reactions. One was that Boltzmann might indeed be a paranoid schizophrenic. The other was that he kind of hoped he wasn't, and there really was a watcher back there, because that would really be something!

Boltzmann reflected for a long, long moment. Then he said, in a stiff voice, “Please wait here, Viktor, I need to attend to something upstairs.”

Boltzmann leaned his cue against the wall and headed for the stairs. As he passed the mirror Viktor did a double-take, because it looked like he wasn't reflected in t, though Viktor might have blinked.

#          #          #

When Boltzmann came back downstairs he said, in his Bad Actor Voice, “I am sorry but I may be coming down with something. I think I should retire. Excuse me, I'm very sorry.”

Viktor said not to worry, there was a bug going around, and they headed up the stairs. Halfway up, Viktor saw a note taped to the wall. It hadn't been there when they came down the stairs, so this must be what Boltzmann had been up to. It said, in block letters: “Put this note in your pocket. Do not indAcate (SIC) in any way that you have received it. Read it when you get home.”

Viktor put the note in his pocket.

Boltzmann showed him to the door, apologized again, and said goodnight. When they shook hands, Viktor thought Boltzmann’s hand felt cold, so maybe he was coming down with something.

Viktor walked home as naturally as possible. When he got home he went into his study, pulled down the window shade, and read the note. It told him that Norbert wanted to talk to him, but the house was bugged. However, if Viktor wished to speak candidly about Plan Z, he should wait till 10:15 PM, then go around to the back of Boltzmann’s house, moving as quietly as possible. On the edge of the woods there was an old tool shed. Viktor was to open the door quietly and wait in there.

“Wow,” thought Viktor.

45)                The Tinfoil Shed

To help the time pass Viktor had a glass of wine, and thought all sorts of crazy thoughts about what might be in the shed, hoping that S&M toys were not one of the things.

Finally the hour arrived, and Viktor snuck through the woods to the shed. It was in a low area of Boltzmann’s backyard, bordering the wetland. The ground was soft, and there was skunk cabbage all around. Viktor could smell it even more in the darkness.

When he pushed on the door, a wave of warm air hit him, for there was a heater in the small room, but that was far from the most surprising aspect. The cramped room was entirely covered – not just the walls but also the floor and the ceiling – in tinfoil. Behind the tinfoil on the walls and ceiling there was insulation. In one corner of the room there was a machine that looked like a dehumidifier, but which Viktor thought was probably a white noise machine. There were several gizmos with digital read outs hanging on the wall. In the center of the room were two aluminum beach chairs. Over each there was an umbrella also covered in tinfoil.

It reminded Viktor of something, but he couldn’t remember what it was. Not until he sat in one of the beach chairs and reached, kind of automatically, for the spot in the arm rest where the lounger on the alien ship had had a cup holder with a can of Dr. Pepper in it.

It was about ten minutes – that felt like a hundred – before Boltzmann entered the tool shed. He was wearing a parka and his trapper hat, and kept them both on even though the room was quite warm.

He closed the door carefully behind him and then pulled another sheet of tinfoil over the door, but before he said a word he took one of the gizmos off the wall and waved it over Viktor’s body.

“Can I trust you?” he asked, when he was satisfied that Viktor wasn't wired in any way.

Viktor said yes, he could, and asked him what this was all about.

Boltzmann explained that his house was bugged, and he was under close surveillance whenever he left, so he had constructed this room as a place where they could speak candidly, while “they” thought he was sleeping.

Viktor asked if they couldn’t have just met at his house, but Boltzmann said that Viktor’s house was probably bugged too.

Viktor was flattered. “You built all this just so that you could talk to me?” he said.

Boltzmann said yes, but he needed to warn Viktor that he was not 100% sure about this room. It was possible that they had figured it out but pretended not to know in order to entrap him. “In which case you will also be caught in the trap,” he said.

When Viktor asked who “they” were, Boltzmann explained that they were the United States of America. He was working on a very secret project, so secret that he was surprised they have allowed him to see Viktor at all.

“You must have come up exceptionally clean on their background check,” he said.

“What background check?” Viktor was about to ask, then remembered the calls HR had received, the car that had followed him, and the clicks on the phone line.

Boltzmann cleared his throat. “I became worried for your safety when you said you had a dangerous secret you wanted to tell me. Do you still want to tell it to me?”

Viktor nodded, and Boltzmann looked eager to hear what it was, but when Viktor said that he would have to promise to never tell anyone, because it really might be dangerous, Boltzmann’s look changed and he said he couldn’t do that because everyone talked under torture.

Viktor said that he’d read something along those lines in one of his father’s spy thrillers, but he didn't think it would come to that in this case, and told him he would understand if he ever told anyone about Plan Z after being tortured.

So now Viktor told him about the skin cancer correlation he thought he’d found, and the company people who had visited him, and the idea he’d had of uncovering secrets like that, and revealing them for the good of mankind. He’d have to do it anonymously, he said, because he’d gotten the sense that it could be dangerous. And maybe that was better, anyway, to just do his good deeds in secret, kind of like Zorro, he said, which was where the “Z” in “Plan Z” had come from. It was Project Zorro.

“How will you keep your work anonymous?” Boltzmann asked.

Viktor said that he could send “hermetically sealed” messages to newspapers. No fingerprints. No DNA. No nothing.

“And what about your research?” Boltzmann asked. “How will you cover your digital footprints?”

Viktor admitted that that would be more difficult.

“They will find you almost immediately,” Boltzmann said. “You have no idea how sophisticated their tools are. They are very likely watching you even now, and if you do this there is a high probability that one day you will be sitting in your living room, reading, and the phone will ring, and the moment you pick it up …”  He used his hands and eyes to express the idea of a large explosion resulting in Viktor’s kaputness.

It wasn't just Boltzmann what had said, it was how he’d said it. He’d said it as if he knew all this from personal experience and had literally seen it happen several times before.

“So you don’t think I should do this?” Viktor asked.

Boltzmann leaned toward him. “Let me be clear, Viktor. I am not saying that you should not proceed with Plan Z. I think you should, because you need to do something. I am only saying that if you do, you must go in with your eyes open and take suitable precautions. Otherwise it could end very, very badly.”

#          #          #

Now Viktor asked Boltzmann if he could tell him what kind of secret work he was involved in. because he couldn’t imagine what he could be doing at The General.

“Think,” replied Boltzmann. “What do they have at The General?   Something you also have at NFG, for that matter.”

“Huge computers?”

Boltzmann smiled a toothy smile.

“Huge computers that are,” Viktor continued, “at least in the case of The General, buried eleven stories deep underground, if the rumors are true, to prevent their files from being erased by electromagnetic interference or some massive catastrophe above ground.

“If the rumors are true,” Boltzmann replied with a wink.

“What you use them for?”

“I can not tell you that,” he said. “For your own good. And mine.”

Viktor wanted to ask him if his work had to do with Star Wars, that he was using the supercomputers to calculate multiple trajectories, or the casualties in different scenarios, but he did not, because he didn't want “them” to have to kill him. Also, it was entirely possible that Boltzmann was a nut case. So he took a different tack.

“What about your garage?” he asked. “Can you tell me what’s out there?”

“That is different,” Boltzmann said.

“Is it related to your work?”

“Yes and no.”

“Can you just tell me about the ‘no?’”

Boltzmann thought for a long time, then said, “Perhaps. But I will need time to think about that,” and you should be getting home. Your wife will be returning soon.”

Viktor looked at his watch and saw that it was 11:30, and Marlena’s shift ended at 11:00.

“OK,” he said, “but thank you for sharing all this with me.”

“And thank you for listening,” Boltzmann said, in a more sincere tone than Viktor remembered hearing before – in the tone of a man who has been carrying too many secrets, for too long, with absolutely no one to talk to. Which was, Viktor thought as he walked home, not that different from his own situation.

46)                Half a Million Buckeroos

On the last day of May, Viktor went to the “Get Your Act Together” show, which was held in the cafeteria, just like in his daydream. They used an “Applausometer” (which was, SVP Drysdale joked, “patent pending”) to rank the acts, meaning that as the audience applauded, two on-stage employees – a Level IV older black man and a Level II younger white woman who looked like she might be Hispanic – would angle an eight-foot needle to read anything from zero to a hundred.

The only act that Viktor liked at all was a fellow who did imitations of famous people, because (this being a year or so before the “potato” incident) he had Dan Quayle repeat his comment from the VP Debate on 1988: “I have far more experience than many others that sought the office of vice president of this country. I have as much experience in the Congress as [Jack Kennedy](http://en.wikiquote.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy "John F. Kennedy") did when he sought the presidency. I will be prepared to deal with the people in the Bush administration, if that unfortunate event would ever occur.” 

People laughed, but he only got a 7, because this was a Republican crowd.

The winning act was a threesome who did a pretty good imitation of the Kingston Trio singing “This Land is My Land,” which culminated with their seven children carrying American flags onto the stage. But they left out the last verse, about how “some are wonderin'

if this land's still made for you and me.”

Viktor left as the trophy was being presented and the audience was applauding. He felt ashamed, as if they were all booing him for not performing.

#          #          #

As Viktor drove home that night he noticed that a black sedan seemed to be following him. A mile or so later it was gone, but there was a navy blue sedan in more or less the same spot.

“Uh oh,” Viktor muttered, “The Death Star’s guns have stopped firing. Stabilize your rear deflectors. Watch for enemy fighters. They're coming in!  Three marks at 2-10!  (EXPLOSION). It's no good, I can't maneuver!   Stay on target.  We're too close!  Stay on target!  Loosen up!   (EXPLOSION). Gold Five to Red leader, lost Tiree, lost Dutch. (EXPLOSION).”

Miraculously, the navy blue sedan was gone. But a few minutes later a maroon sedan tailed him right to the entrance to Avalon Acres, but didn't follow him in.

Viktor went into his study and pulled down one of his father’s spy thrillers and, with his photographic memory, was able to find the exact passage where the author talked about how government agents would tail people with “relays” of cars.

Did this mean that “they” knew – or suspected – that Boltzmann was telling him things?

If so, it sounded like that could be dangerous.

That thought scared Viktor. But it was a delicious fright.

#          #          #

On Sunday, after watching video of the Shuttle carry the [Spacelab](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spacelab "Spacelab") into orbit, Viktor decided that he would not stay beyond his 20 year anniversary on September 1st – not one single day. So, on Monday morning he called HR to schedule a meeting. Three days later he met with a young woman in the Tower, and the first thing she told him was that he was mistaken – his 20 year vesting date was December 1st, not September 1st.

Viktor said she was the one who was mistaken and told her that he had started on June 1, 1970, as a temporary employee, and gone permanent as of September 1, 1970.

That was true, she replied, but there was a three month probationary period before full-time employment became official, and that had ended on December 1, 1970.

When Viktor protested that no one had told him about that rule, she said that it might not have existed back in 1971, but it was clearly stated in the Policies and Procedures Manual, which Viktor called the PP.

Viktor was so upset that he steamed out of the woman’s office and marched straight out of the building, not even bothering to tell people he’d come down with H1N1 or anything like that.

#          #          #

On the drive home Viktor mentally calculated what it would cost him if he were to leave immediately instead of waiting six months, and even he – an actuary – was astonished at the result. Leaving early would mean that he would retire at 72.5% of his salary rather than 80%. That 7.5% was equal to about $6,600 per year. If he lived another 40 years, that would be $264,000. Add in the loss of 6 month’s pay, and the total climbed to almost $300,000. If you assumed that his investments could grow a squinch faster than inflation – not that they had been doing so lately – the total impact on his net worth by the time he croaked might be half a million buckeroos.

Half a million dollars for 6 months was $2,777.77 per day, including Saturdays, Sundays and holidays. For that much money he easily could afford a $200 a week fine from the Association, so the minute as he got home he put on some old pants and his SHAKE IT UP t-shirt and got back to work in his garden. He was kind of hoping that Richard Troy would show up to protest, but of course he, and all other able-bodied men in Avalon Acres, and an increasing number of the women as well, were at work.

47)                What Happened in Cincinnati Stayed in Cincinnati

The next time Viktor and Boltzmann played pool they had a loud fight. It started when Viktor asked Boltzmann if he would be more specific about what he did at The General. Boltzmann shot back that Viktor was asking too many questions, and he wasn't comfortable talking to him about his work since they were competitors. Viktor replied that he thought Boltzmann was kind of egotistical and he wasn't comfortable talking to him, either. Then Viktor marched off in a huff, and Boltzmann slammed the front door behind him.

As Viktor steamed home he thought to himself that they’d given a pretty good performance. For the man in the mirror.

#          #          #

Viktor and Boltzmann now had a new signal. If Boltzmann parked his car on the left-hand edge of his driveway, that meant he was available for a meeting in the tinfoil shed that evening. And if Viktor turned off the lamppost at the end of his driveway at or about 9 PM – he varied it by a few minutes so “they" wouldn’t catch on – that meant that Boltzmann’s invitation was accepted.

Because of one thing and another it was June 10th before the planets lined up. Viktor was sure of the date because, while he was waiting for 10:00 to roll around he watched TV and saw there had been a huge parade in New York City – they called it the “mother of all parades” –  for the Desert Storm Troops.

At 9:00 he killed his lamppost light. That meant he still had an hour to wait. He wished they could meet sooner, but he understood the reason. Boltzmann would go to bed at 9:30, as usual, then climb out his bedroom window, onto his back deck, and out into the yard.

Viktor went to his office to recheck his retirement calculations. They were in the fake leather attaché case he’d received for attending the boondoggle in Cincinnati, with his initials – VAH – on the flap. The attaché case reminded him of Paula Bancroft, and the instant he thought of her he realized that it was a Monday night, so her husband would be at his poker game. That seemed so lucky that it couldn’t be just a coincidence. Acting quickly, so he couldn’t change his mind, in much the same way that he had on the night in Cincinnati when he called her room at almost midnight, he dialed her number.

As soon as he heard her voice he felt better. And she sounded glad to hear from him.

He told her about his retirement dilemma, and said he was tempted to just leave right now.

She replied that it sounded like one of those tough choices, where logic told you one thing, but your heart said otherwise.

The word “heart” reminded him that she’d said something very similar, as they sat on the couch in her room, looking at the Cincinnati skyline, their hands almost touching.

“You know me,” he said. “What do you think I should do?”

There was a long pause, and then she said that she thought he should work the six months but use the time to explore his options, and maybe see a therapist, which didn't bother him the way it did when Marlena said it.

He told her that he’d re-started his garden in the front lawn, and he thought that was good therapy, and he’d have radishes to eat in another week or two and he was looking forward to that. Radishes were the first thing he had grown in his boyhood garden, and just the idea of them made him feel young again.

“You're still young,” she said.

He wanted to tell her about Boltzmann, but he stopped himself because it occurred to him that his phone might be tapped. It did not occur to him, though that this phone call would show up on their long distance bill, where Marlena might see it. Either that or, as Dr. French would later suggest, it did occur to him but he wanted Marlena to see it.

They talked for so long that it was 9:55 before Viktor knew it. He said that he had to go, in a way that made it sound like Marlena had just showed up. Paula said she understood, and she hoped he would call again and let her know what he’d decided.

After they said goodbye he held the phone by his ear, to savor the moment and not be the first one to hang up. The line went quiet when she hung up. And then, three or four seconds later, he heard a click.

A shot of adrenaline ran through him, as he thought it might be her husband. Then he calmed himself with realization that it was probably one of her kids. Because Marlena wasn’t home, Viktor did not even consider that the click might have been at his end.

48)                The Lives One Many Versus the Life of One Latvian Defector

Viktor slipped on a black sweatshirt and a black cap – lest his semi-bald pate reflect any moonlight – and sneaked out his back door at 10:00. When he got to the tinfoil shed he sat in the same chair as before.

Boltzmann arrived about five minutes later, sat in the other seat, and commented right away – with a toothy grin – that he’d noticed Viktor working on his garden again.

Viktor smiled back, told him about the “revised” date for his vesting, and how he had calculated that it would cost him $2,777.77 per day to leave now. So he was going to stick it out at work, but he was “invest” $200 of it in his garden.

He thought Boltzmann would be pleased to hear he was pushing ahead with the garden, but Boltzmann’s grin froze, and then began to slowly melt.

“What is it?” Viktor asked, though he was pretty sure that he knew.

“May I ask you a question?” Boltzmann said.

“Go right ahead.”

“Suppose you had gone to the doctor this afternoon, and learned that you had only six months to live. Would you continue to work at the National Financial Group for your last six months?”

Viktor wondered for a second if Boltzmann was actually talking about himself, because he had seemed ill lately – not just colder, and paler, but somehow less substantial, like you could almost see through him from certain angles.

Viktor replied that if he became seriously ill they would give him sick leave, but then conceded that he knew what Boltzmann meant, and the answer was that he would probably quit work right away.

“Then perhaps you should quit now,” Boltzmann said.

“But I’d be giving up a ton of money.”

“A ton of money that you will not really need, since you told me that you have been very frugal for your whole life, and have no children.”

Viktor conceded that that was also true. But then said, “But let’s say I did quit. Tomorrow. What would I do with those six months?”

“Live them,” Boltzmann said. “You don’t know how lucky you are that you can.”

When Viktor asked Boltzmann to explain what he meant by that, Boltzmann told him his story, or what he said was his story, and the person speaking was a different Boltzmann than Viktor had seen before – it was a person without the egotism of the Brilliant Boltzmann, the gawkiness of the Adolescent Boltzmann, or the defensiveness of the Wary Boltzmann. It was a very soft and touchable Boltzmann, who began by confessing that he had been dying to tell this story to someone for many years now.

#          #          #

Born in Latvia, in 1957, the young Boltzmann was quickly spotted as a child prodigy. In the same way that promising athletes were pushed into training programs for the Olympics, he was taken from his family and pushed into programs for mathematics and, later, into military research for the Soviet aerospace program.

“I have had no personal life since I was nine,” he said.

He came to a symposium at MIT in 1985, shortly after Ronald Reagan was sworn in for his second term. His “masters” wanted him to bring back whatever information he could glean about America’s efforts. Ronald Reagan had proposed Star Wars in 1983, and had recently used had just used the phrase "evil empire.”  Also, this was right when the new LGM-118A’s “Peacekeepers,” – MIRV’ed missiles, he explained – were being deployed.

The CIA invited Boltzmann to defect and work for them. They said they would get his parents out of Latvia, one way or another, and get his father the urgent medical attention he needed. When Boltzmann accepted, they circulated a story that he had been drugged and kidnapped, as Vitaly Yurchenko claimed to have been.

“What do you mean ‘claimed?’” Viktor asked.

“Yurchenko was a fake defector who was later awarded the Order of the Red Star,” Boltzmann said.

The CIA gave Boltzmann a new identity and installed him in New Mexico with an imaginary family.

Viktor asked him if he had gotten the name of one of his sons wrong on purpose when he was talking to him – to signal that something was rotten in Denmark.

Boltzmann said no, he was not that clever. It was just that he wasn't very good with names, which struck Viktor as funny.

When Viktor asked him if he’d worked at Sandia Labs he shook his head again and said no, there was another facility, buried deep in the mountains of the same name – but that was common knowledge, so he wasn't giving away any big secrets by telling Viktor that. But he did have to be careful about what he told him. “For your own good,” he said.

“Does your work have to do with Star Wars, so-called?” Viktor asked.

“Only indirectly,” Boltzmann replied. “My job is more about the chess game – or Go might be a better example – that we have been playing with the Soviets.”

“And we’ve been kicking their butts, right? Viktor asked.

Now Boltzmann’s magnified eyes seemed to look in two directions at once, and his mouth twisted into a smile that was also a grimace. “Yes, and no,” he said.

The good news was that by making more progress than the Soviets were, and leading them to believe that we were making even more progress than we were, we had pushed the them to the brink financially, and that was among the reasons why the Union had collapsed. But the bad news was that the situation was in some ways worse now, because the central command and control structure was gone, and had been replaced by a bunch of rogue generals. So the odds of a full-scale war had gone way down, but there was actually a higher possibility, that a drunken general would throw a handful of missiles at us, and one or two might get through.

The scenarios, he said, were shocking, and even his pale and pimpled face seemed to blanch as he said it. He went to work each day and played war games in which millions of people died. Millions were saved, but millions died, and he was finding it increasingly difficult to sleep at night. He had wanted to quit his job for more than a year now, but he couldn’t leave.

“Because of your parents?” Viktor asked.

Boltzmann shook his head slowly. No, his father had died, and his mother now said that she would not leave his grave behind. Also, with the Singing Revolution, Latvia would soon be free, in any case.

“Then why can’t you leave?” Viktor asked.

Boltzmann smiled a long, patient smile, during which Viktor realized what the reason was, and asked him if it was that he knew too much.

Boltzmann nodded gravely.

“But would they…”

“Yes,” Boltzmann yes, “they very well might, using the justification that protecting the lives of hundreds of millions of Americans is more important than protecting the life of one Latvian defector.”

That reminded Viktor of Spock, and “the lives of the many.”

There was a long silence. And then Boltzmann spoke again. “There is one other factor,” he said, ‘and it is one that reminds me of you.”

“Yes?”

“My possible death is not the only thing holding me back. I am like an inmate in a mental asylum. This is the only world I have known for the last six years, and it is a very structured and safe world where everything makes sense. My freedoms are limited but, like a dog in a crate, there is security in those limitations. When you said that you do not know what you would do if you left your job, I was reminded that I feel the same way. The difference is that you can leave.”

“And you think I should?  Leave right now?”

“I think you need to do something!” he said. “And soon!”

Because Viktor didn't know what to say to that, he made a joke, which was that both their dilemmas might be resolved if one of those rogue generals tossed a missile at Hartford.

“Is Hartford on their list of targets?” he asked, doubting very much that it was.

“That information is classified,” Boltzmann replied, “but you would do well to wonder why The General’s computers are thirteen stories below ground.”

“I thought it was eleven stories,” Viktor said.

“You are probably correct,” Boltzmann conceded, with what looked like a wink.

49)                “How Friendly?”

Viktor did some research and found that the things Boltzmann had said about Vitaly Yurchenko, Reagan’s Evil Empire speech, and the LGM-118A’s were all real. So his story might be true. On the other hand, Norbert H. Boltzmann was a smart fellow, and if he was going to fabricate a fantasy – either because it was one he believed, as a paranoid schizophrenic, or because it was the cover story of someone in a Witness Protection Program, or he was a wacko terrorist, or an alien – then it would have also sounded true.

So Viktor next asked himself the question that pragmatist William James would have asked, which was, did it really matter if Boltzmann was talking about a reality, or a delusion?  He was trapped either way – by reality, or by his imagination. And Viktor was kind of trapped, too. So maybe it was more important that Viktor come to his own decision, rather than try to figure out whether Boltzmann’s story was true or not.

Viktor thought about this a lot in the next week but couldn’t decide what to do. He called his mother, and she quickly shifted the conversation to some problems she was having with her eyes. He called his father, and Viktor Sr. said that if Viktor had pursued career in aerospace, as he had advised, he wouldn’t have this problem. He called McNutty. McNutty said the job market was impossible, so Viktor should hang in there. He didn't call Paula because it wasn't Monday yet.
