Pretty much the only person Viktor didn't talk to about his dilemma was Marlena. But at that very moment, as luck would have it, Marlena had something to ask him.

#          #          #

Viktor’s radishes were almost ready to eat. His cucumbers had spread and made cool tents where the fruit would soon appear. His three tomato plants looked like they were about to flower. And a row of corn in the back was halfway up to his knee.

Viktor was reveling in this when Marlena was suddenly standing in front of him, clutching something in her fist. She looked tremendously upset. Viktor was afraid that she’d been looking for something in his filing cabinet and had found his collection of Cat Books. But when he saw the Bell System logo on the envelope he knew it was even worse than that.

In a voice so tight it sounded masculine, Marlena asked Viktor if he would please come inside, because she had something she wanted to discuss with him in private.

He took his time about washing up, hoping the hospital would call with an emergency, but no such luck. Finally he took a seat in the living room, where Marlena was waiting, like a parent. Looking down at the phone bill, she explained in a voice that was as cold and level as pond ice that she’d been going through the phone bill, as Viktor had asked her to do, to identify the calls she made for her charity work. In doing so she’d come upon a 35 minute call to Minneapolis on the evening of May 20th – a night when she was at work. She thought it might be a mistake, so she called the phone company, and they verified it. In fact, they said, another long call had been made to the same number on June 4th – another night when she’d been at work.

Now she paused and looked up from the bill and at Viktor. Viktor returned her look, trying not to appear in any way guilty.

She said that she had still thought it might be a mistake, so she called the number in Minneapolis, and spoke to a woman named Paula Bancroft.

“Do you know her?” she asked Viktor.

Viktor pretended to reflect for a moment, then nodded and said yes, he did know her, that he had met her at the “boondoggle” (weak chuckle) in Cincinnati. She had a job similar to his at a big firm out there. They were colleagues.

Something big had changed. For as long as Viktor could remember, Marlena had not pressed him about anything. She might say things like, “It would be nice if you would tell me more about what you are thinking,” or “it seems like you had a lot of wine last night,” but all Viktor had to do was ignore her suggestions and nothing would happen. Now, suddenly, she was standing her ground.

“Are you saying that you called this woman about a business question at 9:30 at night?” Marlena asked, looking incredulous.

“It was only 8:30 out there, but, yes. And I also wanted to see how she was doing. We had become friendly at the convention, and so I wanted to see how things were going with her.”

“You had become ‘friendly’ with her?”

“Yes,” Viktor nodded.

“How friendly?”

Luckily for Viktor, the grandfather clock in the living room stopped ticking at exactly that moment. While time was frozen, Viktor was able to walk around behind Marlena, look at himself over her huge shoulders, and test out a couple scenarios. In one he denied that he and Paula were friendly in “that way,” but he could see that he did not look credible, and was blushing. In Scenario II he joked that it had been hard to tell “how friendly” they were because the room had been so dark. That definitely didn't work. In Scenario III he confessed that there had been a little “interest” between them, but Paula was married, too, and “nothing “big” had happened. That looked like it might fly, so he went back to his seat and tried it.

It didn't work very well.

“What do you mean by “nothing big?” Marlena cried.”

“I mean that there might have been a little ... “

Before he could finish the sentence she told him to stop, that she didn't want to hear it. Not now. Not at this moment when she’d had a really bad day at the hospital, her mother detested her, and people were dying of hunger in Somalia.

Marlena stood and looked away, at some distant scene – perhaps one of those three. Whatever was happening caused her to breathe so heavily that Viktor thought she might be the one to have a cardiac event.

Finally she turned back to him said, “Viktor, we need to see a couples counselor.” But he didn't think she had just come to that conclusion. He thought she’d probably had it in mind right from the start.

Marlena had brought counseling up obliquely a few times before, saying that she thought many couples benefitted from counseling, or that counseling was something one could do to head off problems, not waiting until there were problems, and so on, but this was the first time she’d come right out and suggested that they see a counselor.

“It isn't just this incident,” she said. “You’ve been very distant lately. I think you're going through a Midlife Crisis, Viktor, and it's possible that I'm going through one as well,” she added, in a comment that he did not give the weight it deserved.

“Well, let’s give it some thought,” he said, meaning let’s hope that you get busy with one of your save-the-world projects and forget all about it.

“I’ll ask my friends for the names of some people,” she said. “And you might ask your men friends if they know any good couples counselors.”

“None of my men friends have ever seen a counselor. They all committed suicide the day before the appointment,” Viktor said with a laugh, but Marlena had already gotten up to go.

Viktor sat there for a moment thinking that between the $200 a day fine from the Association and a bunch of counseling sessions, he was quickly burning through the money he was saving by not quitting before December 1st.

50)                Boltzmann’s Friend’s Schrodinger Box

Assuming that what Boltzmann had said about his work was true, Viktor felt ridiculous. While his neighbor was helping to prevent a worldwide nuclear war, or make one more winnable, all Viktor had managed to do so far was protect his pension, grow some radishes, and have an impossible crush on a woman who would probably never leave her three kids. The next time he met his crew-cut neighbor – in the tinfoil shed – he said as much, that he felt like he wasn't doing much with his life. And the things he was doing didn't amount to much. And – Boltzmann was right – time was ticking.

“I’m tempted,” Viktor said with a boyish grin, “to use my famous coin toss method.”

When Boltzmann objected that one shouldn’t just toss a coin, Viktor explained that his coin toss method was different. For example, if he was trying to make a really important decision, like whether to wash his car on Saturday afternoon, or wait until Sunday, he would tell himself, “Heads I wash the car today, tails I wash it tomorrow.”  But – and here was the difference – if the coin came up heads and he was disappointed by the idea of washing his car today, he’d wait till tomorrow. He didn't use the coin toss to make the decision – he used it to find out how he felt about something.

“However, there are two problems,” Viktor sighed. “One is that I’ve used this method too much in the past that I know the trick.

“The other problem is that I don’t know what the question is. Is it heads that I leave work now, or tails that I wait six months?  Is it commit myself 100% to project Zorro on my own time?  Is it that I fly out and see Paula?  Or go back to grad school and figure out what makes the light from RR Lyrae stars even more variable than my feelings?”

Boltzmann looked at Viktor and said, “You are stuck, Viktor. You need to reboot. That is maybe what you were trying to do when you went skydiving, but it was not enough to jump out of a plane, because you knew that the odds of your dying were miniscule.”

Viktor nodded. “Yes, I even told Marlena that the drive out there was statistically more life-threatening than the jump.”

“Suppose that there had been a 50% chance that you would die?”

“I wouldn’t have jumped. I mean unless we were supposed to see the couples counselor the next morning (chuckle).”

Suppose you had jumped anyway, even knowing that there was a 50% chance of death. How would you have felt when you touched the ground?”

“Relieved, I guess. No, more than that. Maybe (look of enlightenment) reborn.”

“Exactly.”

Viktor wasn't sure where his new neighbor was going with this. Was he going to suggest that he play Russian Roulette to reboot himself?

Boltzmann reflected for a long time, like a chess player looking ten moves ahead, then said something that Viktor never would have predicted.

“There is a famous thought experiment called Schrödinger’s Cat?  Did you perhaps read about it in your studies?”

“I think I know what you're talking about,” Viktor said. “It’s the one where he puts a cat in a box with some poison and a 50-50 switch. Half the time that poison will be released, half the time it won’t. He throws the switch, and now the question is … is the cat dead or alive?”

“Yes. And what’s the answer to the question?”

“Well, I hate cats, so I hope the answer is yes. But I think the real answer – or at least what you're supposed to say if you want to pass the test – is that the cat is both dead and alive.”

Boltzmann said he had it basically right, that the cat was “propagating in isolation” until you “took a measurement.”

Viktor said he liked that phrase better than having a Midlife Crisis. But where was this going?

After another long pause Boltzmann said that he’d had a friend, when he was at University, in Riga. They were studying Quantum Physics and his friend brought a Schrödinger box into class and said he was going to do the experiment. The professor thought it was a joke, so his friend told everyone that there was a cat inside and then flipped the switch.

When the cat came out alive everyone was relieved. The professor still thought it had been a joke, but it had been real. Boltzmann’s friend continued to do the experiments, and a couple students began to bring him subjects. Eventually the administration raided his room and found two dead cats in a plastic bag on the fire escape. Boltzmann’s friend argued that the university’s psychology department killed more than a hundred guinea pigs every a year after they’d been used in a course in Behaviorism, but they said that was different, and he was expelled.

“And then?” Viktor asked.

“He continued to do his experiments. Even after he launched a successful career he continued to do his Schrödinger experiments."

“You mean he still does them?”

“Yes.”

“Wow!  How long has he been at it?”

“More than twenty years.”

“Do you have any idea how many has cats your friend has … actualized?”

“1,842.”

“Your friend has killed 1,842 cats!”

Boltzmann nodded solemnly.

Viktor thought for a moment. “What if a cat survives the experiment?”

“He puts it in the same place as the ones that died.”

“He buries them?”

“Something like that.”

“But why?”

“Because it wasn't a trial, Viktor. It was an experiment. And he doesn’t want to take the chance that he will come upon the same cat again and contaminate his data. Or have suspicious people find his subjects.”

“So there’s no escape for the cats.”

“Or for us, Viktor. There’s no escape for us, either.”

“So why does he do it?”

Boltzmann said that his friend’s stated reason was to generate a random number. When a subject lived, he assigned it a number one. If it died, he marked it as a zero. At this point he had a binary number 1,842 digits long, and he was using that number – in whole and in part – in his work, rather than consulting an official book of random numbers.

Partly to lighten the conversation, Viktor noted that he had always found it kind of absurd that there was an official, published list of random numbers. It was like a phone book full of unlisted numbers.

Boltzmann didn't comment on that, either. Instead he said that, while generating random numbers was his friend’s practical reason, he thought there was more to it than that.

“Yes?”

“Some people,” Boltzmann said, “come home from work and drink martinis to ease their mind. Some take drugs. Others, I have heard, pick at their flesh with a razor. Many watch sports. These Schrödinger experiments are what my friend does.”  He said this with such sincerity that it felt like it made sense.

As Viktor walked home that night he felt certain of two things, but unsure about a third. The two things he felt certain about were (1) that Norbert H. Boltzmann was the “friend” to whom he was referring, and (2) his Schrödinger box was in the garage. The thing he felt uncertain about was whether Boltzmann was really suggesting that he – Viktor – give it a try to resolve his own emotional dilemma.

51)                Viktor and Marlena See Dr. French

Marlena got the name of a counselor from a friend at work, and she and Viktor went on a Monday after work. Dr. French’s office was in a brownstone in the South End. With exposed woodwork and decorative moldings, it felt more like a home than an office, and Viktor liked that, though he wasn't at all excited about seeing a therapist.

Having brilliantly calculated that a hundred dollars an hour for fifty minutes was two dollars an hour, Viktor was already irked when the door to the office didn't open till ten dollars past 6:00. His annoyance doubled when he realized that the therapist had not opened the door for herself, but had used some kind of automatic door opening device. And his annoyance tripled when he saw her, because she was almost as overweight as Marlena, sitting back in a comfy looking chair with her shoes off and her stockinged feet up on an ottoman – kind of like Jabba the Hut, Viktor thought.

“I apologize for not letting you in myself,” she said, in a raspy voice, and when Viktor saw it – an oxygen tank by her side – he felt bad.

Dr. French explained that she was not a medical doctor, nor did she hold a doctorate – in fact she was self-taught.

Viktor liked that.

He and Marlena sat in hard-backed chairs, at about a 45 degree angle. Dr. French. looked at each of them in turn, and then asked Viktor to please describe, in just a sentence or two, why he was here.

“I’m here,” he said, “because Marlena thinks I'm having a Midlife Crisis.”

He thought Dr. French might tell him he was ducking the question by answering her question about his thought with Marlena’s, but she just turned and asked Marlena the same question.

“I'm here because of Viktor’s infidelity,” Marlena said.

Viktor wanted to raise his hand and protest that infidelity had not been established, but Dr. French was already saying that, in her experience, infidelity was more often the symptom of a broken relationship, rather than the cause of one, and then asked Marlena to talk about their relationship.

Marlena laughed too hard, and launched into a ten minute description of the many ways that Viktor had been “unavailable” to her in the last few years.  She said he was always just “off in his little world.”  She had no idea what he was feeling, and didn't think he gave a “damn” about what she was feeling. And she didn't “buy” this idea that infidelity was just a symptom. “We took vows,” she said. “We made promises. And he broke them.”

Before Viktor could raise his hand,  Dr. French said that before they went any farther she wanted to better define “infidelity.”  Many people, she said, defined it as certain physical acts. In her view, the acts were less important than the feelings.

“I hope you're not going to tell him that a drunken blow job is not infidelity,” Marlena snarled.

Dr. French replied that she thought that yes, a drunken blow job certainly was a type of infidelity, but it was also possible to be unfaithful without having committed any sexual acts.

Like Jimmy Carter committing adultery in his heart, Viktor thought.

“Suppose,” Dr. French said, “that there was a woman with whom Viktor felt he could talk about the things that he does not talk to you about, and he did. Suppose he had lunch with her several times a week, or they went out for drinks, and he opened his heart to her. Even if they never crossed a certain line, would that bother you more or less than a ‘drunken blow job.’”

Marlena didn't give an inch. She said that both acts were “reprehensible.”

Now Dr. French asked Viktor if he would please describe what had happened.

Viktor said that he’d met a woman at a business conference in Cincinnati. He’d recently phoned her a couple times, and Marlena had seen the calls on the phone bill, and had asked him if anything had happened.

“And, did anything happen?” Dr. French asked.

Viktor saw Marlena turn to look at him, though he himself was still looking at Dr. French.

“Well,” he said, “I guess that depends on what you mean by ‘anything.’  We went out to dinner – a whole group of us. When we got back to the hotel everyone else went to the bar, but Paula and I went to our rooms. She was on the 9th floor, and I was on 11, and when she got out of the elevator she looked back at me in a way that I took to mean that she might want me to follow her. I didn't. I was tempted, but I didn't. I went up to my room and had a couple drinks from my mini-bar.”

Viktor paused for a moment.

“Then, about an hour later – I guess it was, close to midnight – I called her and asked her if she wanted to talk. She said she did. I said we could meet in the lobby, but she said no, I could come to her room if I wanted to. So I went.”

Marlena shot up like Vesuvius. “You went to her hotel room!”

“Yes.”

“This hussy called you at midnight, and invited you to her room, and you went!”

“I called her, and she’s not a hussy.”

\`                    “Well, I sure hope you used a condom,” Marlena snarled, and then thundered out, slamming the door so hard that Dr. French’s oxygen tank tipped over.

#          #          #

There was a long silence after Marlena left. Finally Dr. French said, “We still have twenty minutes left. Would you like to tell me how you feel about what just happened?”

“I'm not sure which one of me should go first,” he said. He meant it as a little joke, but she didn't bat an eye.

“Well, I think I have two kind of opposite feelings,” Viktor said. “On the one hand I’m thinking that if I told Marlena that nothing happened, it might make a difference.”

“Are you saying that nothing happened?”

“Yes. I mean nothing happened in the physical sense of the word. We held hands at one point, but that was it. But you’re right, a lot happened in the other sense. We talked for a long time – till two in the morning. I told Paula things that I had never told Marlena, kind of like you just said, and Paula told me things about herself.”

“If she had offered to sleep with you that night, would you have gone to bed with her?”

“Maybe. But I think it would have had to happen right away. Once we started talking, it was too late. But even right away, I don’t know,” Viktor said. Then, after reflecting a moment, he added, “But, you know, if I didn't sleep with her, I think it would have been more out of the idea of loyalty. I’d been faithful to Marlena for our whole marriage – for twenty plus years. But it wasn't so much Marlena I was being faithful to her, as faithful to the idea of fidelity – you know, that you dance with the one who brought you. So maybe that doesn’t count. I mean, if infidelity isn't even on the table, does fidelity mean anything?”

Dr. French said that was for him to answer, not her, then asked, “And what is the other feeling, Viktor?”

“The other feeling is that I'm not sure that I want to fix our relationship. Maybe that’s why I didn't start right off by saying that nothing happened. Can I tell you a story?”

When Dr. French nodded, Viktor told her the story about the rainy night when he thought Marlena might have been killed in a car accident, and how part of him had hoped that she had died.

Dr. French just closed her eyes and felt his words. In that long silent moment Viktor thought to himself he thought that if he flipped his one koruna coin and it came up “heads, save the marriage,” he would feel disappointed.

52)                A Box Big Enough for Two?

Instead of going home that evening, Marlena went to her mother’s house, and the next day she phoned Viktor at work to say that she had the day off and was going to use it to move some of her things to Manchester. She said she needed some time, and space, and would see him at their next appointment with Dr. French, next Monday.

Part of Viktor wanted to tell her now that nothing had happened in Cincinnati, but he didn't. He told himself that it was something they should talk about with Dr. French, but his real reason was that he wanted a separation. So he just said yes, he agreed that a little time alone would be good for both of them.

When he got home from work he took a Power Walk in the woods and used the half hour to think about Boltzmann and the Schrödinger Box. The number of experiments – 1,842 – seemed unbelievable. But over 20 years that worked out to less than 2 per week, even allowing a few weeks a year for sick days, vacations, holidays, and so on.

Viktor wondered how Boltzmann would obtain so many “subjects,” but that wouldn’t have been difficult, either. There were innumerable flyer papers that often offered free kitties every week, plus signs on bulletin boards at Gil’s and other places.

Another person might have been disturbed by the idea that someone had actualized 1,842 cats in the last twenty years, but Viktor wasn't. He found it exciting. Not because he didn't like cats, but because it made Norbert H. Boltzmann sound like a rebellious hero, a man who was not afraid to buck the conventions and petty morality of society.

Right then Viktor froze in his tracks. Standing on the trail – not fifty feet ahead of him was the same eight point buck he’d encountered when his father took him hunting in the fall of 1966. The deer recognized Viktor, too – he could tell by the way it turned its fine head and looked him right between the eyes.

Viktor had raised his father’s 30-30 and locked the iron sights on the spot in the animal’s chest that his father had taught him to aim for – the heart. The deer did not move. Rather than run, it challenged him with its eyes.

“Squeeze,” his father reminded him, squeeze so slowly that you're surprised when the gun goes off. But Viktor jerked the trigger. The gun sounded the crack of a huge branch. He missed, and the deer was gone in two bounds.

He told his father that he’d just been nervous, but his old man didn't believe him, and never took him hunting again.

Now, with no rifle, Viktor clapped, and the buck bounded into the woods.

#          #          #

The next time Viktor saw Boltzmann he told him about his separation from Marlena, and said that now they were both separated, even though Boltzmann’s wife was imaginary.

Boltzmann didn't really get the humor. He just asked Viktor if he had given any thought to what he was going to do next with his life.

Viktor said that he thought he would definitely have to work till December 1st seeing as how, if he and Marlena got divorced, he would have half as much money. “They say two can live as cheaply as one,” he quipped, “but only for half as long.”

Boltzmann smiled thinly.

“The good news,” Viktor continued, is that with Marlena living in Manchester I'm free to come see you at any time. Maybe we should start playing pool again.”

“I don’t think that would be allowed,” Boltzmann said. “You were asking too many questions.”

“Speaking of which,” Viktor said, “can you tell me what’s in your garage?”

“What do you think is in my garage, Viktor?”

“Oh, I’ve had a few theories. One is that you’re an alien and you have a space ship or a portal out there. Another is that you’re a terrorist and you’re building some kind of Doomsday machine out there, like the Deathmobile in the movie ‘Animal House.’”

“The what?”

“Never mind. Another theory is that it's a teleportation device like in ‘The Fly.’  Did you ever see that movie, ‘The Fly.’”

Of course Boltzmann hadn't seen that movie, either. In fact it was possible that he had never seen any movies, or maybe just “Dr. Zhivago.”

“And my fourth theory is that there’s a Schrödinger Box out there, and you and your friend are one and the same person.”

Boltzmann went quiet, and then sighed and just admitted it, with what looked like relief. “Yes, there is a box out there, Viktor, and yes, I am the ‘friend’ of whom I was speaking.”

Viktor said he wanted to see it, but Boltzmann refused on the grounds that there were other things in the garage that were classified.

“You mean if I saw those things, your handlers would have to kill me?”

Boltzmann shot him an intense look. “This is not a joke, Viktor.”

#          #          #

There was a long silence between the two men. It felt to Viktor like they’d reached some kind of turning point. He appeared to have solved the puzzle of what Boltzmann was up to, though it could have also been that one of his other theories was actually correct and Boltzmann was using the Schrödinger Box idea to hide the fact that he was actually a terrorist, an alien, or a mad scientist.

So now the question going through Viktor’s mind was, what next?  And then, as if he had read his mind, Boltzmann suggested an answer.

“However,” he said, “you could build your own Schrödinger Box.”

Boltzmann now told him that, while the principles being tested were very advanced, the mechanism itself was fairly simple. There were just three essential parts – a soundproof box, a device capable of releasing a poisonous gas, and a 50-50 switch.

“You’re leaving out one very important part, aren’t you?”

Boltzmann narrowed his eyes in concentration, but he couldn’t think of what this other part was.

“The cat,” Viktor said.

“Oh yes, you will need subjects as well,” Boltzmann conceded, and then said that if Viktor was interested in building a Box he would draw up some plans for him.

“Could I make one that would be big enough to fit both Marlena and I inside?” Viktor asked. “So I can see if our relationship is dead or alive,” even though he already knew the answer to that question.

53)                Yes

When Viktor and Marlena met in Dr. French’s waiting room a week later he told her that he she looked different.

She smiled and said she’d lost 12 pounds.

“Wow!  In just a week?”

She said no, she had been losing weight for a couple weeks.

Her mood seemed brighter, too.

Both of those things annoyed Viktor.

#          #          #

When they sat down in the office Viktor was planning to tell Marlena right away that nothing had happened in Cincinnati, but before he could say a word Marlena announced that she had a confession to make.

Dr. French – who seemed to have been briefed – told her to please go ahead.

Marlena said that, first, she wanted to say that she was sorry that she’d been so angry with Viktor.

Dr. French asked her to please speak directly to him.

She turned, looked straight at him, which was awkward for both of them, and said that she was mad at him for what had happened in Cincinnati, but perhaps this was one of those cases where when you find yourself pointing your finger at someone you should actually – and she demonstrated this with her own finger – turn it around and point it at yourself.

Viktor had no idea what this meant, and that must have been visible because now Marlena had to explain that there was also someone else in her life, too.

Viktor was stunned. He never, ever, in a million years, would have predicted that.

“Is it anyone I know?” he asked.

And Marlena stunned him even more when she said no, it was no one he knew – it was a woman at the hospital. Her name was Patty. She quickly added that nothing had happened between them, at least in the one sense of the word. But in the other sense, quite a bit had happened.

“How long has this been going on?” he asked.

“Since April,” she said.

Since she had suggested that Norbert H. Boltzmann might be gay, Viktor thought.

#          #          #

Now Viktor was the one who got angry. Not that Marlena was seeing someone. But because he’d “wasted the best part of his life on her.”

Dr. French encouraged him to voice his feelings, and he did. He said he’d married her, had given up the career in Astrophysics he had dreamed of, because of the child she was unable to have. He’d stuck with her hoping that she would be able to have a child, even though his father said to move on. He had then stuck with her because he didn't think it would be right to dump her for being infertile. He had hoped to adopt a child or two, but he now realized that she’d been the one who had been more resistant to that idea than he had, putting up one obstacle after another. They had moved apart emotionally after that, but he’d been loyal. And by the way, he had not had sex with Paula. Or anyone else. He had been 100% faithful for their entire marriage. And it had all been for nothing. Twenty years of his life was gone, for nothing!

Viktor was so upset that in this entire speech he didn't crack a single joke.

Marlena was in tears. And so was he. And all this had only taken two sessions!  So much for the joke about how it takes only one psychologist to change a light bulb, but two hundred sessions.

#          #          #

When Viktor got home he was pleased to see that Boltzmann’s car was parked on the left side of the driveway. He wasn't supposed to send his lamppost signal till 9:00, but he tried it anyway, flicking the lamppost on and off a couple times. But the sun didn't set until about 8:30 at that time of year and Boltzmann was probably out in his garage at that moment saying, “Don’t worry, you won’t feel a thing, Fluffer-Nutter.”

He went out to that garden to pick some zucchini, cucumbers, and tomatoes. Then, to kill time, Viktor got out one of his dead cat books. As he flipped through a few funny pictures of what you were supposed to do with your girlfriend’s cat he had the odd thought that maybe he should send a copy to Marlena, because her girlfriend probably had cats. He had no idea why he thought that, he just did

While he had his secret library open he read another chapter from Kitty Kare, the one titled “Moving to An Apartment With a ‘No Pets’ Policy,” which read: 

If you are not allowed to have a kitty in your new apartment or condominium, one option is to take kitty to the pound where it may be adopted by a happy family before the seven day waiting period expires and it is gassed. If you can’t bear to be separated from kitty but still want to respect the rules at your new apartment, consider the following:

Step 1:  Bonk kitty on head with rubber mallet available from Kitty Kare Katalogue.

Step 2:  Pose kitty in favorite position, such as in the process of disemboweling pet parakeet named Birdie.

Step 3:  Spray kitty with 10-15 layers of clear polyurethane allowing appropriate drying time between layers (consult manufacturer’s specifications).

Step 4: Mount finished kitty on wood base with name plate which can be ordered from Kitty Kare Katalogue (add one week for engraving).

#          #          #

By the time 10:00 rolled around Viktor was as drunk as a college freshman, and made his way noisily to the tinfoil shed. Boltzmann said that he had noticed Viktor’s light flicking on and off. He also could see that Viktor was drunk, and asked him what was up.

Viktor told him about the counseling session and Marlena’s announcement.

“It's weird,” he said, “because I think I’ve wanted very much to be released from my relationship with Marlena, but now that it looks like I am I feel … shattered.”

Boltzmann was probably the worst person to tell something like this to because he really was an alien when it came to relationships.

“But I’ve decided,” Viktor said. “Yes. I want to build a Box.”

Boltzmann said he shouldn’t decide while he was drunk. Viktor replied that he’d decided when he was sober. Boltzmann hesitated, but then agreed.

He suggested that Viktor use a small refrigerator like college students often had in their room as the box. Because these units were heavily insulated they were relatively sound proof. Also, you could keep Dr. Pepper in there so it wouldn’t look like what it was.

The heart of the device was the poison gas mechanism. Boltzmann suggested dropping cyanide pellets into hydrochloric, or muriatic acid. Viktor could get muriatic acid at a hardware store. The type used for treating concrete was concentrated enough. “Tell them that's what you want it for,” Boltzmann advised.

“And what about the cyanide?”

“If you are still serious about this when you are sober, I will provide some.”

The most complicated part, he said, was that Viktor would need to build a 50-50 switch and a mechanism to release the pellet half the time.

“I think I can handle that,” Viktor said. “I made some killer science fair projects in my time.”

“Then that's it,” Boltzmann said.

“Except for the subjects,” Viktor replied, and asked Boltzmann where he got his.

Boltzmann’s answer surprised him. He said that he didn't get them. They were provided to him by his employer. Whenever he requested a subject, it would be placed in a gym bag in the trunk of his car while was at work.

“And what about at the other end of the cycle?” Viktor asked.

“I prefer to handle that part myself,” he said. “For completion.”

He didn't say, though, what that involved, or whether the live subjects were treated any differently from the others.

54)                Aside From Being Green the Tablets Gave No Indication of Being Lethal

Viktor called Paula the next Monday evening. First he told her about was how the raccoons had eaten all his ripe corn the day before he was planning to pick it. And then he added, “By the way, Marlena and I are separating.”  He did not mention the role Paula had played because he thought it would upset her.

“My God,” she cried, “there must be something in the stars.”

“What do you mean?” Viktor asked, although he somehow knew exactly what she was about to say, which was that her husband, Rick, had told her just three days ago that he was seeing another woman and was leaving her.

“Does this mean that you and I …” Viktor asked.

“If that's a proposal, I accept,” Paula gushed, and went on to say that the kids would “adore” him.

Wow, thought Viktor. All my problems solved – a new wife and three kids –  just like that!

#          #          #

When he actually called her, about an hour later, before he said a word she said, “Oh my god, Viktor, have you been watching the news?”

He said no, what was up?

She said that a man named [Jeffrey Dahmer](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Dahmer "Jeffrey Dahmer") had been arrested after the remains of 11 men and boys had been found in his [Milwaukee, Wisconsin](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee,_Wisconsin "Milwaukee, Wisconsin"), apartment.

Viktor resisted the urge to ask if her  husband had been among them.

When he told Paula about his separation from Marlena she was silent for a long moment, and then said, “Oh my God, how awful,” or words to that effect. Women seemed to born with that response, he thought.

Viktor said that he was actually feeling some relief. He thought their relationship had died a long time ago, but he would have been too loyal to leave it.

“I know,” she said. “I know the position only too well.”

He understood what that meant. She was still not available. And, being from the Midwest, she probably never would be. Unless her husband were to leave her.

Viktor told her that their separation meant divvying up their assets, which meant he would need to work till December 1st, but that wasn't a big deal – just a bit over four months now, and he could use the time to figure out his next steps. Also, it would take time to work out the divorce, sell the house, and so on.

This time, instead of saying “Oh my god how awful” she said that she thought that sounded like a fresh start might hold a lot of promise for him but …

“But what?” he asked.

She said that she was worried that he would be lonely.

“Not on Monday nights,” he cried.

Rather than chuckling at that she reminded him how that was what they had talked about till two in the morning that night in Cincinnati – how they were both so lonely.

“And it's even worse now that you and I are in separate time zones,” he said, which was a reference to a running joke he had made when they were at the Boondoggle about how he just couldn’t believe that Cincinnati and Hartford were in the same time zone, because Cincinnati seemed so much more exotic.

Before they said goodbye he told her about the click he’d heard on her line the last time. She said that it certainly had not been her husband, but it might have been one of the kids. So Viktor hung on for a full minute after she hung up. He heard nothing. However, if her kids (or anyone else, he later thought, looking back on it) had been listening to the call, they would have known he was waiting for them to hung up and would have waited until he did, no matter how long that took.

#          #          #

During the last days of July and the first days of August, while the wild blackberries were ripening in the woods, and the melons and winter squash were ripening in his garden, and the [United States](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States "United States") and the [Soviet Union](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union") were signing the [START I](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/START_I "START I") treaty, our hero-to-be built his Schrödinger Box.

He didn't take it as a “sign” when someone had put a mini-fridge out on recycling day. He just popped it into his trunk.

“It doesn’t work,” a woman called to him from her front steps.

“That’s OK,” he said, “I know how to fix them,” though for his purposes it was better if it didn't work.

As he set it up in the basement he thought of the chapter from “Kitty Kare” titled “How To Transmit Kitty Through Space.”

Did you know that you can transmit your kitty through space just like they do in Star Trek. This can be useful. For example, if kitty wants to visit a friend’s kitty for the afternoon but your car is in the shop, just follow these simple steps:

Step 1:  Place kitty in microwave.

Step 2:  Telephone friend.

Step 3:  You and friend both set microwaves to Maximum Power Level and 2 minutes time.

Step 4:  You and friend count down in unison from ten to zero.

Step 5:  At “zero,” you and friend press start buttons together.

Step 6:  Observe as your kitty begins to enter hyperspace.

Step 7:  After about one minute (longer for a large kitty, or on high humidity days) kitty should disappear from your microwave and reappear in friend’s microwave.

Step 8:  If kitty does not appear in friend’s microwave press star 69 on phone to retrieve kitty.

#          #          #

The 50-50 switch was tricky. Viktor considered just using the switch from his coffee pot, which only worked about half the time, but wound up building a “Wheel of Fortune” where a circuit was open in 12 of 24 positions of the wheel, and closed in the other 12. He tested it with an Alka-Seltzer tablet and the tablet dropped 98/200 times, giving him an ELR (expected loss rate) of 49 (kitties, not dollars).

“Good enough for Government work,” he muttered, borrowing another line from his old man.

When he told Boltzmann he was ready, his neighbor gave him a plastic bag with 12 cyanide tablets in it. They were smaller than the Alka-Seltzer tablets, but aside from being green gave no indication whatsoever of being lethal.

55)                The “First Wife” Joke

Viktor and Marlena had their third and final meeting with Dr. French on Monday, August 5th.

Dr. French asked them each if they had reached a decision about what they wanted to do next. When they both nodded she asked them if either of them wanted to work to save the marriage. When Marlena shook her head and Viktor shook both of his, she sighed and said that in that case all the remained was to try and wrap things up on an honest note, and maybe learn what had gone wrong in the hope that it would help them lead happier lives in the future. How did that sound?

They both nodded.

Now she asked each of them if they could think of a moment, or an incident, in the twenty years of their marriage that they thought summed up the problem they’d had.

Marlena jumped right in and said it was Viktor’s “first wife” joke.

“You're kidding,” Viktor cried.

#          #          #

The “first wife joke” had happened at a cocktail party the winter before last. Some new people had moved into the neighborhood. Viktor met them at the bar and then took them over and introduced them to Marlena, saying “I’d like you to meet my first wife, Marlena.”

Marlena thought that was a rude thing to say.

Viktor said that said that he had thought it was funny because no one ever dreamed that he would have a second wife. If he had, he never would have cracked the joke. “Everyone in Avalon Acres knew that we were as solid as the Rock of Gibraltar,” he said.

“Not the new people.”

“OK, I guess you have a point there,” he conceded, but insisted that he was just having some harmless fun. And it wasn't like he’d introduced her as his soon-to-be ex-wife or anything like that.

“Your humor,” she said, has grown nastier and nastier over the last few years. You have made more and more jokes about our relationship. And then you cover your jokes with more jokes.”

Viktor asked her what she meant by that.

“You made a joke about me being messy, and when I asked you if you were really upset about it you said no, that if it was something serious you would never joke about it, so if I wanted to know what was really bothering you I should ask myself what you never made jokes about. So I did that, and a couple of the things that came up were my weight and … and our sex life.”

“What sex life?” Viktor blurted.

#          #          #

When Dr. French asked Viktor to tell a story from his side he was tempted to say that it was all the acronyms. What he meant by that was that Marlena was always blaming their difficulties on acronyms. She was ACOA and might have a bit of BPD. He was ADD, and OCD and might have AS (Asperger’s). But the only acronyms he thought were worth talking about were VAH and MMH.

He didn't tell that story, though, because he had already said it, several times, to other people, including McNutty, who liked the ending, and because it wasn't true – he had never really wanted to talk about VAH and MMH.

Instead, he mentioned the child with no thumb. Their first adoption candidate had had been Korean, which wasn't a big deal in his mind, though his father had had a different reaction. The second had suffered from fetal alcohol syndrome, which was a big deal. The third one had had a harelip, which hadn't bothered Viktor very much. The fourth candidate was perfectly healthy in every regard except that she had no thumb on her left hand.

Viktor had thought that was sad, “but not the end of life as we know it,” and pointed out that there was an operation to make one of the fingers work like a thumb, but Marlena would not even consider it. And that was when he’d realized that Marlena did not want to adopt a child at all, and especially not a little girl.

Marlena made a face. “Why especially not a little girl?” she asked.

“Because you were once a little girl,” he said.

Marlena looked stunned, but did not object.

Viktor went on to say that his theory was that Marlena had never received the love and attention she had wanted from her parents – resisting the urge to call it a different kind of Attention Deficit Syndrome – and that, because she hadn't gotten it, she didn't want anyone else to get it.

“That was maybe even part of what attracted her to me,” he said. My father was an egotist, a bit like her mother is. And my mother is out of the picture because of all her ailments, like her father was with his drinking. So I never got much attention when I was a kid, either. Then we formed a relationship in which neither of us gave the other person when they needed either because we couldn’t, or because would have too jealous.”

Viktor had not really thought of all that before. It just came pouring out with a spontaneity that suggested it was either very true or total bullshit.

Probably the former, given how quiet Marlena got.

#          #          #

Dr. French said that Viktor and Marlena had both said a lot, and if was possible that if they continued to talk like this they might be able to save their marriage.

Marlena shook her head adamantly and said, no, she did not want to do that. She had had enough.

Now she looked directly at Viktor and said, “If you want to, you can tell yourself that this is my fault, that I let myself get fat, or whatever. But the reason it didn't work out because you didn't make any effort to make it work. You didn't respect me. You didn't love me. You were just off in your work and your daydreams. But if it makes you feel better, go ahead and tell your pals that your wife became a ... “

For some reason she didn't want to say the word “lesbian.”  Instead she just said, “Well, you’ll probably make a joke about it.”

Viktor was tempted to say no, he wouldn’t tell that to his pals. Because he didn't have any pals. And he didn't really, because Boltzmann was from another planet, and McNutty didn't quite count, and there wasn't really anybody else. So he just said that no, he would not do that, that he would do his best to tell the truth, and not make any jokes about it.

Marlena snorted her disbelief.

#          #          #

Dr. French asked them if they wanted to continue to analyze what had gone wrong, and when they said they didn't she said that recommended a mediator to help them with their divorce. They were able to see the man the very next day. It took them only thirty minutes to agree on all the major points: they would split their cash and investments in half; there was no common debt; each would be responsible for any personal debt, of which there wasn't much; each would keep their own retirement plan; there would be no alimony or palimony; there were no kids to support or educate; and they would sell the house and split the after-commission proceeds.

The mediator was able to sum all this up on one page.

“A lawyer ought to be able to boil this down to about two hundred pages for ten thousand dollars,” Viktor joked. As it turned out, the lawyer would raise issues they had never thought of, and didn't really care about – such as what would happen if, among other ridiculous possibilities, Marlena was pregnant by Viktor but didn't know it – and the final bill would be almost $20,000.

56)                Little Blue Eyes

While eating his meatloaf at Carl’s Deli, Viktor scanned the flyer papers for free cat ads. He found an address in Farmington that was on his way home without being in a direct line, which he thought was a smart idea. He called the number from a phone booth on the way back to the office, rather than from his office phone, and arranged to go at 5:45 and see the kittens they had advertised.

Viktor felt good that he had a plan in motion, and that feeling of practical efficiency only increased as he followed the map to an unnamed subdivision that was much like Avalon Acres, with five or six different models of three-bedroom ranch houses. They were older styles, though, and it was an older layout – the streets were straighter, and the houses were all almost exactly the same distance apart. On the plus side, the trees and plantings were so well established that they felt almost real.

Viktor found the street and then the exact house – 84 – with the name Crawford on the mailbox. He pulled into the driveway, and the moment he turned off the motor his feeling of competency was swamped by anxiety. He felt like he’d pulled up to a date’s house, and had to meet her father first.

Sure enough, a meaty-faced man opened the door. Using Mnemonics, as his father had taught him, Viktor nicknamed the fellow Broderick Crawford, and he actually did look a bit like the guy from “Highway Patrol,” though he didn't speak as rapidly.

When Viktor said he was there about the kittens, the man said they were expecting him but “the wife” was out shopping.

Crawford pointed to a red and white cardboard beer case in a box in the corner of the kitchen. “I can't stand the things,” he said. “But the wife took in a stray, and now look at what we got.”  As he spoke he kept one eye on a small TV, which had the pregame show for the Red Sox and Royals.

There were six gray and white kittens. Although Viktor had said that he was looking for “a” kitten, he now wondered if he should get two, because if he took one and the experiment didn't work, he’d have to get another, and if he took two and it did work, he could always let the second one go.

“I might take two,” Viktor said. “If that's alright. My daughter will be delighted.”

“Take em all if you want,” the man said.

\`                    Viktor asked a couple questions to suggest that he cared about the kittens. How old were they?  Was the mother a nice cat?  And which of the kittens were boys, and which were girls?   He did not ask if they were allergic to cyanide.

Viktor picked two, justifying it by saying things like, “this one is cute,” and “this one is nicely patterned.”

He was embarrassed that he had nothing to put them in because a true cat lover would have brought a basket, and kitty toys, and would have petted the kittens, whereas Viktor had yet to even touch them.

Crawford didn't give a darn. He produced a beer box identical to the first one and stuffed some newspapers in it. Viktor lifted the kittens out of the one box – frightened by how soft and light they were – then settled them in the box, thanked Crawford, and left as quickly as possible.

#          #          #

When Viktor got home he took a unusual amount of time going through the mail. Most of it was junk mail. For those, Viktor stuffed the literature into the prepaid reply envelope to mail it back to the junk mailer – that way they would get to pay the postage both ways, and Jimbo’s job would be a little more secure.

He had a glass of wine with dinner (burgers) and another while he was cleaning up, then watched a couple innings of the Red Sox game,

Once it was good and dark out, and the moment felt right, he got the red and white beer box out of the trunk, laid a piece of plywood over the top so the kittens couldn’t climb out, and took it into the basement,. Both kittens were mewing, and he realized that he didn't have any cat food. But maybe it wouldn’t matter.

First he prepared the Box, filling the bowl with muriatic acid and placing a cyanide tablet in the mechanism. Then, rather than selecting one of the two kittens by going “eeny, meeny, miney, moe,” Viktor just reached in with his eyes closed and grabbed one. He forced himself to look closely at it, because he thought that if this experiment was going to mean anything he needed to experience it fully.

He now wished he’d gotten a cat, because the fact was that he’d never hated kittens the way he hated cats, and this one was very cute. Its eyes were light blue and innocent. like his own.

“Little Blue Eyes,” I’ll call you, Viktor murmured, because that seemed kinder than calling it Subject #1. He placed the kitten in the mini-fridge and closed the door.

He didn't want to be able to hear the kitten moving around, so now he put on a pair of ear muffs.

“Last chance,” he told himself.

“Fire when ready,” he replied.

Then he pressed the button and, in the same motion, hurried up the stairs, to not hear anything.

#          #          #

Viktor watched the Red Sox for fifteen minutes that felt like fifty. He thought he might feel a great suspense about what was happening (or not happening) downstairs, but he didn't. He was more excited about the fact that the Red Sox had a man in scoring position.

When the runner failed to score, Viktor asked himself if he thought the kitten was alive or dead.

“Alive,” he replied.

“Why is that?” he asked.

“Because if this is going to mean something, I wouldn’t want it to be so easy.”

Viktor got his wish, but the Red Sox didn't get theirs, losing 2-0. As the announcer said that they were now seven games under .500 Viktor thought to himself that he, by contrast, was only half a game under.

57)                Subjects 2-7

When Viktor saw Boltzmann the next night, in the tinfoil shed, he told him that he had completed two experiments.

Boltzmann smiled a huge, toothy smile. “And?” he asked, with uncharacteristic eagerness.

“So far it's Kittens 2, Viktor 0.”.

Subject #2 had had the same coloring as Little Blue Eyes, except for one totally white paw. But a very different temperament. Where Little Blue Eyes had been affectionate, rubbing against Viktor’s arm and mewing, White Paw – as he called him – was more provocative. He batted at Viktor with the white paw, like a tiny boxer, then dodged, darted, and batted again, as if he wanted to play tag or some other game.

Viktor thought White Paw was fun, and was pleased that he came out OK.

“Did you dispose of the subjects?” Boltzmann asked.

Viktor cleared his throat. “Not the way you would have,” he said. “I drove over to Gil’s, ostensibly to buy a tiny bottle of dish soap for a hundred dollars, and released them out back, by the dumpsters. I think someone will pick them up, they’re so cute.”

Boltzmann frowned.

“Are you concerned that you might wind up with one of them?” Viktor asked

“No, I only use cats. No kittens.”

“But kittens can grow into cats, if there are no teenage boys or Latvians around.”

“Exactly. So I would prefer that you were more rigorous.”

“Where do you ‘dispose’ of yours?”

Boltzmann hesitated, then said, “In The Farmington River.”

“But there’s so much traffic on Route 44.”

He told Viktor there was a spot, near the golf course, where there were picnic tables and a footbridge. The river was fairly deep at that spot, and fast moving, and there were no picnickers or golfers there in the evening. Boltzmann put his subjects in a black plastic trash bag with a brick.

“What if a subject is still alive?” Viktor asked.

“I treat them all the same,” Boltzmann said.

Later, as Viktor was getting ready to leave, Boltzmann said there was a pile of bricks out back from an old patio, and Viktor could take a few if he wanted to.

On his way home Viktor grabbed one. But just one.

#          #          #

Viktor made a few procedural changes before the next experiment. He plugged in the mini-fridge so the inside light would go on when he shut the door and the cats wouldn’t thrash about as much. He brought a CD player down into the basement and played ZZ Top tunes – loud – after closing the door but before pressing the button, to drown out noises. And now he fed the subjects about an hour before the experiment, thinking that if he got sleepy after eating, cats might, too. Boltzmann would have argued that these changes constituted a change in methodology, but Viktor didn't think it mattered.

“I'm not in this for the long haul,” he muttered. “In fact, I'm not sure that I’m in it for the short haul.”

#          #          #

Subject #3 had been purchased, according to the woman – who stressed the word “purchased” – because the previous two cats had given their daughter asthmatic attacks. They had tried giving the girl medications and steroids, but none of them had solved the problem. The breeder had assured them that this type of cat – a Sphynx, which was basically hairless – was hypoallergenic, but the problems had persisted, even when the cat was kept in another room, and now the girl had to use an inhaler, even at school. Her father was eager to get rid of the beast, but the mother seemed frustrated, and more mad at the girl than the cat.

Viktor thought the cat looked like the pet of a Star Trek alien. He called it “Bat Cat,” because it had such huge ears. Perhaps it could actually hear Viktor’s thoughts with those ears, because it was very wary, and he had some difficulty catching it. But once inside the Box, though, it went dead quiet, like it was hiding from something or someone.

Even after Viktor opened the door it just crouched there, watching him.

#          #          #

Subject #4 was a very young Calico cat – tortoise-shell and white – with a black patch over its left eye, like a pirate. Viktor called it CK, the Calico Kid, even though it was a female. In the World According to Viktor, all cats were female and all dogs were male. CK was a hunter. Its owner had given Viktor a toy mouse on a string, and the cat was all over that. When Viktor finally put it in the Box he put the mouse in there, too.

After pressing the button he went upstairs and watched footage of Atlantis landing that morning. The Calico Kid landed safely, too.

#          #          #

When the score reached 4-0, our hero-to-be paused his experiments and ran a test on the Box using a cyanide pellet instead of Alka-Seltzer, because he thought either the lighter weight or the green color of the cyanide pellet was messing things up.

It worked perfectly the first time.

#          #          #

Viktor nicknamed Subject # 5 the Wild Man. This cat – a mixed breed – scratched Viktor’s hand when he was trying to put it into the Box, and thrashed about so violently once inside that the mini-fridge shook. Viktor had had to go upstairs and wait half an hour before Wild Man went quiet. During that time he watched a news program about how Dan Quayle had ripped into the legal profession, asking questions like, does America really need 70 percent of the world's lawyers?  And, it is healthy for our economy to have 18 million new lawsuits every year?  Marlena would have been outraged, even though malpractice premiums had gone through the roof at her hospital, but Viktor was more or less on Dan’s side.

Finally he tiptoed downstairs and pressed the button. The thrashing started again and Viktor went back upstairs. Viktor hoped that meant that Wild Man was kaput, but he scratched him again on the way out.

#          #          #

Subject # 6 was a female cat with feline AIDS from East Hartford. Viktor named him Death Star. His condition reminded Viktor of a chapter from “Kitty Kare,” so before the experiment he opened to the chapter titled, “Can Feline AIDS be Transmitted to People?”

This is a question, that Doctor K hears more and more, as the number of infected kitties continues to rise. Presently, scientists do not think Feline AIDS can be transmitted to people. If scientists, who by-the-way did not discover AIDS until 1981, even though it had been around for a long time, are right about this, then you have nothing to worry about.

This Death Star did not get blown up by Star Fighters. The owners had told Viktor that this cat should not be released into the general population, especially since it was a female, but Viktor figured that the worst that could happen was that it would put a slight dent in the estimated ten million feral cats plaguing the United States.

#          #          #

Viktor was pretty sure that #7, his lucky number, would be the one. Although he knew better than anyone that the odds on Experiment #7 would be mutually independent of the first six – i.e. 50% – he also knew that the odds of 7 subjects in a row surviving were only 1/128. So if this one survived, it would at least be interesting, statistically.

Although this cat, a mackerel tabby with a white chin, did not look at all like Dandelo, in “The Fly” (a white ragdoll, or some-such), Viktor was pretty sure that this seventh try would be the one, so as he put this cat in the Box he whispered, “Don’t worry Dandelo, you won’t feel a thing. And tomorrow you will be the most famous cat in the world.”

No such luck.

58)                Magic Eight Ball

It was a Saturday, and when Viktor looked out his window that morning he saw that the first leaves of the season were falling, partly because it was fall, and partly because Hurricane Bob, though still a long ways away, was starting to blow.

Were the falling leaves a sign of death?

If Viktor was looking for signs of death there was another one on the news that night. Wade Frankum went on his shooting rampage – eight dead and six wounded – in Sydney that same day.

Viktor had just about had it with these experiments, and he thought that if the cat survived tonight – a 1/256 shot – he would call it a day because enough was enough. Or maybe he would do one more because the next cat would be number nine, which would be too ironic, if cats were supposed to have nine lives.

He’d brought a few flyer papers home and found a good candidate about ten miles west of Avalon. That was good, he thought, because he’d gotten all his other subjects from the east, toward Hartford, or across the river.

He’d used a pay phone to make all the other calls, but he didn't feel like driving up to the convenience store, so he called from the house. And did not listen to hear if there was any click on the line after he spoke to the people.

#          #          #

The owners said they would be out in the daytime, but could Viktor come at about 8:00. He headed out at about quarter of, just as the sky was setting. As soon as he got onto Route 44 he noticed a car with one headlight – a padiddle, Marlena had called them – behind them. But a mile or so later it was gone.

When he got to the house, in Barkhamsted, only the husband was home. And the house seemed quieter than quiet, which made Viktor uneasy in some way that he couldn’t pin down.

The cat was advertised as a Maine coon cat, but it looked like a mutt to Viktor, if cats can be mutts. The ad had said that they were giving the cat up because they were moving, but the man just said his wife was sick of it.

It was getting late, so Viktor wrapped things up, putting the cat in a carrier that another owner had given him, and headed out to the car. On the drive home he another padiddle seemed to be following him, but he was pretty sure that it was missing the headlight on the other side. Either that or he’d looked at the other one directly, and not through the rear-view mirror.

#          #          #

When Viktor was eleven, his grandfather passed away. He had been very fond of his grandfather, but the news had almost no effect on him. He had told himself he should cry, and had squeezed out a tear, even though he got the news by telephone and didn't have to show his feelings to anyone, but he really hadn't felt much.

Viktor felt much the same way when he looked at the body of Magic Eight Ball, which was the name he’d given to Subject #8. He didn't feel anything. The cat didn't even look dead – he just looked like he was sleeping. Viktor only took a quick and then. still holding his breath, hurried upstairs and gave the mini-fridge a few minutes to air out.

When he came down and took a longer look he decided that if he felt anything it was disappointment that the actualization of this cat hadn't had any effect on him. He’d imagined that it might be like the moment when his parachute opened. But he didn't feel anything like that. It had taken so many tries that all the juice had drained out of it, and only a weary relief remained.

Viktor wrapped Magic Eight Ball in a plastic bag. He felt heavier than he had when he was alive, though Viktor didn't know how that could be. He put the bag back in the mini-fridge. Even if it wasn't cold in there, at least it was dark.

#          #          #

When Viktor saw Boltzmann that night his neighbor could tell right away that he had been successful, although he didn't use those words. If a cat lived it was a “1.”  If it died it was a “0.”  Neither digit represented success.

When Viktor told him that he hadn't felt much, Boltzmann looked disappointed. “It may take a few times,” he said”

Viktor shook his head and said no, he didn't think this was his cup of tea. And he didn't have any practical need for binary numbers like Boltzmann did.

“Still,” Boltzmann said, “you did it.”

Viktor smiled a thin smile. “Yes, I did, and in my life there haven't been that many things that are so … memorable.”

“No regrets?”

“No. But I'm done. I’ll take Magic Eight Ball to the River tomorrow night, I guess. As the hurricane is approaching should be a good time. And after that … well, I have a few other things on my plate right now. Like a divorce.”

Later, Viktor would rewind this tape of what turned out to be his last meeting with Boltzmann for almost ten years almost as many times as he had rerun the “Guns of Navarone,” to see if there was any clue that Norbert had known what was going to happen next. Viktor’s conclusion would be that he did not. Because he didn't see any signs of it. Because Norbert H. Boltzmann was far from the best actor on the planet. And because he didn't think that he was capable of that kind of betrayal. Boltzmann could annihilate a hundred million Commies without blinking an eye, but Viktor didn't think he could betray someone who was, quite possibly, his only friend in the world.

59)                It Was Like Getting Pregnant the First Time You Had Sex

“It was like,” Viktor would later say, “getting pregnant the first time you had sex.”

On the Sunday that Viktor took Magic Eight Ball to the river he puttered around the house and worked in his garden. The garden was past its peak, but there were plenty of tomatoes, zucchini, carrots. His winter squash were almost ready to pick and let harden. Because there was so much green, it didn't look so bad from the road now. Also, it had been a couple months since the initial shock, so people seemed kind of used to it. It had been ten weeks since the Association had served him notice, and nothing had happened. Viktor was beginning to think that maybe it was a bluff. He’d called it, and he felt good about that.  Even if he wound up having to pay $2,000, it would be worth it.

He didn't water the garden because Hurricane would be making landfall in Rhode Island soon. He did tie the tomato plants down securely.

A couple people had crossed their picture windows with tape. Viktor thought that he wouldn’t mind if his blew out since it needed to be totally recaulked, and then the insurance would cover it.

As he waited for the sun to set he did not have any wine because he didn't want to be drinking and driving.

#          #          #

At 8:30 put the bag in the backseat of his car and headed off for the picnic spot Norbert H. Boltzmann had told him about. He didn't see anyone following him but he took an exit and did a loop through a subdivision and no one followed him.

He was a little disappointed by that.

He pulled into the parking lot and waited for a few minutes. The air was full of crazy storm energy, but the spot was quiet. He could hear Route 44 in the distance.

For some reason he thought about funerals. There had never been a service for his father, and he missed that sense of closure. He’d been to the one for Marlena’s father, but that hadn't meant that much to him, though it had been incredibly loaded for Marlena.

He asked himself what he had learned from his experiments.

He couldn’t think of anything.

But it wasn't over yet.

#          #          #

When he was ready he got out, opened the trunk, and took out the bag. He walked into the middle of the bridge with the bag, then held it out over the dark water. Even before he got his arm fully extended there was a blinding explosion of light and sound. Spotlights hit him from multiple directions and an amplified voice told him to “Freeze,” and not drop the cat.

“How do they know it's a cat?” he wondered.

Police officers clattered toward him from both ends of the bridge and grabbed him brusquely by his two arms. One of them examined the bag and commented that it was already dead.

Viktor wondered, “Did they think I’d drop a living cat in the river?”

They pushed him into a police car, which had appeared from nowhere, read him his rights, and told him they were taking him to the station. His car was being impounded as evidence.

As the police car pulled out onto Route 44, Viktor figured out what must have happened. There had been a couple articles about missing cats, and Boltzmann was worried that the police would soon catch him. So he set Viktor up. The whole thing about the Box had been part of the plan. And that was why he had told him to take a brick. So none of it meant anything. It was all just a trick.

This thought upset him more than whatever consequences he might face. He was profoundly disappointed in Boltzmann. And suddenly he felt terribly alone. Marlena was gone. Boltzmann was not really his friend. Paula was not available. What did he have in life?

60)                Kilroy Was Here

They booked Viktor and put him in a cell, after first taking away his belt and shoelaces so he couldn’t hang himself. He was tempted to ask them how they’d known that he was thinking of just that, but decided that this was not the ideal time for humor.

When he asked for his phone call, they said that before his call there were two gentlemen who needed to see him.

“What gentlemen?” he asked, but before he got an answer they were already walking into his cell.

There were two of them – a tall, thin, older man, and a shorter, pudgier younger fellow. Viktor wasn't 100% sure, but he thought the taller man was the better dressed moving boss. And the younger, chubbier fellow looked like the man he’d seen shoveling Norbert’s snow.

The tall man introduced himself as Mr. Smith, and the chubby one quickly added that he was Mr. Wesson. If he’d said it with a smile it might have made a nice joke, but he said it with such a straight face that it came across as menacing.

Viktor guessed that they were from a three-letter agency. And not Triple A.

Wesson pulled out a pack cigarettes and offered Viktor a smoke.

Viktor pointed at the No Smoking sign, but Wesson just snorted and continued to hold the pack out.

Viktor took the cigarette but refused the light Wesson offered him saying that he himself didn’t smoke very much but thought perhaps he could use the cigarette later to bribe a guard to give him back his shoelaces so he could commit suicide.

Neither man chuckled or even smiled indulgently, Wesson especially. Now Smith said that they had a request to make of him, and Wesson added that it was a request that Viktor would do well to consider.

Smith paused for a moment, made direct eye contact with our hero-to-be, and then said that their request was that Viktor take full responsibility for what had happened, including the deaths of however many cats were involved, and not implicate, or even mention, any other person or persons.

“You mean not implicate Norbert H. Boltzmann? Viktor asked.

“Mr. Smith meant what he said, that you do not implicate or even mention any other person or persons,” Wesson said, toughly.

“Why not?”

“I am not permitted to tell you,” Smith replied.

Viktor did some quick thinking and then asked if the reason for this offer was that Norbert H. Boltzmann was somehow involved in a secret government project. The minute he said it he wished he hadn't, because now they would strangle him with his own shoelaces to make it look like suicide.

Mr. Wesson looked at Mr. Smith, and then Mr. Wesson looked at Viktor and said that they were not allowed to answer any questions of that sort.

Viktor thought for another minute and then asked if they had badges or anything like that.

Mr. Wesson said that they were not allowed to carry identification.

“Then how do I know are who I think you are?” Viktor asked.

Wesson got a piece of paper out of his briefcase and passed it to Smith. Smith looked at it, made a disapproving face, then asked Viktor if he had indeed claimed $21,723 in charitable contributions on his 1991 taxes?

Viktor said yes, that was exactly right.

Wesson whistled in incredulity.

Smith asked him if he had receipts for every item over $250. And not just for 1990, but also for 1989, 1987. In fact, for the last 20 years.

“OK, what is it that you want from me?” Viktor asked.

Wesson said, in an annoyed voice, “We already told you. We want you to roll over. Take the fall. Plead guilty.”

Smith gestured to Wesson to lighten up, and then said, in a more pleasant tone, “If you help us, in addition to not being audited, you will be offered one of three rewards a year after your arraignment. He nodded at Wesson, who read the options off a piece of paper.

“Option #1 is that you will have the good fortune to receive a lottery ticket good for a substantial payoff, though you will be responsible for all taxes.”  Then added, “Unless you try to avoid them, as in the past.”

“Option #2 is that if you remain at NFG, you will be promoted to Level IV one year from now, which will result in a significant increase in your pension. If you run the numbers, this looks like a better choice than Option #1.”

“And the third option?”

This time Smith spoke, without notes. “Option #3,” he said, “is that one year from now, if all goes well, we will tell you the truth about your father.”

Viktor’s heart skipped a beat. “What do you know about my father?” he asked.

“Everything.”

“Do you know if he’s dead or alive?”

“The man said ‘everything,’” Wesson snarled.

“And what will happen to me if I plead guilty?” Viktor asked.

“If you express remorse, you will get a stiff fine, though much smaller than the bonus we are offering. You will have to complete 20 court-appointed therapy sessions. There will be community service, probably in an animal rescue. And you will be on probation for six months. Not bad, considering.”

“Can you give me a minute?”

“Take two,” Smith said, and looked at his watch. Wesson went to the wall and wrote, “Kilroy was here” in pencil.

Before one minute was up Viktor said that he had one question to ask before he decided.

Wesson spun, pressed his pudgy face into Viktor’s, and said, “We’ll ask the questions, if you don’t mind,” but Smith waved him away and told Viktor to go ahead.

“My question is, did Norbert Boltzmann set me up?  I mean, the police were waiting for me. Someone must have tipped them off.”

Smith reflected for a long moment and then, kind of to Viktor’s surprise, answered his question. “No,” he said. “Dr. Boltzmann did not set you up. We set you up.”

“To protect him?”

“You had your one question,” Wesson said, “Now what's your answer?”

When Viktor said that he would do it, they didn’t say “thank you” or “way to go buddy.”  They simply went over a few details. Viktor was to spend the night in jail and call Marlena in the morning. She was scheduled to get off work at 7:00, they said, but would be working till 9:00 on account of the hurricane. He was not to tell Marlena anything. There would be some paperwork to do, and he should be released by about 11 on bail. From Marlena they would take a check.

“And we were not here,” said Wesson. Then the two men turned and walked toward the cell door, with Smith limping in a way that led Viktor to believe that he had at one time or another been shot and/or tortured.

After they were gone Viktor went to try the cell door, because they didn't seem to have locked it behind them, but right at that moment an officer appeared.

“Do you want your call now?”

“No, those two men suggested that I wait and call my wife in the morning,” Viktor said.

The officer looked left and right, then smiled and said, “What two men?”

61)                The Krazy Kat Killer

Viktor enjoyed his night in his spotlessly clean cell. The bed was firm, and military corners reminded him of Boy Scout camp, or growing up, because his father had insisted on, even after they’d moved off Fort Bragg. That got Viktor wondering if his father was at this minute in a much less sanitary cell in Eastern Europe. He wished he’d told Smith & Wesson that he would only do what they wanted if they would at least tell him whether his father was dead or alive, but it was too late now.

There was no clock in the cell and Viktor didn't wear a watch, but when he heard an increasing commotion out in the office he guessed it was about 8:00, which reminded him that today was a work day. He pressed his call bell and a young officer arrived faster than at a high-class hotel, not that Viktor had ever been to a high-class hotel.

Viktor said he would like to make his call now, and the young man took him out to an office area. No one seemed worried that Viktor would grab a gun and escape, and that disappointed him.

Viktor said that in addition to calling his wife he would like to call his office and tell them that he would not be in today, if he would be allowed two calls.

“Your office is probably closed,” the officer said, “on account of the hurricane.”  He checked a computer screen and said yes, NFG was indeed closed for the day.

It was a few minutes after 8:00 when Viktor called the hospital, and Smith & Wesson were right – Marlena was still there. It took a while for them to get her on the phone, and then she sounded worried, like she thought Viktor had had a heart attack.

When he told her he’d been arrested he could sense her concern morph into annoyance. She asked him what it was for, and said that it was probably best to not talk on the phone but could she come and bail him out?  There was another long pause and then she said she would, but in an annoyed tone. She could be there by 10:00. He said that was fine, that they weren't doing a Rodney King on him. “Not yet, anyway,” he added.

The officer shot him a look, then took him back to his cell.

As Viktor waited for Marlena he had a funny thought, which was that he used to say she was like Florence Nightingale. Now she was Florence Night-in-jail. It was a good sign, he thought, that he was still making silly jokes, and in some ways he felt more alive than on a day when – at least if there hadn't been a hurricane – he would now be at his desk not finding any causes or correlations for cancer and wishing he was doing something else with his pathetic life.

#          #          #

After Marlena paid the bail the police gave Viktor back his things. Among then was his one koruna coin. When he picked it up it felt different – as if someone had substituted one that was older or newer. It was the same date, though.

Viktor asked Marlena if she would drive him home because his car had been impounded as evidence.

“Were you in an accident?” she asked, then leaped to: “Was it a DUI?”

He said no, and told her a version of the story. He said he’d been doing some scientific experiments. He’d used cats as his subjects and one of them had, unfortunately, died. He had been caught dropping its body into the river, and the authorities somehow thought that he was behind the disappearance of a larger number of cats.

“Good lord!” she cried, and then, about two seconds later asked him if Dr. Boltzmann, as she liked to call him, was in any way involved.

Viktor said no. That was a lie, and Viktor did not like to lie, but he’d made a promise, and he was a promise-keeper even more than he was not a liar, especially when it might be a matter of national security.

As they pulled into Avalon Acres a big white moving truck with the word SMITH on the side was just turning out onto Route 44. Viktor thought he recognized the pudgy young man in the passenger seat, though he only got a momentary look.

Marlena dropped him off and then left just saying, “We’ll talk about this more at a better time.”

Viktor got the mail out of the mailbox and the paper off the front steps. Before he opened the door, though, he looked down at Boltzmann’s house.
