It didn't look any different than it did any other Monday at about noon. But it felt different, like his coin. And he was willing to bet that Boltzmann’s car would not be in the driveway that evening – not on the left side, not on the right side, not in the driveway at all.

#          #          #

The newspaper said that Hurricane Bob wasn't the only thing that was moving and shaking the world that morning. The whole darned Communist Empire was rattling itself to pieces, like a car with a bad tie rod. There had been a coup. Hard-liners were in command. And their drunken fingers were inches away from the buttons, just as Norbert H. Boltzmann had described.

Viktor looked through the entire paper, and didn't see anything about his arrest. That made sense, because it was a morning paper. But there must have been a story in the afternoon paper, or on the radio, because curiosity-seekers began driving by at about 2:30.

He didn't realize that's what they were at first, or why kept pointing at his house like there was a unicorn on his roof.

Right about then the phone rang. It was McNutty, who told Viktor that there was a story on the radio about how some “Krazy Kat Killer” had been arrested for offing fifty or sixty cats. And it was Viktor.

“Fifty or sixty!” Viktor cried, “that’s impossible!” But in the same moment he calculated that Boltzmann had lived in Avalon Acres for about 34 weeks, so fifty or sixty cats worked out to less than two per week, which was exactly his long-term average.

McNutty said that police divers had gone into the Farmington River near the golf course and come up with forty or fifty bodies, right where the Krazy Kat Killer had been about to dump another one. They had video of it. And people were very upset.

“Is there anything I can do to help?” McNutty asked.

Viktor said no, there wasn't, but he really appreciated the thought. And he did. It was good to have a friend, he thought, never guessing that he would very soon require the muscular services of another one.

62)                Mob and Bob

The first few passers-by had looked like lost tourists. Then an older couple parked across the street. The man got out and pointed at Viktor’s house. The woman seemed embarrassed and motioned for her husband to get back in the car, but he waved her off. A couple minutes later another car pulled in behind them and a younger man got out and spoke to the older man. Then the wives joined them. One of them had a camera and took pictures of the house.

Viktor felt annoyed, but perhaps also tickled in a way – at his fifteen minutes of fame. He pulled down all the blinds, but watched the action through a crack.

By 4:00 there must have been five cars and a dozen people across the street – some from the neighborhood, others from elsewhere – all talking and pointing, some holding afternoon edition newspapers. Viktor was eager to see what the paper said, but he didn't want to go out. He turned on the TV but there was nothing there.

At about this time Viktor began to get phone calls from the press. Their tone was belligerent and pestering. After three such calls he took the phone off the hook, and now he was feeling more angry than intrigued.

At 4:30 it started to rain and the wind blew in gusts that shook the trees and thudded against the house. According to TV reports, the Rhode Island coast would absorb most of the hurricane, and the greater Hartford area would only experience rain and wind, but people were advised to stay indoors. As the rain picked up, a few of the curiosity-seekers left, and others retreated to their cars. That was the good news. The bad news was that the people who remained seemed to draw a dark energy from the storm.

At 5:30 a TV news truck parked on Viktor’s side of the street. It had a microwave dish on top.

“My God,” Viktor thought, “they’re going to put it on the 6:00 news.”  He was upset. But also thrilled.

A newswoman who Viktor didn't recognize and her camera crew began interviewing people. Almost simultaneously, some people showed up with signs. “Drown KKK” said one. “SICKO,” read another. A second camera took shots of those.

People had now moved from the other side of the street to the curb on Viktor’s side, though they had not yet crossed his property line. Then a man wadded up a newspaper and began throwing balls of it onto Viktor’s lawn and garden. When the TV crew filmed him, other people began throwing things.

At quarter to 6:00 a second TV truck showed up, and now Viktor could hear a couple helicopters overhead.

People began to chant, “Krazy Kat Killer, Krazy Kat Killer, Krazy Kat Killer,” over and over again. With this chanting, Viktor felt fear in the pit of his stomach for the first time.

He’d had the TV on all afternoon, for company as much as anything else. At 6:00 he heard the opening anthem for the news. Outside people were huddled under tarps and watching monitors. Viktor went into his living room and at about 6:15 there it was – his own house, as seen from the street, and from the air.

The announcers said that a man from Avalon had been arrested on Sunday night dropping a dead cat into the Farmington River, not far from the Blue Fox Run Golf Course. Upon investigation, the police had discovered the corpses of “scores” of cats, every one of them in a black plastic bag with a brick in it. Cats had been disappearing from the area for some months and there might be a connection between the two stories. They did not give Viktor’s name or address, but it was evidently in some of the papers or on the radio.

The mood calmed a bit when the news crew left at 6:30. But then, as the sky darkened with the storm, and the day, the crowd became mobbish. Soon there must have been fifty people out front, and when they started throwing things this time,, it wasn't just newspapers. Viktor heard some branches hit the house, some clumps of sod, and then small stones.

One of the mob knocked over his mailbox. Another person ripped the box off the post and took it away, evidently as a souvenir.

Viktor had a gun – the twenty-two his father had given him for his 12th birthday – and he thought of firing a warning shot in the air. But he didn't have a permit for the gun. And who knows how the crowd would react. The best thing would be to stay in the house. Or, if things got really bad, blast out of the garage and make a run for it in his car.

Right then someone ran up to the garage with a can of spray paint and wrote something on the door. Viktor couldn’t see it, but he guessed that it had at least three “K’s” in it. And maybe a “F” or two.

As if on command, the mob began to rise like a tide onto the lower half of Viktor’s lawn. They stood on the border of grass that remained below the garden, then crossed into the garden itself. They pulled up the stakes and held them like swords. Someone pulled up a carrot and ate it. Right then a rock hit his picture window hard, and cracked it.

Viktor took a deep breath. “Run,” said one voice. “Fight,” said another. He must have been more angry than scared because he opened the front door and stepped onto the porch.

“Get off my property or I’ll call the police,” he cried.

“You're the criminal!” they replied. And then they began chanting “Krazy Kat Killer” as loud as at the ball park.

One of his own tomatoes hit Viktor in the shoulder. Another vegetable hit him on the cheek. He knew he should retreat, but he had spent his whole life retreating. So he stepped down and waded into the crowd, making a fist.

Within two seconds he’d been punched several times and was on the ground.

Within five seconds he’d been kicked in the side.

He looked up and there was a circle of angry people staring down at him.

He got kicked again, so hard that he saw stars.

Then a deep voice commanded, “Back off assholes!” 

The heads above him all turned to look off to the right, and then backed off, out of view, as someone else approached. Viktor was sure it would be a police officer, but it wasn't. It was Chuck “Flat Top” Nelson. Chuck reached down a meaty fist and yanked Viktor up.

Chuck was wearing a Giants jersey, and brandishing a baseball bat. It was kind of a mixed metaphor, but it worked.

Viktor walked over to the Nelson’s house. From there he watched as a police car arrived and told people to leave. The car remained for about half an hour, then left.

Viktor and Chuck sat in the living room sipping Canadian Club while Claire vacuumed upstairs, even though it was 10:00 at night.

Viktor thanked Chuck.

Chuck gave him a long, suspicious look, and then asked, “Did this have anything to do with the feller at the end of the cully sack?”

Viktor hesitated, then did the right thing. “Off the record,” he said, “it might have.”

“I thought so,” Chuck nodded. “Otherwise it would have been a huge coincident that the nut job moved out this morning.”

That was it. Other than that, they just talked about sports for a couple hours, and then Viktor slept fitfully on their couch.

63)                Early Retirement

When Viktor got to his desk the next morning he had a message from HR. The same young woman who had told him, just one month earlier, that he had to wait till December 1st to retire now said that he would qualify for a full pension on September 1st, which was only 9 work days away. He had more than that in accrued vacation days and sick time, she said, and so she recommended that, all things considered, he leave today. She didn't say a word about what had happened over the weekend.

Viktor accepted the offer and signed the papers.

As he packed his things and left NFG, people looked at him, but not in the same way that they looked at people who had been RIF’ed.

“Well,” he said to himself once he was outside, “that was pretty easy after all.” 

He didn't feel terrible. If anything, felt a sense of lightness and relief.

#          #          #

Viktor wasn't just famous for fifteen minutes. It was more like fifteen days, though he was more infamous than famous.

Friends and co-workers who’d been interviewed all said the Krazy Kat Killer had seemed pretty normal – “almost too normal,” was how one person put it – though he’d been acting “weird” recently. He’d told a number of people he was unhappy in his job but uncertain of what he wanted to do with his life. He’d said he was having a Midlife Crisis. He’d dug up his front lawn to put in a vegetable garden. He was in the middle of divorcing his wife, a nurse who was involved in many charitable causes. He’d always had a “thing” about cats. He had (Viktor had no idea how they knew this, since they never searched his house) a collection of Dead Cat Books. At one point, a few years earlier, he’d put a dart board with a cat on it on the back of his office door, but had taken it down when people complained. On his computer at work there was a lot of information suggesting that he thought cats were responsible for many diseases. He might have thought he was doing the world a favor as he set out to “exterminate” them. Innumerable people – way more than eight – had identified him as the man who’d responded to free cat ads in the paper. His method of killing the cats appeared to have been some kind of gas chamber with cyanide in it, because there were traces of cyanide in the blood of the dead cats the police had found. In total might have killed a hundred cats before he was apprehended by police.

Aside from the total number of cats, the moniker of Krazy Kat Killer, and the words “exterminate” and “gas chamber,” Viktor wasn't that upset by the stories. He might have even been disappointed when the activity seemed to die down.

#          #          #

One of the most interesting things about the stories in the press was that there was not a word about Norbert H. Boltzmann, or Viktor’s Schrödinger Box.

Viktor’s lawyer thought it was “odd” that the police had not gotten a warrant and searched his house for other evidence.

“Do you have any idea why that might be?” he asked.

“I have no idea,” Viktor said.

The lawyer reflected for a moment, then asked Viktor if he had any friends in high places.

“I know a guy who’s co-captain of a candlepin bowling team,” Viktor replied, referring to Chuck “Flat Top” Nelson.

The lawyer did not laugh, and mused that it almost felt like the FBI or CIA might be involved.

“My father did some work for the CIC,” Viktor said.

That raised an eyebrow. “And what about you?” the lawyer asked. “Do you have any involvement with those people?”

“If I did, I wouldn’t be able to tell you,” Viktor replied, borrowing a line from his old man.

#          #          #

The arraignment was held on Monday, September 2nd. Against his lawyer’s advice Viktor pleaded guilty to killing at least sixty-six cats. To his lawyer’s astonishment the judge gave him a mild sentence. A five thousand dollar fine, six months of community service in an animal rescue, 20 visits of court-mandated therapy, and a six month probation. He said it was because of Viktor’s outstandingly clean record – not even a library fine.

The arraignment had taken place behind closed doors. When Viktor and his lawyer came out, Smith & Wesson were seated in the hall outside. Smith smiled at him. Wesson did not.

“Do you know those men?” his lawyer asked once they were outside.

“What men?” Viktor replied with a smile he didn't bother to hide.

#          #          #

There was almost nothing in the papers about Viktor’s sentencing. One reporter wrote a story claiming that it was being hushed up, but then he didn't write anything else.

When Viktor went to see his probation office she assigned him to work one day a week at an Animal Rescue in Barkhamsted, and she approved Dr. French as his court-mandated counselor. He had to see his Probation Officer once a week, and he was not to leave the state, but if he didn't get in any trouble in the next six months, completed his community service, and Dr. French gave him a good report, he’d be a free man on March 1, 1992.

“Yeah,” Viktor thought to himself. “And then will do I do?”

64)                Viktor Gives Marlena a Hug

Now that he wasn't working, Viktor had plenty of time for chores. He had the picture window replaced after telling the insurance company a branch had hit it during the hurricane, which wasn't technically a lie. He painted over the KKK on his garage – chuckling that it looked like three strikeouts –  but the paint was a different shade or white and glossier, so you could see that something had been painted over. He tidied up his garden, but it was kind of running out of steam at this point, anyway.

There was now a For Sale sign on the lawn in front of Norbert H. Boltzmann’s house. Although Boltzmann was clearly gone Viktor kept looking over there as if expecting to see him pull into the driveway. Then, one evening, after a couple glasses of wine, he decided to do some detective work.

He waited till it was dark, then walked to the vernal pond and then doubled back. He hid in the bushes and pushed the garage door clicker he still had from Dan Ball. He expected the door it would just shudder against the lock as it had before, but it motored up and the light went on inside. Viktor left it like that for a moment, then hurried in and clicked it down again.

The garage was empty, except for items that Dan Ball had left because he wouldn’t need them at his condo – a lawn mower, gardening tools, some cans of paint, and so on. Viktor did find a few clues, though. There were a couple morsels of dry cat food under some shelves. And it looked like a heavy had been dragged across the floor, because there were white scrapes in the gray concrete.

The door from the garage to the rec room was no longer locked, nor the one to the kitchen. Viktor went upstairs first. Though he left the lights off, everything looked just as it had when Norbert was there, which is to say just as it had when Dan Ball was there.

In the pool room the computer, fax machine and so on were gone, but the furniture was still there, and the pool table.

The mirror behind the pool table was gone, and there was a nail hole where it had been. Viktor inspected the hole and thought it seemed too big for just a nail, like a wire might have come through there.

When he examined the pool table itself had to smile. Four balls were lined up on the head rail with the numbers all nice and level. They were the 1, the 8, the 4, and the 2.

#          #          #

When Viktor looked up, the mirror was back on the wall. Was reflected in it and, standing behind him, Norbert Boltzmann.

“Was there a camera behind this mirror?” Viktor asked.

Boltzmann nodded.

“And did they figure out about the tinfoil shed?”

Boltzmann nodded again.

“So that's how they knew when I was going to the river?”

“Yes,” Boltzmann said. “I was not the one who tipped them off.”

“I never thought you were,” Viktor lied.

“I appreciate that,” said Boltzmann.

“I don’t suppose you can tell me where you went?”

Boltzmann shook his head.

“I enjoyed meeting you,” Viktor said.

Boltzmann smiled a toothy smile.

“I think what happened may wind up being good for me,” Viktor added.

“I hope so,” Boltzmann said. “You deserve it.”

#          #          #

Viktor went out to the tool shed. As he expected, there were only tools in there – not a speck of tinfoil. The only footprints he could find in the mushy ground seemed to be his own. It reminded him of when he’d gone out to look at where the alien ship had landed out behind his house in New Hampshire.

Another interesting detail was that the pile of bricks was gone. But not totally gone. They’d been moved to his own backyard.

#          #          #

A couple days later, Marlena and her friend Patty came by with a We-Haul. Patty was not at all what Viktor expected. She was only about thirty years, thin, blonde, and very attractive. He never in a million years would have guessed that she was a lesbian.

Marlena and Patty boxed and loaded up her things. Marlena said she didn't want any of the furniture – especially not the things her mother had given them, like the grandfather clock or the fake leather club chair.

Viktor said that he’d sell that stuff with the house and she’d get half.

“Don’t worry about it,” she replied.

She asked him how he was doing.

He that he still had “Phantom Wife Syndrome.”  It was a medical joke so she got it, and gave him a little chuckle.

He said that he hoped everything worked out great for her and Patty, and moved toward her to give her a hug.

She froze for a moment, then accepted it, and then started crying.

Patty moved toward her protectively, then let them be.

Viktor found that he was sobbing, too.

“There were a lot of good parts,” he said.

“Yes,” she said, “there certainly were.”

But after they had left he couldn’t actually think of too many times that had been all that good. Not after the first few years.

65)                Viktor Adopts Dandelo

Viktor was assigned to work every Thursday afternoon at an animal rescue out in Barkhamsted. As they were showing him around on his first day he spotted a cat that he recognized. It recognized him, too. It was Dandelo.

They said he’d been there for a couple weeks.

“I’ll take him,” Viktor said, without thought or hesitation.

The young woman showing him around was delighted, but the older woman who ran the place regarded Viktor with suspicion.

Viktor thought that his time at the Rescue would just be a penance, but it was much more than that.

“I now see them as living beings, and it have a much warmer feeling for them,” he told Dr. French. Then, after an extended beat he added, “But I’m developing a darker view of human beings after seeing some of the things that people have done to their pets.”

He liked his drives out to Barkhamsted in the fall, with the leaves changing. And he liked the people he worked with, most of whom were volunteers in their teens. He didn't mind that they were highly judgmental, because it felt like they were trying to make things better.

Whenever his name reminded someone of what he’d done, and they looked at him like he was a sicko, he wanted to tell them that he’d only killed one cat. But he was loyal to his promise to Smith & Wesson. Also, Dr. French helped him see that the number didn't matter that much, and he should not try to make what he had done seem smaller. To the contrary, he should try to see it as big as possible. She also pointed out that his penance had to be much broader than apologizing for what he had done to that one cat. He had also hurt Marlena. And he had hurt himself.

He did his best in all these regards, and it must have worked a little because on his last day the Rescue threw a mini-party for him with cupcakes and apple juice. He brought Dandelo, who was reluctant to go in at first, and then joined the party. People said good things about Viktor and even made a few jokes that were close to the edge. Even the woman who ran the place allowed as how he had “been punctual, and good to work with.”  When it was his turn to speak, Viktor said that his time there had meant a great deal to him, and then handed the manager a $5,000 donation.

She looked embarrassed.

#          #          #

Although Viktor had concluded that it probably didn't make sense for him to try to go back to grad school in astrophysics, he took the GRE’s. He did very well in quantitative reasoning, but not as well as before, and only so-so in the other areas. He spoke to the Admissions at Cal Tech and a couple other places, and they were politely discouraging.

At the same time, he did a little reading and came to the conclusion that modern astronomy – like modern actuarial science – was now the realm of supercomputers, and people hardly even looked through telescopes anymore.

#          #          #

Selling the house was an ordeal. There were tons of curiosity seekers, because of The KKK incident. Viktor had hidden the mini-fridge away, but he could see people searching for it, or other evidence of what had happened, because an “investigative journalists” had uncovered more details than even the police had unearthed or, as Viktor joked to himself, un-rivered, perhaps through leaks from three-letter agencies.

Boltzmann’s house was also for sale. It didn't help, the real estate agent said, to have two houses for sale right next to each other. The good news was that Viktor’s house was a good deal cheaper, not being one of the A-Plan models.

The dead garden in the front yard was a major drawback, so Viktor had it sodded. Soon after that he got a pretty good offer with no conditions, and the people wanted to close before the end of the year, for tax reasons. Although it was substantially less than he had hoped to get, the house had nonetheless tripled in value since he and Marlena bought it in 1974 – a compound growth rate, Viktor calculated in his head, of 6.64% over 17 years.

They closed on New Year’ Eve. That same day, the Dow Jones closed at its high for the year, 3,168. Although Viktor’s investments hadn't kept pace, he’d done OK.

The moon was not full that New Year’s Eve, but Viktor didn’t take that as a sign – positive, negative or otherwise.

#          #          #

He called Paula a number of times, and enjoyed talking to her, but it was clear that she needed to stay where she was, even if she wasn't happy. She said that she knew that wasn't ideal, but it was the best she could do. He told her he respected that decision, and he did.

There was woman at the end of the street whose husband had left her after what was rumored to have been a sex change operation (him, not her). She said that there had never been any such operation, but her husband had indeed gone off with a very masculine man, so she could see where the idea came from.

She was and thin and nervous. They went on a few dates and – once Viktor was officially divorced – they slept together. When he said it had been a long time, she asked him if he and Marlena had not had regular sex.

“Oh yes,” he said, “very regular. Once a Platonic Year.”

“Only once a year?” she cried.

And then Viktor explained that that a Platonic Year was the 26,000 years that it took the Earth to wobble through a complete precession of the equinoxes and the North Star returned to its position in the sky, and there was some truth to that. It had been a long time since north was north.

After Viktor closed on his house she suggested that he should move in with her. He was tempted, but worried that she drank too much. He himself had pretty much stopped drinking.

He rented an apartment in the same complex where Dan Ball had moved instead. They had a pool table in the common area and he spent a lot of time there shooting pool with Norbert H. Boltzmann, his father, and whoever else showed up.

#          #          #

On [January 26](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_26 "January 26")th [Boris Yeltsin](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin "Boris Yeltsin") announced that [Russia](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia "Russia") would stop targeting cities of the [United States](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States "United States") with [nuclear weapons](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapon "Nuclear weapon"). In return, [George H. W. Bush](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush "George H. W. Bush") announced that the United States would stop targeting Russia and the remaining [communist](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism "Communism") states with nuclear weapons,

“That’s good news for America,” Viktor thought. “But bad news for Norbert H. Boltzmann. He wondered where he was, and did some research, while fearing that he might find that a person by that name had died in a car crash. But there was no trace of him – not even that he had ever lived in Connecticut.

#          #          #

To succeed at something, as much as anything else, he took a CPA course, finished it in record time, and passed his exam with a very high grade. He told people that he could now answer the question, “How many accountants does it take to change a light bulb?”  When they asked him what the answer was he’d reply, “How many did it take last year?”  

He said that he could also help them make a small fortune. Provided they started with a large one.

People were laughing at his jokes again. Not because they were funny. But because Viktor was more likeable than he had been for a few years there.

#          #          #

As the 1992 Winter Olympics ended, and Orion rose later and later in the evening, Viktor spent some serious time wondering what he should do next.

He had the humorous idea that maybe he should get a Magic Eight Ball and ask it some questions. But he didn't.

He knew he had to get away, so he tried to ask a simpler question first:

Where next?

He thought about moving to his mother’s house for a while, but that would be moving backwards. He did think he might like to move north. If not New Hampshire, then maybe Vermont. Vermont was like New Hampshire but greener. He had hardly ever been there but …

It hit him like a bolt of lightning – not only had he been to Vermont, but he’d been there with his father. And if, by any chance, his father was still alive, there was a certain town in Vermont where he might be. And if he wasn't there himself, it was where he might look for Viktor. And even if neither of those eventualities came to pass, it sounded like it might be a sweet spot.

66)                The Nine Things Viktor Learned from Dr. French

Viktor had told himself the court-mandated counseling was a pretty sweet deal. He would get to see Dr. French 20 times, and the court would pay her. That was worth two grand right there. Not that it was two grand he would have otherwise spent.

At their first session, Dr. French asked him why he’d come to see her.

He said that he was there because the court told him he had to go twenty times in order to get free. “You know,” he added, like in the movie ‘Slap Shot.’  Two minutes, by yourself, you know and you feel shame, you know. And then you get free.”

Dr. French said she hadn't seen “Slap Shot” recently.

Viktor smiled at the word “recently.”  It was his kind of remark.

Viktor figured Dr. French would now ask him if there were any other reasons why he was there but she didn't. She just nodded, said that sounded like a good reason, and asked him if there was anything he would like to talk about while he was there.

It took him a while to get going. But once he did, he found that he had plenty to talk about.

#          #          #

Over those sessions, Viktor had quite a few insights. The nine most important insights (Viktor resisted the idea of ten, because everyone made lists of ten) were:

#          #          #

Insight #1:  Everyone wants something from life. What Viktor wanted was connection. He wanted to be part of something, ideally something important, but he would settle for something unimportant. He hadn't gotten that in his family of origin because his father was an egotist and his mother was a hypochondriac. He tried to get them to connect with him by being funny, or brilliant, but they only connected with the behaviors, not him. It didn't help any that they moved every other week so he never had any long-term friends or even favorite places.

“I should have at least joined a candlepin bowling league,” he quipped. And then added, “or gotten a dog. Yes, I think a dog might have made a big difference.”

#          #          #

Insight #2:  When Viktor was sick in college it wasn't just the flu. He’d come loose. He’d pinned his hopes on becoming a World-Famous Astronomer, and when that dream slipped away from him he had nothing to replace it. He thought he’d found a connection with Marlena, but he was just sick and desperate. What he wanted from her was kind of what she wanted from him, but neither of them had it to give, as they had previously discussed.

#          #          #

Insight #3:  He was probably right that Marlena wanted to be a patient and have him say “Oh my god, how awful.”  That was why she got pregnant so young. It was possibly why she couldn’t conceive. But Viktor couldn’t reach out to her because he was not standing on the ground himself. All those times when he looked down to see if he was casting a shadow – that wasn't just a joke.

#          #          #

Insight #4:  Because Viktor wanted desperately to be part of something, he built something. But it was not the right something. Marlena was not the right person, and he’d known that right away. His job was not the right one, and he’d known that right away. The suburb where they lived was not the right place, and he had known that right away, too. He settled for the wrong things because he was too afraid to have nothing. He always thought he was always the most honest person in the world, but he wasn't. He didn't lie, but he wasn't honest, either, especially with himself.

#          #          #

Insight #5:  Viktor tried to escape from the lies – and become alive – in daydreams. The daydream in which Viktor had a star named after him, which made him a star. The daydream in which Viktor joined was part of the Agency, with his father. The daydream in which Viktor was not only an alien, he was kind of a secret agent alien.  

#          #          #

Insight #6:  Norbert H. Boltzmann seemed to offer a bridge to one or more of those daydreams, or maybe a new one. If nothing else, Viktor hoped that Boltzmann could bring him to life in some way. If Norbert H. Boltzmann had not existed, Viktor would have had to daydream someone like him. And maybe he had, to some extent, though there had been a man living in that house.

#          #          #

Insight #7:  The eight cats were not the subjects of Viktor’s experiments. He was. And when he looked at the body of Magic Eight ball, and felt nothing, it was because he – Viktor – was not alive.

#          #          #

Insight #8:  The real reason Viktor did his experiment was to destroy his false world. He was too rational to do it himself, too loyal to leave Marlena, so he blew it up. He knew Marlena would see the phone calls. He knew the tinfoil shed was bugged. He knew he would get caught.

#          #          #

Insight #9:  The most important word in “World-Famous Astronomer” was not “Astronomer.”  It was “famous.”  The reason Viktor wanted to discover why RR Lyrae stars pulsed the way they did was not because solving that mystery would bring any great benefit to the world. It was so that people would applaud him. It was the same reason he used his spoon backwards and put his mittens on his feet, the same reason why he studied so hard in high school, the same reason he told jokes, the same reason he had been delighted to be analyst of the year, even though he despised insurance.

“But it isn't it immature to just crave attention,” Viktor asked Dr. French.

“Yes,” she said, “it is. But it is very human. And it is very you.”

So it didn't matter if he didn't go to grad school in Astrophysics. But he would need to find something. And to do that he might need to take some chances.

“Which bring us back to the joke about how many therapists does it take to change a light bulb,” Viktor said.

“Do you mean only one therapist but twenty visits?” Dr. French asked him.

“No, I mean only one therapist, but the light bulb has to really want to change.”

“And do you really want to change, Viktor?”

“Yes.”

Now she smiled the first warm smile he could remember, patted him on the head like he was a little boy, without raising her hand or rising from her chair, and told him that he would change, and would get happier, day by day, though there would be setbacks along the way.

A year later she died.

67)                Viktor Revisits Shoreham

Viktor went to Shoreham because he thought he might find his father there. On account of something that had happened back in the summer of 1961, about when Gus Grissom was piloting the second Mercury flight.

Viktor’s father was at that time the regional manager for a company that sold circuit boards. His home office was in Manchester, but he took long road trips – sometimes overnight – to Portland, Bangor, Rutland, Burlington and the like.

One day, since school was out, Viktor Sr. asked Viktor Jr. if he’d like to go along on an excursion to Burlington and then back, and our hero-to-be said sure, figuring that his father wanted a long drive to tell him about the birds and the bees.

They got up early, and drove most of the morning because this was before Route 89 was completed. In Montpelier, Viktor’s father showed him around his office, but didn't appear to do any business there. When they arrived in Burlington, Viktor Sr. didn't go into his office at all. Instead, he went into a diner, sat at the counter reading the newspaper and drinking a quick cup of coffee, while Viktor waited in the car, which he had parked a block and a half away, even though there spaces right in front of the diner. Viktor had good eyes, though, and could see that after his father had been there only a couple minutes a man with a newspaper came in, sat beside his father, and also had a cup of coffee. Then the other man hurried off, taking a newspaper with him. His father finished his coffee, and then came out with a different wrong newspaper.

After lunch at a different place they drove to a spot where Viktor could skip rocks out onto Lake Champlain while his father sat in the car and did a crossword puzzle in the newspaper, which he then dropped in the trash. Then they drove all the way back to Cleveland, arriving after the sun had set. Had his father driven more than 300 miles, much of it on small roads, simply to check in at one office and have a cup of coffee in a certain eatery?

The trip only made sense if you assumed that there was something unusual about that newspaper, and that his father had taken Viktor along as a disguise. That would have also explained why his father took back roads for the last part of the trip. He said they were taking a short cut, but looking at it years later, on the map, Viktor could see that it was definitely not shorter. Not if it took them, as it did, through the quaint town of Shoreham, Vermont.

But, to get to the point. As they drove along, rather than having conversations about the birds and the bees, they played a game called, “Powers of Observation.”  You would spot something along the road – a roadside vegetable stand for example – and then, a few miles later, you’d challenge the other player to remember something about it. What color was it?  How much were they charging for a dozen ears of corn?   Etc. The idea was to sharpen your powers of observation.

On the way up to Burlington, a few minutes after they’d passed through Shoreham, his father asked him, “Did you happen to notice what semi-famous person was born in that town we just passed through?”  And Viktor was somehow able to recall a small sign in the center of the town that said it was the birthplace of Levi P. Morton (Vice President of the United States from 1889 to 1893). He was proud of his answer, and so was his father. He said that he was really coming along as a player. But the best part of the story came on the trip back, when Viktor turned the table on his father and asked him to recall the same name. And he said Morton P. Levy. The result was a rare victory for Viktor Jr..

As a result of that incident, the name Levi (Parsons) Morton became a game between the two of them. Once or twice a year they’d ask each other the name of that fellow in Vermont. Viktor always remembered, but his father continued to – or maybe pretended to, since his father was infallible – reverse the names.

Viktor Sr. was the one who’d proposed turning Levi P. Morton into a secret password. Perhaps he feared that his secret work would cause him to be held hostage at some point, so he told his son that if anyone ever contacted him pretending to be him, or if Viktor ever got a message from his father that he thought might have been sent under duress, he should ask for the name of the guy in Vermont. To which Viktor replied, “And if you get it backwards I’ll know it's you.”  Whereupon his father, not one to laugh a lot, laughed a big but nervous laugh of his own.

Which is why, some thirty years later, Viktor decided to head up to Shoreham. Because he thought it was possible that his father was actually living there.

#          #          #

On March 1, 1992 Viktor’s probation ended and he was allowed to leave the state. He did so immediately, and just in time, as it turned out, because half an hour after he left Hartford a rogue Commie general launched twelve missiles and one got through, wiping out Hartford. When Viktor saw the mushroom cloud in his rear view mirror he stepped on the gas.

“That was close,” he murmured to Dandelo, who was curled up in the passenger seat.

Before leaving town Viktor had done a tricky thing, which was to the Registry of Motor Vehicles and sweet-talk a young woman into believing that they’d made a mistake with his name. They had him down as Viktor A. Hal, but he was really Harold A. Victor, as it seemed to say on some impressive looking documents written in Czech.

When she looked skeptical he interrupted her train of thought by telling her how Anton Cermak, another Czech, had saved America by “taking a bullet” for Franklin Delano Roosevelt. In fact he might have just been hit accidentally, but Viktor didn't worry about that detail.

“He was my great uncle,” Viktor lied.

She smiled a smile that looked knowing, and stamped his papers, and with those small changes the Czech-sounding Viktor Hal became the English-sounding Harold Viktor, who people were less likely to look at askance and  ask, “Oh, are you the Krazy Kat Killer?”

Viktor kept his old identity as a financial shell, so he was now kind of two people.

If not more.

68)                Viktor Meets Tabitha

Viktor spent three days looking for his father in Shoreham, without any luck. He fell in love with the place, though – especially the stars, which were as bright and clear as the stars had been in New Hampshire when he was a boy (brings up telescope). So when he saw an ad for a room in a farmhouse a couple miles out of town that could be rented by the week, he thought maybe he would hang around a little while. He liked the idea of being in the country. He thought Dandelo would also like the opportunity to get outdoors, though he worried about him killing birds so he put a bell on him.

When he phoned the number on the ad, a woman with a Spanish accent answered the phone. When she said her name was Tabitha, Viktor guessed figured she was the housecleaner.

The house was a huge, gray and white Victorian at the top of a hill – kind of a cross between the houses in “Psycho” and “Christina’s World,” was how Viktor would later describe it to McNutty. When he got to the top of the long driveway a tall, dark-skinned woman with dreadlocks was waiting for him, as if she had somehow known exactly when he would arrive. When she introduced herself as Tabitha and said she was the owner, Viktor was embarrassed.

“Don’t worry,” she said, “that happens all the time.”  Even though he had not said a word.

Viktor told her that his name was Harold – Harold Viktor.

She gave him a penetrating look and replied, “Te conozco bacalao, aunque vengas disfrasao."

He said, “What?”

“It's nice to meet you,” she smiled.

#          #          #

It was a thirteen room house with forty-nine acres out back. Tabitha did healing work in one of the rooms. She rented a number of the other rooms to people with hard-to-spell names who practiced other “touchy-feely” arts, like acupuncture, Astrology, or crystal healing. When Viktor remarked that his profession – accounting – was pretty far removed from all those fields, Tabitha said that, to the contrary, money was an important part of every profession, and life, and it would be good to have a bookkeeper in the “family,” as she called it.

When Viktor said that he might only stay for a week or two she gave him a look that suggested that she knew otherwise.

While they were talking, two children came in to ask Tabitha a question. Viktor couldn’t help but notice that neither of them had any arms. After they’d left, Tabitha explained that she’d adopted the twins – Demyan and Detana – two years earlier from a family that lived near Chernobyl. She also had a baby, Chin Ho, who she had adopted a year earlier. Chin Ho had a rare blood condition and was only expected to live two or three years.

Viktor thought of the girl without a left thumb who he and Marlena had rejected, and he was ashamed.

Feeling his shame, Tabitha put her hand on his head for a moment, and he felt immensely better. That is to say, he was still ashamed of what he’d done, but he understood it better.

He took a room in a turret with windows that looked out over the backyard, where he could see the outline of a vegetable garden about the size of an Olympic pool, dormant at the moment.

#          #          #

Viktor decided he would stay at least until the fall. He asked Tabitha if he could have a patch in the garden for his own and she said no.

He was stunned.

He could not have a patch, she explained, because the whole garden was common ground. But he could work out there as much, or as little as he liked, and take as much, or as little from it as he needed.

It was too early in the season to plant anything – especially this far north, but Viktor was eager to get his hands dirty, so he went right out and started turning the soil over and working compost in with a pitchfork. Within a few minutes Demyan and Detana showed up and wanted to help.

Viktor was amazed by how much they could do – especially working together – without arms, or hands. They called him “Uncle Hal.”

A week or so later he took them fishing. Dandelo went along. Viktor noticed him eyeing some birds that were hopping about, and was pleased to see that he did not go after them.

The four of them came home with a mess of perch with yellow bellies and orange fins. Tabitha said he knew a wonderful island recipe. Dandelo got some tasty left-overs.

While Tabitha was cooking – and Viktor thought that the smell of her cooking was so much “zingier” than Marlena’s – Viktor played a game with Demyan and Detana in which he also had to pick up blocks with his toes. They thought it was hilarious, how bad he was at it, and gave him some tips that helped. As they played he noticed that Tabitha kept sneaking looks at him that were like the look you give someone when you recognize them from somewhere, but can’t quite think of the place. He hoped she was not wondering if he was the weirdo who’d killed sixty-six cats down in Connecticut.

#          #          #

One night, after the three kids were in bed, Tabitha told Viktor her story. She’d been born in the Dominican Republic in 1961 – the same year he’d met the aliens, and first visited Shoreham. From the moment she was born she’d been hailed as a healer of Los Misterios –  Vodou practitioners who could “help or hurt” with a look or a touch – but as she was growing up she didn't see any evidence of it.

Hurricane David hit the DR in 1979. It was neither waves nor winds that caused the most damage, but flooding caused by the rainfall. More than two-thirds of the island’s crops were destroyed, and 2,000 people died, Tabitha’s mother, father, and two youngest sisters among them, when their home, which was on a river, was washed away. One of the only thing Tabitha was able to rescue was a pale blue rocker with a straw seat, which had floated a couple miles downriver. It was remnants of the same storm, Viktor realized, with a start, that hit Boca and washed out his Analyst-of-the-Year mini-boondoggle.

With her parents gone, Tabitha became head of the household, at 18, and took care of her younger brother and sister for five years. She supported them by being a healer. People would come to their house – which they had rebuilt further up the hill – and Tabitha would either look at them in a certain way or lay her hands on them and they would often, though not always, feel better. In return they would give her food or provide services.

“Did you really have powers?” Viktor asked.

“At first I didn't think so. I was thinking it was just because the people were believing I had powers, and so they were getting better from their own good feelings. But then I found that I did have some small powers, perhaps because I was needing them so badly. And little by little I was learning how to use them.”

She came to the United States in 1985, ostensibly as a housekeeper, but was soon doing healing work. One of her clients was a rich woman who was going blind. Tabitha cured her. In return the woman helped her to become a citizen, and left her the house in Shoreham plus a chunk of money in her will. Tabitha moved up to Vermont in 1987 – the same year, Viktor’s father was supposedly killed in a car accident.

She had been afraid that the town would reject someone like her, but Shoreham welcomed her with open arms right from the start.

When she saw how many rooms the house had she knew that it was her destiny to fill them with children. She adopted the twins in 1989, and Chin Ho in 1991. She hoped to have eleven children in the end, she said, and she knew that two of them – a boy and then a girl – would be of her making.

As she said that last part she gave Viktor a look that he did not understand. Partly to break the spell of that look, Viktor told her that he had also started his life as an illegal alien. He meant it as kind of a joke, but Tabitha took it literally and said, “I can see that. You come from as far away as your flying dinosaur birthmark (which he had not yet showed her). And you are still very, very far away.”

In the weeks that followed, Viktor found that when he talked to Tabitha about this or that, things had previously been ideas began to take on shape and become true objects. And the objects he touched – be it just the handle of a shovel, or a fork – now seemed more solid and substantial.

69)                Viktor Comes Clean

Viktor wound up being the bookkeeper/financial advisor for all the touchy-feely practitioners in the house, and others that they knew. He showed them how, by running their practices as businesses, they could use Schedule C to deduct many expenses and keep more of their money. He also showed them how they could save even more money, which was to never buy any insurance they absolutely didn't have to buy – like major medical and automobile liability. Everything else, he said, was as wasteful as buying lottery tickets.

Instead of paying Viktor – who they knew as Harold – in money, they paid him in services. So Viktor had his chart read. He got acupuncture once a week. And he put an amethyst under his pillow, which Vanya said would cure him of getting up to pee three times a night. And Dandelo got acupuncture for a sprain in his right rear leg.

#          #          #

As a future World-Famous Astronomer, Viktor had always been skeptical of Astrology, but he had nothing to lose, since it was free.

Pamela had frizzy, ginger hair and green eyes. She said his chart had Mars in 22 degrees Virgo, and this suggested that he was by nature very careful and systematic, that he paid great attention to details, and was always seeking perfection, which sometimes caused him to get bogged down in details. “Does that sound true?” she asked.

He said it was more than true –  in fact, he often used attention to detail specifically to avoid thinking about deeper issues.

She went on to say that had an extremely strong sense of responsibility, which at times caused be overly critical of himself and anyone who didn't live up to his impossibly standards.

“Another bulls eye,” he exclaimed.

“The problem,” she said, “is that Astrology doesn’t usually work for a person like you. because they are too skeptical.”

Viktor had been thinking exactly the same thing, but now that she was taking it away from him, he flipped and wanted it.

“Isn't there anything else in there?” he asked.

She smiled, as if the first part had been a trick, and said, “Well, you also have Venus in 23 degrees of Pisces, which means that you have a romantic nature and a very creative imagination. In fact, your private fantasies can be so much more appealing than the reality around you that it is difficult for you to leave them.”

“That fits, too,” Viktor said, and suggested that they continue their work for a bit. And not just because Pamela had ginger hair and green eyes.

#          #          #

The next time Viktor went to Hartford he got his telescope out of storage and brought it up. The kids loved going out at night and looking at planets and nebula with him. So did Tabitha. One evening he pointed up at the sky and said that every single star that she could see up there, and many zillions beyond that, was moving away from the earth – some of them at incredible speeds.

“Are none of them moving toward us?” she asked.

“Not a one,” he said. “And that's the truth.”

When she looked a little concerned he said, “I wonder if they know something?”

That got a chuckle out of her.

Later he explained that it was just because the universe was expanding. To which she replied, “That is one way of looking at it.”

#          #          #

One of Viktor’s clients mentioned that there was an opening on the Shoreham Conservation Commission. He sent in a letter of application and was interviewed by the Selectmen, because it was an appointed position. One of the questions he had to answer was whether he’d ever been arrested. When he said that he had, and told them that he had killed a cat in a scientific experiment – without revealing his real name, or that he’d confessed to killing sixty-six cats – one of the three selectmen made a sour face, but another one nudged him in the elbow and said that he was the fellow living at Tabitha’s house, so he was approved unanimously.

Whenever a builder or homeowner needed to do work within a hundred feet of a wetland, the Cons Com would do a site walk, and then issue conditions to protect the wetland – hay bales and so on – or require the applicant to replicate new wetlands equal to twice the area of any wetland that needed to be destroyed.

There were nine members on the Commission. Four of the members were vehemently anti-development and used the regulations to squash as much activity as possible. The other four respected the rights of people to develop their own land, and were more liberal. Viktor, who was both anti-development and a Libertarian, was often the swing vote.

#          #          #

Since Viktor worked at the house, and played with the twins quite a bit, and even watched Chin Ho from time to time, when Tabitha had to run an errand, he saw quite a lot of her, and after a while he noticed something that surprised him, because it didn't seem right.

The thing he noticed was that, while Tabitha was clearly a person who did a lot of good in the world, and brightened people’s lives, there was something dark and burdensome about her, as if she was unable to do the same for herself.

One night after the kids had gone to bed and Viktor and Tabitha were sitting in the living room reading, he asked her about it.

She looked up at him with astonishment. “Hal,” she said, “You are surprising me. Is this the kind of question you would ever have even been asking your proper wife?” for Viktor had told her a bit about his previous life.

He said no, he would not have. “Though I probably should have.”

“Indeed, you should have,” Tabitha said, and frowned at him. But then she began to tell him, in a voice he’d never heard before, how difficult it had been for her – at 18 – to not only raise her siblings, but protect them, how there was a lot of darkness in the world, things that would hurt you, or eat you, and she had always viewed what she did as a struggle against the forces of darkness – white magic versus black, health versus sickness – and he was right, it was a very heavy burden to carry.

When she was done he said to her, “You know what you could use?”

She shook her head.

“You could use a good laugh,” he said, and then he told her the stupidest, silliest joke he could think of – the joke about the Big Chief who needs to fart, but can’t, adding that she was kind of like Big Chief No Laugh. And she laughed as loud as the chief farted, though she was not destroyed by the laughter.

When she was done laughing she said, “Thank you. You showed me something that was too close for me to see. That bad man was always hiding in my own shadow. But I see him now, and can scare him away. Thank you, Viktor.”

Viktor was pleased by that. But before he could be too pleased he realized that she’d called him by his real name.

“How long have you known?” he asked.

“I am not measuring such things in time,” she said. “It feels to me like Viktor has always been your name.”

“I'm sorry I deceived you,” he said.

“It's not me you have deceived,” she said. “It is yourself, which is worse, because if you are not who you think you are, then who are you?”

She suggested that Viktor come clean, and he did. The good people of Shoreham not only did not blame him, they said they understood, though the kids – and even those yet to come – would always call him Uncle Hal.

70)                First Comes Love …

The Big Chief No Fart joke not only opened up Tabitha– it opened up Viktor as well. Soon, Viktor was cracking everyone up, though it was not so much with his quips and odd ideas. For example, it was during that year’s baseball season that he came up with his idea for the Negative Wave. The way it worked was that when everyone was standing, to cheer the team on at a particularly tense moment, the Negative Wave would begin with a section of the bleachers sitting down and throwing their arms up, and then would roll around the ballpark like the trough of a regular wave. He had also suggesting that we might counter the rising levels of the ocean by cultivating more sponges.

Or, Chen, the meditation guy, was in the kitchen talking on the phone while was cooking and when he burned his bread he shook her dreadlocks and said that he had trouble doing two things at once. When Viktor replied that he, by contrast, had trouble doing only one thing at a time he did a double-take, and then laughed hilariously.

And there were the usual jokes as well. Like when he described an overly talkative couple as Chatty Cathy and Gabby Hayes.

One of the people who was laughing the hardest was Tabitha, and, little by little, like the tiny cracks in that German dam, she began to change. It was the other practitioners who were the first to notice. Pamela, the green-eyed astrologer, asked Viktor if anything was “going on” between he and Tabitha.

“You mean …?” he asked.

When she winked at him he said no, absolutely not.

“Are you sure?” she asked, still smiling. “She seems so much lighter.”

Vanya, the crystal healer, sort of tried to take credit for it, attributing it to the blue lace agate she’d been using on Tabitha’s forehead.

Viktor didn't ask Dr. K, the acupuncture man, what he thought, but he noticed that Tabitha now had many fewer magic marker dots on her arms and legs – the points she was supposed to work on with Moxa between visits.

As for himself, his hair seemed to be thicker, and he was only getting up once a night to pee, though it could have just been the sweet country air.

#          #          #

On a night at the very beginning of April, Viktor took the twins vernal ponding. Dandelo, who was no longer limping, went along, as usual, his bell dinging in the darkness. They hiked a good ways into the woods to a pond that looked like it would go totally dry in the summer, and therefore not have any fish. Viktor told the kids that the yellow-spotted salamanders would emerge from their tunnels on a rainy night and crawl down here on their short legs to lay their eggs, leaving out the part about how the eggs got fertilized because he didn't think it was appropriate for them, and also he actually didn't know how salamanders did “it.”

As they approached the pond they heard spring peepers and the sound of the woods frogs, which the kids thought were ducks. The pond water was as black as the night, and très cold – there had been ice on the pond just a week or two earlier – so they all wore boots. The problem was that with boots on the twins would have no way to catch a salamander.

Viktor waded out a ways and shone his flashlight into the dark water. Bingo!  He grabbed one, and showed it to the twins, who squealed with delight, then flipped off their boots and waded in. Viktor winced, sympathetically, but erupted in joy when Denyan caught one with her toes, and not only that, it was a rare, blue spotted salamander, smaller, and more delicate than its yellow cousins.

When he got home he told Tabitha they had been very careful handling them because mole salamanders breath through their skin.

“As we are all doing,” she said.

#          #          #

Soon the vegetable garden was going gang-busters. One afternoon in June Viktor was out there with Tabitha and the three kids picking strawberries when he spotted a glint of light in the woods.

His heart skipped a beat. Had they found him?  The people Norbert worked for?  Reporters doing a follow up story on the Krazy Kat Killer?  The nice people from the shampoo company?

Viktor walked over to the spot and the ground had been trampled by what looked like smallish footprints.

When he got back to the garden Tabitha explained it to him. She worked topless in the garden and there were a number of teenage boys who spied on her. She knew it and she didn't care. They were good boys, she said, though they did regard her with awe and fear when they would see her – dressed – in the General Store.

Viktor, who had made a point of not looking at her bare breasts, now looked at them directly. Unlike Marlena’s huge white breasts and pink nipples, Tabitha’s were small, but perfect, with nipples that were almost black.

“I understand,” Viktor said, with a smile.

And she smiled back at him, like a black Mona Lisa.

#          #          #

Viktor made a trip down to Hartford to deal with some paperwork at NFG. When he saw the Tower from miles away, on the highway, it looked smaller than he remembered it. By the time he was in town and standing beneath it, it looked even smaller than that.

After he finished up at NFG he drove out to his old house. Just as he pulled up, a school bus came down the road. Two kids jumped down and ran into his old house.

Suddenly, Viktor began sobbing.

As he sat in the car sobbing he felt Tabitha watching him from the passenger seat. He turned to look at her, closed his eyes to hide his tears, and suddenly they were in bed. She had her dark, sinewy arms around him and he had his head on her chest. One of the fine nipples he had looked at in the garden was right there, so he took it in his mouth. He saw stars and planets. He saw a reddish orb that was his home planet. He saw her home with the river rushing past, down below. He heard the twins giggle, but when he opened his eyes it was some other kids running past his car.

This time, instead of sobbing, he smiled.

#          #          #

One night a month or two later Tabitha asked Viktor if he had been faithful to his wife throughout their marriage.

“Yes and no,” he replied.

Another person would have said that that wasn't an appropriate answer, but Tabitha – sounding kind of like Dr. French, Viktor thought – just asked him to continue.

When he told her that he thought he had been more faithful to the idea of loyalty than to Marlena herself, she did not react critically.

When he added said – if he was going to speak honestly – he would have slept with Paula that night in Cincinnati, she asked him what about now?

He shook his head and said no.

“For what reason?” she asked.

“Because she is” – and he realized this as he was saying the words – “too much like me.”

Tabitha accepted that, was silent for a few minutes, then asked him if anyone was in bed with him now.

He was about to say no, when he remembered what had happened in Hartford. So he said, “Yes, there has been someone, though I think it was just a daydream.”

“No it was not,” Tabitha said, and with that she rose from her pale blue rocker, moved over, and touched him in the center of his forehead with a long, ET-like finger. He stood, put his arms around her, and kissed her. Then they went to bed for all the years that they had missed each other.

71)                Smith & Wesson Tell Viktor The Truth

Exactly one year after Viktor Hal pleaded guilty to killing at least sixty-six cats, he was driving home from the General Store in Shoreham. There was a program on the radio about how the rematch between Bobby Fischer and Boris Spassky was just starting, and Viktor shook his head in amazement and muttered, ”How about that.”

As if his comment had precipitated it, a dark sedan suddenly appeared directly behind him. It was very close, and the only good reasons Viktor could think of was that they wanted to read his bumper sticker, which said, BAN BUMPER STICKERS.

He slowed down and edged toward the shoulder to let it pass, but it didn't want to pass. As they approached the State Forest, the sedan flashed its headlights at him. Viktor pulled in at the parking lot by one of the entrances. The sedan pulled in behind him, and our hero-to-be watched it through his rear view mirror, keeping his engine running.

He hoped it was an unmarked police car, but he hadn't been speeding or anything like that – and the police would have had a blue light. His next thought was that it was an irate motorist, but he hadn't done anything aggressive. Then he hoped that it was just someone who was lost and needed to ask him for directions, since there were no street signs out here.

He rolled down his window and looked back at the sedan and saw that all three of his guesses had been wrong.

#          #          #

Agents Smith & Wesson got out of opposite sides of the sedan. Smith – the serious one – motioned to Viktor to follow him, and the three of them took a walk in the woods, with Viktor in the middle.

“How did you find me?” Viktor asked.

“It was not the most difficult assignment we ever faced,” quipped Wesson.

Viktor had the sudden thought that Smith & Wesson reminded him in some ways of the two aliens – a serious one and a funny one, with a wit similar to his own.

“We said we would come and we are men of our word,” Smith said, and then reminded Viktor of his three choices. Option #2 – remaining at NFG and getting promoted was no longer applicable. But he could still (Option #1) elect to receive a lottery ticket good for a substantial payoff, or (Option #3) they would tell him the truth about his father.

Viktor chose the truth about his father.

Smith stopped walking and Viktor, walking beside him, stopped as well. Wesson continued for a few paces, then turned and faced the two of them in a position from which he could shoot Viktor if necessary.

“There is good news and bad news,” Smith said.

Viktor asked for the bad news first.

The bad news was that his father was dead.

Viktor asked if he had worked for them and Smith said that his father had not done any missions for the Agency in the years following Korea. He had been “totally retired.”

When Viktor looked skeptical, Wesson added that if he hadn't been retired, he never would have pretended that he wasn't.”

Viktor didn't believe either of them. But that isn't what he said. What he said was, “I guess I should have gone for the winning lottery ticket.”

#          #          #

Now Viktor asked the agents what the good news was.

“Before we tell you the good news,” Smith said, “I want to offer you a word of caution.”

“Yes?”

“Be very careful with Project Zorro. When hundreds of millions of dollars are at stake, people can be ruthless. We will do what we can to protect you, because you are a ‘legacy’ – the son of a hero – but there’s only so much we can do.”

Viktor asked how they knew about Project Zorro, and they reminded him that they’d bugged the tinfoil shed. And Viktor’s house.

“Also, we tortured Boltzmann,” Wesson quipped.

“Really?” Viktor asked, his eyes popping.

“Just a little,” Wesson said, with a chuckle that might have meant they really did, or might have meant that they really didn't.

“And the good news?”

“The good news,” Smith said, was that Viktor’s 1990 tax return had been selected for auditing because of what looked like excessive charitable contributions. But they had made the problem “go away.”

#          #          #

Smith & Wesson asked Viktor to remain in the woods for ten minutes. When he got back to his car, their sedan was gone, and as of this writing (2012) none of his tax returns (or those of any of his clients) were ever audited.

72)                …Then Comes Tabitha With a Baby Carriage

That winter of 92 was so cold that dogs were sticking to Route 74. Or so Viktor said to Tabitha, who was chopping vegetables with a chef’s knife.

She chuckled.

Viktor said that he thought the winters might get better with global warming, at least up in Vermont, though mud season would be worse.

She didn't chuckle at that. She said she was very worried about what would become of the world – not so much for her, as for her children.

“Do you still plan to have eleven children?” he asked her.

She nodded.

“Including two of your own making?” he asked, in a voice gone suddenly hoarse.

It was an interesting question, given that Tabitha had said she would not have a child out of wedlock. Making love was OK, but no babies.

She turned, and gave Viktor a look that turned him inside out. “What are you asking?” she said.

“I'm asking you if you would like to get married. To me.”

She snorted then held up the knife and replied, *«*Si la vaca ha venden por libras, porque comprar la vaca entera? »

Viktor’s Spanish had come along enough that he understood she was saying, but he wasn't sure which one of them was the cow. He didn't know how to answer in Spanish, but he did know how to go over and put his arms around her and go, “Moooo,” which made her laugh hysterically.

#          #          #

On January 1st of 1993, Czechoslovakia split into Czech Republic and Slovakia. A few weeks later, Bill Clinton was sworn in as the 42nd President of the United States. On the 31st the Buffalo Bills made it to the Super Bowl again, but fell to the Dallas Cowboy, 52 to 17, for their third consecutive Super Bowl loss (with one more to come). On that same day, a few hours before the Super Bowl, Viktor and Tabitha got married in the barn.

Viktor wore a tie, for the first time in twenty-five years, and not because Tabitha asked him to. The minister was not affiliated with any church or political party, and the ceremony did not include any words. Viktor and Tabitha just stood silently before about a hundred guests for about twenty minutes (some said it was more like a week, but didn't feel long) without saying a word. Then they kissed and the party started.

#          #          #

Viktor moved into the master bedroom, but kept the turret room as his study and worked on Project Zorro up there, often late into the night. The roof was so filled with papers that it stayed really warm in the winter.

#          #          #

Tabitha became pregnant immediately, if not sooner, as Viktor later calculated. It would be a boy, she said, with absolute certainty. That caused a bit of a stir in Viktor’s mind, because an heir was probably the number one thing that his father had always wanted from his son, and probably still wanted if he wasn't dead. His father would not have wanted a mixed-breed grandson, but he would have settled, and would have wanted the boy to be named Viktor A. Hal III, no matter what colors he was.

Viktor asked Tabitha what she thought of all that, and she said that there was no need to name him Viktor A. Hal III.

When Viktor asked her why not, she explained that that had been his name for as long as anyone could remember.

#          #          #

Viktor A. Hal III (V3) was born on September 7, 1993. He had blue eyes and tan skin.

When Viktor noticed him grinning impishly and using his spoon backwards he took Tabitha aside and told her to not laugh. “Otherwise, the next thing you know he’ll be putting on his mittens on his feet, and after that … well, you can see where it leads.”

#          #          #

V3’s sister, Vicky, was born on September 7, 1995. She had dark eyes and dark hair, but pale skin. Together they made a nice pair.

“Are you up for six more?” Tabitha sighed.

“You mean to make eleven?” Viktor asked.

When she nodded, he said sure, but he thought they should adopt the next five, and so they did.

#          #          #

Hakim (meaning wise and insightful) came to them in 1997 from Ethiopia, at the age of nine. Because he’d been hungry his whole life they had a lot of trouble getting him to eat properly and he always had a thin, haunted look. He eventually went back to Ethiopia, where he was killed in an uprising that he himself had instigated. At the memorial service Viktor said he was proud of him for being “a man of action.”

#          #          #

Tatiana – another Russian baby – came to them in 1999. Many people felt, from the moment she arrived, that she was destined to become a saint, like her namesake. As of this writing (2012) she was only fourteen, and acting more like a teenager than a saint, but you never know.

#          #          #

Although Viktor was growing older at the rate of one year per year, he felt like he was growing younger. And the world felt more solid and substantial by the day.

“If present trends continue, I might be a real person by the time I die,” he said. And it wasn't a joke.

73)                Zorro Strikes

Our hero to be had found love, he had found connection, and he had plenty of kids to play with, but he had still not found the thing that would give him the applause that Dr. French had had helped him see that he needed – the World-Famous Something.

As September of 1999 rolled around he thought he only had two shots left. He could (a) become a stand-up comedian, or (b) make a big breakthrough with Project Zorro. Of the two, Zorro seemed the more likely. His little room in the turret was stuffed with data, and more was becoming available every minute as the Worldwide Web expanded at the speed of light. But Viktor so far had been unable to connect any important dots that hadn't already been connected.

Then Tabitha made a suggestion. “In making such a searching,” she said, “it is important that you do so with compassion.”

When he said he wasn't sure what she meant she said, “Do not be looking for villanos, is my advice. Indeed, there are villanos aplenty, but they are not your responsibility. Picture instead the people you might be helping, like Demyan, Detana, Chin Ho, V3, Vicky, Hakim, Tatiana, and their billions of cousins today and forever. Feel their pain, and try to help them.”

Viktor tried that approach and he did not make any more progress, though he did feel less frustrated. But then, one afternoon, he was talking to an accounting client about the cancer treatments that had given rise to her medical deductions, just shooting the breeze, really, because they were done with her taxes, when he noticed that there were two things about her that were unusual, though not really odd. One was that she was very fond of a certain soda (not Dr. Pepper). The other was that she was left-handed.

Viktor’s neurons lit up like a pin ball machine. Could it be possible!

As far as Viktor knew, no one had ever sorted his kind of data by left and right-handed people, either in a life study or a cohort study. As soon as the woman left, he rushed up to his study in the turret, and almost the minute his fingers hit the keyboard he discovered not one or two, but five or six products that seemed to have a high correlation with certain cancers in left-handed people. And, since about 10% of the world’s population was left-handed … that was a pretty big pile of people.

#          #          #

The products of the shampoo company were not involved in any of Viktor’s left-handed findings, but because the things Norbert H. Boltzmann said had scared him, Viktor wanted to publish his findings anonymously. More because of the kids than himself, he said. Tabitha wouldn’t let him. If this was about revealing the truth, she said, then he had to be truthful. He reassured himself that the finding was so broad that, while it would affect a number of different products in the market, no one company would look like it was targeted. Also, once the data were out, they would have no reason to harm him – and a boatload of reasons to not get caught doing so.

The reaction was unbelievable. Within days, Shoreham was mobbed with reporters. Around the globe, researchers were feverishly confirming Viktor’s results, and extending them to other products. Some of the world’s best PR firms tried to smear his name with the KKK incident, and his biracial marriage, but the power and goodness of what he’d done washed their complaints away as surely as Hurricane David had taken away Tabitha’s home and parents. Viktor was as hated by corporate America as Ralph Nader (this was a couple years before Nader helped George W. Bush get sort of elected), but at the same time they coveted his seal of approval on their products.

“You were wanting attention?” Tabitha said, with a smile.

Viktor had gotten so much attention he had to take vacations secretly, using the name Harold Viktor. He was famous. And he was – finally! – a hero.

Although he refused to endorse any products, he accepted some speaking appearances, authored (with a ghost writer) two books, and appeared in five movies. The fees he earned from this were all donated to charities related to the ailments that plagued many of their children.

In the years that followed, Tabitha’s house on the hill became a shrine for left-handed pilgrims. They’d point to the window of the turret in which Viktor had discovered the things that had been so good for the world. If they came at night they were likely to see him up there, his light shining, for he was looking into other angles, such as people who could curl their tongue. However, much like Barry Mann (“Who Put the Bomp”), Venus (“Shocking Blue”) and the Penguins (“Earth Angel”), this would be Viktor’s only hit.  It was a big one, though. One article estimated that his research might save close to a million lives a year around the world, year after year after year, even after Viktor himself was dead and gone.

74)                Norbert Appears and Disappears

On May 9, 1775, about a hundred Green Mountain Boys had assembled, with various other forces, near Hands Cove in Vermont, to cross Lake Champlain – which is more like a river than a lake that far south – and capture Fort Ticonderoga. Exactly 225 years later, our hero was jogging very near that spot when, about fifty yards ahead of him, there was a stirring in the bushes.

Viktor stopped and his pulse jumped to about 150, because there were bears in these woods. But it was something even more dangerous-looking. A figure dressed entirely in black stepped out of the woods and, staring straight at Viktor, blocked the path.

A bolt of adrenaline shot through our hero. It was a hit man from one of the companies he’d offended!  Or some crazy vigilante type.

Viktor turned, to run, though he pictured the dark figure raising his arm and firing a single, silenced shot into the center of Viktor’s back. Either that, or there would now be an identically dressed second man blocking the trail behind him.

But halfway through his turn, Viktor stopped. Then slowly looked back. Because there had been something about this man. Not the black parka.  Not the sunglasses. Not the beard. It was …

Bingo!  It came to him. The man was wearing exactly the same style of trapper hat that Viktor’s mysterious neighbor had worn those many years ago, and this man was about the same height, and build.

It was him. It was Norbert H. Boltzmann.

Viktor’s heart leaped with a joy which would, sadly, be short-lived.

#          #          #

Viktor suggested that they sit by the edge of the lake and chat, but Boltzmann – not that that was his name anymore – was afraid they would be too visible. Someone on the far shore could have binoculars. A satellite could be hovering overhead. Instead, they found a fallen tree in a dark hemlock forest, and sat there, side by side.

Boltzmann told Viktor he looked well, and commented that his hair loss seem to have largely reversed itself. “Are you using that stuff?” he asked.

Viktor shook his head said no, that he wasn't using anything, and told his friend about Tabitha and how she had helped him to change.

“It used to be,” Viktor said, “that if I picked up a cup of tea, it was just a cup of tea of a certain size and shape that was in my hand. Now I feel like I really connect with it – the mug, the hot water, the tea, all the places all those things have been in the past, and where they will be in the future. It's like it's alive. And because it's more alive, so am I.”

Viktor did not tell Boltzmann that he looked good, because he didn't. His hair had gone gray, the beard he’d added was also flecked with gray, he seemed less robust, and the sunglasses were not just to hide his face – the bright light seemed to bother him.

Boltzmann told Viktor he was out of the Program, though he could neither confirm nor deny that it had been Star Wars.
