The Agency had given him a new identity, and he had not been approached by any foreign governments. It looked like he was in the clear. But he could not get himself clear emotionally.

“When I was inside,” Boltzmann said, “I didn't have much freedom, but there was also a lot of security. I felt safe.”

“You had what everyone needs,” Viktor said, “An evil enemy. And a mission that required inexhaustible energy. It's pretty hard to top that.”

“Now, I feel constantly at risk.”

“Because of what, do you know?” Viktor asked him.

Boltzmann said no, he didn't know what he was afraid of, and that made it even more difficult to deal with.

“I know someone who might be able to help you,” Viktor said.

#          #          #

Before they even got to the house, Tabitha felt them coming and was standing at the bottom of the driveway, blocking it. She would not allow Norbert H. Boltzmann to come any closer. She would not let him near the children.

Viktor was embarrassed, but Boltzmann said he understood. And this was, perhaps, the real reason why he had come.

Tabitha later told Viktor that it was obvious. Norbert had the “look,” and he had the smell – the smell of chicory.

“You are a zombie,” Tabitha told him. “You died many years ago, in a life before the one you have thought was your own.”

Boltzmann nodded and let out what sounded like relief.

She explained that his soul had been taken. It was being in a bottle kept by a bokor who used it for their purposes. That bokor was gone, since a long time ago, but Norbert’s soul was still in the bottle. He was looking for it, but would never find it, because it was not in this world.

“Do you feel cold?” she asked him.

When he said “Always,” Viktor thought of the trapper hat, the parkas, and how Boltzmann had heated his basement even in the summer.

Viktor asked Tabitha if she could get Norbert’s soul back for him. She said no, that there was only one solution.

“Which is?”

“Feed him salt, and he will return to the grave.”

That was the last thing Viktor wanted to do. And it wasn't really something that Tabitha wanted to do, either. But Norbert H. Boltzmann was willing – even eager – to do it.

It was an elaborate ritual, which they conducted deep in the woods, and when it was over, Norbert looked less dispirited. He thanked Tabitha, gave Viktor a hug, and headed off in a direction that Viktor later established was straight for what had once been Latvia.

“Is there nothing else we can do for him?” Viktor asked, as his friend disappeared into the darkness.

“No,” said Tabitha, sobbing, in much the same way that she must have when she watched her mother and father being carried away by the river.

75)                Chuck Laughed and Slapped Viktor on the Back so Hard That a Filling Came Out

Mick joined them in 2001. A sort of idiot savant, he would soon be able to tell you the make, model and year of any car that passed, as well as the numbers of many of their parts. His talents were such that he could have pursued a number of careers, but he was emotionally too weak to leave Shoreham, so he kept his room in Tabitha and Viktor’s house and opened a junk yard where people would come looking for second-hand parts – and sometimes third and fourth-hand as well – for cars and appliances. But the part of his business that he liked the best was just shooting the shit with his customers, many of whom came just for that reason.

#          #          #

On one of his solo trips into Connecticut, Viktor looked up Frank McNutty. After a few years as a realtor he’d started his own firm, McNulty Properties. It was doing very well.

“People like to buy things from people who are fat and happy,” he said.

“Hey,” Viktor, said, I have a joke for you. And even before he began to tell the Galileo joke, McNutty was doubled over in happy laughter, and Viktor himself was laughing almost as hard.

#          #          #

Asha – the Nature girl – joined them in 2003. She had a green thumb, to say the least. Although biologically unrelated to Tabitha, she was able to cause a plant to flourish merely by touching it. If she walked barefoot across the lawn, a few days later you would see greener grass where her feet had touched the grass. She had to be careful, though, because she had the same effect on weeds.

#          #          #

Also in 2003, Paula sent Viktor an email. She had thought about contacting him a few years earlier, when she read about his great success with Project Zorro, but the time hadn't been right. She said she’d like to talk to him sometime, and left her phone number.

He called her on a Monday night, as before.

She said the kids were very grown up now – 16, 20 and 22 – she and her husband were still together, and probably always would be. But she sometimes wondered what might have been.

Viktor told her that he was now married, but he also sometimes wondered what might have been. He did not mention his comment to Tabitha that Paula had been very similar to him. Because she wasn't anymore. She sounded the same, but he was different, and talking to her made him more aware of it.

#          #          #

Mimi – who made number eleven – who came in 2004, was from the outset in a coma from which she was not expected to ever recover. They kept her – with all her supporting equipment – in the loveliest bedroom in the house, and Viktor and Tabitha and all the kids took turns sitting with her and reading out loud, singing, or whatever. Many other people from the community also came and did the same. Nothing any of them did ever elicited the slightest reaction from Mimi. But it produced deep reactions in them.

#          #          #

Also in 2004, NFG was acquired by The General. Many redundant positions were eliminated, including those of Dan Drysdale and the (not so young anymore) woman in HR.

Tabitha told Viktor he should not gloat.

He did, anyway.

#          #          #

Although her heart was the one organ she had never complained about – perhaps because Viktor Sr. had claimed that territory – Viktor’s mother died in 2005, at the age of 79, of an acute myocardial infarction, a term she would have been able to spell correctly. There was a bouquet of flowers from a “distant admirer,” and when her casket was being lowered into the ground Viktor was almost certain that he saw a tall man with unnaturally dark hair watching the proceedings from about fifty yards away.

#          #          #

In November of 2008 Viktor and Tabitha received an invitation to a wedding. Of Marlena and Patty.

They went.

Patty had conceived a daughter with the help of a friend, via what they called the “turkey baster method.”  This fourteen year-old girl was there, and Viktor thought she looked more like Marlena than Patty.

Tabitha took him aside during the reception and said that none of the things that Viktor had told her about Marlena seemed true – not just that she was quite thin, but she didn't seem dark at all.

Viktor said Marlena had changed dramatically.

Tabitha shook her head and said no, this was who she had been born to be – it was the other person who had not been Marlena – and Viktor thought that made sense.

The next day they went out to see Chuck and Claire Nelson, who were still living in Avalon Acres.

After they talked about the Giants’ amazing victory over the Patriots, and the two World Series that the Red Sox had won since Viktor left, Chuck asked Viktor if he could now talk about what had really happened back then.

When Viktor said he probably shouldn’t, Chuck said he didn't see what the problem was because the Statue (sic) of Limitations must have expired by now.

Viktor reflected that if Norbert H. Boltzmann was where he thought he was, there wasn't much harm to be done. So he told Chuck what had happened.

Chuck laughed and slapped Viktor on the back so hard that a filling came out, and said that was a really good one.

76)                The Blue Moon of December 31, 2009

The next blue moon on a New Year’s Eve came on December 31, 2009. It had risen, as predicted, at about 4:20, and by 5:00, as the sky was darkening, it had hoisted itself above the trees that marked the edge of the State Forest. After dinner, Viktor and Tabitha took the nine kids who still lived in the house out to see it, and Viktor explained to them what a rare thing it was – not just a blue moon, but a blue moon falling on New Year’s Eve – and how it wouldn’t happen again for nineteen years, a Metonic Cycle. Despite his talent with numbers, he did not stop to think that it had also been 17,778 Earth days since he’d last seen his alien friends.

The kids pretended to be impressed, but were eager to get back inside and watch their shows and play their games.

It was almost ten o’clock before Viktor and Tabitha got everyone tucked in. Then they went out to into the field to look at the moon themselves, with two folding chairs, a bottle of champagne, and a blanket for Tabitha, who didn't handle the cold as well as Viktor.

Dandelo followed them, more because he didn't like to be left out than because he was truly interested.

The three of them sat silently for about fifteen minutes, and then Tabitha asked Viktor how he was feeling about what had happened nineteen years earlier.

“I still feel bad about some things,” he said, “but not in the same way as before.”

She waited.

“I mean, I guess the thing I feel worst about is leaving Marlena, but I understand that that wasn't because of the things that happened after Norbert arrived – it was because of the things that didn't happen before then. I mean, as we’ve talked about, she and I should have talked more. And touched more. Or tried to. If we had, I think we still would have broken up, but it wouldn’t have been as traumatic. Or maybe,” and now he shrugged, “maybe it's always traumatic and there’s no way around that. Anyway, she’s happier now than she wasn't with me.

“I also feel bad that my career ended on such a sour note, but I was wrapping that up, anyway. And I do feel bad about killing that cat, but Dandelo says I should let go of that.

“I feel terrible that I wasn't able to help Norbert, but maybe I did, in a way.”

“And on the other side?” Tabitha asked.

“Well, we've brought happiness to a bunch of kids. I’ve done a couple good things for the world. We’ve been happy and … well, I guess there are more pluses than minuses.”

Tabitha shook her head. “This is not a math’s problem,” she said. “Look in your heart, Viktor, and ask yourself what you're feeling. Are you glad you’re here now?”

“Yes. Terribly.”

“Do you feel connected to the world?”

He looked down at the ground. “Yes,” he said, “I do.”

“And what would be happening if the people from your home planet came tonight to take you home?”

“I don’t think I ever truly believed that,” Viktor said. “I think it was probably just one of my daydreams.”

“But what if they came anyway?”

“I couldn’t go home with them because …”

“Yes?”

“because I am home.”

With that, Tabitha put her arms around him for a good long moment. Dandelo, turned and marched into the house, his bell tinkling as he went, just in case they were going to do anything silly.

A few minutes later Mimi started crying and Tabitha went in to attend to her.

Viktor sat by himself for a while. But then his brow furrowed when he heard something. Actually, what he heard was a deep silence – that all the sounds that had been in the air seemed to have suddenly been sucked out of it.

He remembered a similar calmness in the air many years ago, and looked up into the sky. At first he didn't see anything. Then he noticed a dark spot to the right of Saturn –  an oblong area where the sky seemed blacker, and without stars. It reminded him of Casper the Ghost’s portable hole.

As Viktor watched, this black spot grew larger and rounder. And his smile expanded in proportion.

#          #          #

The ship landed in the middle of the field. There was no greenish glow this time, but the full moon rendered it quite visible, including something Viktor had not noticed the first time – a logo, as on an airplane, of a Pterodactyl just like the one on his arm.

Viktor walked over as the hatch was opening and met his two friends at the bottom of the floating steps which pinged down with a xylophone sound. Neither of them had changed one iota from New Hampshire

“We were just passing by,” the funny one said.

“We said we would be back,” the serious one said, “and we are men of our word.”

“Does this mean that it's time for me to go home?”

“It means,” the serious one said, “that you are welcome to return. Mission accomplished.”

“I think I’d rather stay,” Viktor said, without hesitation.

“Yes, we know,” they said in unison. “We were listening. We just wanted to say goodbye.”

“Also,” the funny one said, without speaking, “we’re thirsty.

“Got it,” Viktor replied. “Just give me a minute.”

With that Viktor went into the kitchen and grabbed a cold six pack of Dr. Pepper from the fridge.

“What’s up?” Tabitha asked.

“The aliens have come for me,” he said. “They’re out in the field. I'm not going to go with them, but they’re thirsty.”

Tabitha peered out the window. Viktor couldn’t tell if she could see the ship from that angle or not. All she said was, “Be sure to cut up that plastic thingie so no poor creatures get strangled, or if they take it with them, tell them to do the same.”

Viktor went out and told the aliens they could keep the whole six pack since they had a long trip ahead of them, but to be sure to cut up the plastic thingie.

Before they left the funny one said, “By the way, we intercepted a message for you.”

“Yes?” he asked, thinking of Norbert H. Boltzmann.

“The message is just … ‘Morton P. Levi.’  It was transmitted ten minutes ago from somewhere in your state of Oregon. Does that mean anything to you?”

Viktor’s face erupted in a huge smile and he gave them both hugs, which embarrassed them.

The aliens wished him well, and strode up the floating steps into their ship. The steps pinged up into the belly of the ship with a xylophone sound, the hatch melted shut and then Viktor heard a noise exactly like a car starter turning over but not catching.

Before Viktor could react, the steps pinged halfway back down. The funny one climbed down and banged the side of the ship with a hammer.

“Try it now,” he called up to the serious one.

Viktor heard the engine start. It sounded just like a ’57 Chevy. Viktor laughed and the funny alien sneezed so hilariously that he almost fell off the bottom step, just catching himself by one hand. Then he climbed into the ship and they took off without a sound.

An oval darkness rose into the sky, then veered to the left. As it did it covered the moon for a moment with the result that the moon seemed to wink at Viktor.

He winked back, and then went into the house.

“No regrets?” Tabitha asked.

“Some. I mean, I would have liked to see more of the universe.”

“This is no problem,” she said. “The universe and all the many planets, they are right here.”

\*\*\*\* END \*\*\*\*
